Existence and stability of Sadovskii vortices: from vortex patches to regular vorticity
Kyudong Choi, Young-Jin Sim, Kwan Woo

Abstract
. We consider traveling wave solutions of the incompressible Euler equations in ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} , taking the form of a counter-rotating dipole that touches the symmetry axis. We refer to such solutions as Sadovskii vortices, in honor of Sadovskii’s contribution ( J. Appl. Math. Mech. , 1971). It serves as a model to understand how persistent, coherent, and stable flow structures can arise even in strongly interacting singular configurations such as touching counter-rotating vortex pairs. Typical examples include the Sadovskii vortex patches, whose existence has been rigorously proved recently, and the Chaplygin–Lamb dipole. Our first result concerns the stability of a nonempty collection of Sadovskii vortex patches with prescribed impulse and uniquely determined traveling speed. We show that if the initial vorticity is sufficiently close to one of these patches, then the corresponding solution travels at a similar speed while staying close to members of the class up to a horizontal shift. As the closest such patch and the shift may vary over time due to ignorance of the uniqueness of the Sadovskii vortex patches, we carefully selected the class so that any perturbed solution retains its Sadovskii-like structure uniformly in time and travels nearly at the speed of the patches in the class. This result is obtained via a concentration–compactness argument and several uniform estimates for the associated variational problem, along with a shift function estimate, which is optimal, derived by tracking the horizontal center of mass of the perturbed solution. Our second result establishes the existence of Sadovskii vortices with higher regularity, including compactly supported solutions ranging from vortex patches to functions that are nearly C 3 C^{3} . Until now, the best-known regularity for such vortices was merely Lipschitz continuity of the classical Chaplygin–Lamb dipole, and our result goes beyond this. We also establish their corresponding Lyapunov stability. These are achieved by formulating and solving a family of variational problems that extend the recent framework developed for the Chaplygin–Lamb dipole and Sadovskii patches.

Key words:
vortex patch, stability, Sadovskii vortex, variational method, Lions’ concentration-compactness principle

1.
Introduction

Hydrodynamics is often best understood through rotational motion in a fluid, rather than through velocity alone.
In two-dimensional incompressible flows, the vorticity formulation of the Euler equations captures this idea by tracking how rotational motion—vorticity—is transported by the flow.
This perspective is especially useful for studying the behavior of coherent structures in ideal fluids.

One of the classical configurations is a counter-rotating vortex pair, which appears in various physical settings such as aircraft wakes and vortex ring interactions.
A particularly striking configuration arises when the two vortices touch across the symmetry axis while translating steadily–
Sadovskii vortex
.
Numerical and experimental studies have repeatedly identified this touching state as a terminal point of vortex-pair branches, indicating its special role in the structure of steady Euler flows
[
87
,
83
]
. Its appearance reflects a terminal regime in the family of steady dipole solutions: when increasing the translation speed of symmetric vortex pairs with fixed impulse, the solution branch terminates precisely at the touching configuration. Originally computed by Sadovskii
[
94
]
, this configuration involves a patch of uniform vorticity translating in the half-plane, tangent to the boundary, and enclosed by a streamline. See Section 1.1 of
[
47
]
for a detailed explanation on the history and significance of that.
Moreover, Sadovskii-type dipoles have been observed as asymptotic cross-sectional profiles in axisymmetric 3D flows, such as in the head-on collision of vortex rings.
In such settings, their stability may control long-time vorticity growth, further motivating their mathematical study
[
31
,
97
,
39
,
42
,
65
,
85
]
.

These considerations motivate a systematic study of Sadovskii vortex dipoles from a variational and dynamical perspective.
In particular, one is led to ask whether such configurations arise naturally as maximizers of an energy functional, and whether they are dynamically stable under perturbations.
This paper aims to address these questions in the setting of homogeneous incompressible Euler equations in vorticity form in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mbox{ }\partial_{t}\omega+{\bf v}\cdot\nabla\omega\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0,\\
&\mbox{ }{\bf v}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\nabla^{\perp}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}})^{-1}\omega\quad\mbox{ where }\mbox{ }\nabla^{\perp}=(\partial_{x_{2}},-\partial_{x_{1}}),\\
&\mbox{ }\omega|_{t=0}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\omega_{0},\end{aligned}\right.

with given initial data
\omega_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. Using the
stream function
defined by
\psi=\psi[\omega]:=(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}})^{-1}\omega
, we obtain the
velocity

{\bf v}
from the
vorticity

\omega
by the Biot-Savart law:

{\bf v}({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\nabla^{\perp}\psi({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\mbox{ }({\bf x}-{\bf y})^{\perp}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y},\quad\mbox{ where }\mbox{ }{\bf x}^{\perp}=(x_{2},-x_{1}).

In this paper, we deal with the existence and stability of the
Sadovskii vortex
, which is a profile of a traveling wave solution in the form of an odd-symmetric touching dipole. We establish its definition.

Definition 1.1
.

We call a function
\overline{\omega}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
a Sadovskii vortex if it is compactly supported and satisfies the following properties:

(i)

[Traveling wave]

There exists a constant
W>0
such that the function
\omega:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\omega(t,{\bf x}):=\overline{\omega}({\bf x}-(W,0)t)

solves the Euler equations (
1.1
).
We refer to such
W
as the traveling speed of
\overline{\omega}
.

(ii)

[Dipole]

\overline{\omega}
is odd in the
x_{2}
-variable:

\overline{\omega}(x_{1},x_{2})=-\overline{\omega}(x_{1},-x_{2})\geq 0\quad\text{for }x_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\,x_{2}>0.

(iii)

[Touching]
The dipole exhibits a full-contact at the origin, in the sense that there exists
R>0
such that

\left\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|{\bf x}|<R\right\}\subset{\mathrm{supp}}\,\overline{\omega}.

(iv)

[Steiner symmetry about
x_{2}
-axis]

\overline{\omega}
satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition (see Definition
1.2
) in the upper half-plane

\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:=\left\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:x_{2}>0\right\}.

We define the
Steiner symmetry condition
given in Definition
1.1
-(iv). It confines the distributions of Sadovskii vortices to be even-symmetric and concentrated about
x_{2}
-axis.

Definition 1.2
.

We say that a nonnegative function
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition if the following hold:

(i)

[Even symmetry in
x_{1}
]

f(x_{1},x_{2})=f(-x_{1},x_{2})\quad\text{for all }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
.

(ii)

[Monotonicity in
x_{1}
]

For each fixed
x_{2}>0
, the function
x_{1}\mapsto f(x_{1},x_{2})
is non-increasing on
[0,\infty)
.

One particularly intriguing aspect of
Sadovskii vortices
lies in the interplay between their geometric structure, the induced dynamics, and their regularity properties.
In general, for any odd-symmetric vortex dipole, the vorticity in the upper half-plane generates the motion in its symmetric counterpart in the lower half-plane through the Biot–Savart law, and vice versa.
This interaction is not merely translational: due to the nonlinearity of the governing equations, each vortex can influence not only the position but also the shape of its counterpart.
Crucially, as the two vortex components approach each other, this mutual influence becomes increasingly singular, posing subtle analytical difficulties.

In the scenario we focus on, the dipole is a traveling wave of the two-dimensional Euler equations, meaning that it propagates at a constant velocity while preserving its shape.
A Sadovskii vortex, in particular, represents a limiting case where the two counter-rotating vortices are in complete contact along the symmetry axis.
This contact intensifies the nonlocal interaction between two blobs to its strongest form, yet the structure manages to preserve perfect symmetry and translates steadily without deformation.
This phenomenon suggests a remarkably delicate balance between nonlinearity and symmetry, and raises fundamental questions about the existence, stability, and structural rigidity of such solutions.

1.1.
Main results

We present two main results in order: Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.6
. Each will be proved in Section
3
and Section
4
, respectively.

1.1.1.
First result
: stability of Sadovskii vortex patches.

A typical example of a Sadovskii vortex is a
Sadovskii vortex patch
,
i.e.
, a Sadovskii vortex taking the form of a step function
\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{A_{-}}
for some bounded open set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
with its mirror image set
A_{-}:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\,(x_{1},-x_{2})\in A\}
.
Recently,
[
47
]
proved its existence by solving a variational problem. See also
[
74
]
, which established existence via the Schauder fixed-point theorem.

Our first result concerns the stability of a non-empty collection of Sadovskii vortex patches. Below is a simplified version of Theorem
3.4
in Section
3
.

Theorem 1.3

(first result)
.

There exists a nonempty collection
\mathscr{A}
of measurable sets
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
satisfying
\int_{A}x_{2}\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}=1
with the following properties:

(i)

[Collection of Sadovskii vortex patches] There exists a constant
W>0
such that, for any
A\in\mathscr{A}
, the step function
\overline{\omega}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\overline{\omega}\,:=\,\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{A_{-}}\,=\,\begin{cases}\mbox{ }1&\mbox{ in }A,\\
\,-1&\mbox{ in }A_{-}:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mbox{ }(x_{1},-x_{2})\in A\},\\
\mbox{ }0&\mbox{ otherwise }\end{cases}

is a Sadovskii vortex with traveling speed
W
.

(ii)

[Stability] For each
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
such that, for any bounded initial set
\Omega_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
satisfying

\inf_{A\in\mathscr{A}}\int_{\Omega_{0}\triangle A}(x_{2}+1)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\delta,

there exists a shift function
\tau:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\tau(0)=0
for which the corresponding solution
\mathbf{1}_{\Omega(t)}-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega(t)_{-}}
of the Euler equations (
1.1
), with the initial data
\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{0}}-\mathbf{1}_{{\Omega_{0}}_{-}}
, satisfies

\inf_{A\in\mathscr{A}}\int_{\Omega(t)\triangle A^{\tau(t)}}(x_{2}+1)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ for each }\mbox{ }t\geq 0,

where the shift operator is given by

\mbox{ }A^{\tau}:=\big\{\,{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\,{\bf x}-(\tau,0)\in A\,\big\}\mbox{ }
for each
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, and the notation
A_{1}\triangle A_{2}
denotes the symmetric difference between two sets
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. Moreover, it holds that

(1.2)

\Big|\mbox{ }\tau(t)-Wt\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon(t+1)\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0

for some constant
C>0
depending only on
\left(\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\Omega_{0}}|x_{1}|\right)
.

The proof of Theorem
1.3
above follows directly from a more comprehensive statement, Theorem
1.3
, where the class of perturbed initial data is generalized from step functions to generic
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-functions that are nonnegative in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
. In this extended setting, the shift estimate (
1.2
) can be considered optimal with respect to the power exponent of
\varepsilon
appearing on the right-hand side (see Remark
3.5
for details). This optimality stems from our use of the horizontal center of mass, a quantity that was not utilized in the derivation of the previously known shift estimates for the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole
[
43
]
, the Kelvin
m
-waves
[
44
]
, and the Hill’s spherical vortex
[
45
]
. To the best of the authors’ knowledge, this optimality is established here for the first time.

Although some numerical simulations argue for the uniqueness of the Sadovskii vortex patch (see,
e.g.
,
[
74
]
), the uniqueness

\,\#(\mathscr{A})=1
is currently unknown in a rigorous way. That is, we are unable to exclude the possibility that the Sadovskii patch closest to the evolving solution
\Omega(t)
in Theorem
1.3
may vary over time. Nevertheless, its overall
Sadovskii-like
nature—in particular, the touching behavior—is preserved throughout the evolution in the following sense:
for some uniform constant
R_{0}>0
, it holds that

\Big|\,\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|{\bf x}-(\tau(t),0)|<R_{0}\}\,\Big\backslash\,\big(\Omega(t)\cup\Omega(t)_{-}\big)\,\Big|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,\lesssim\,\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ for all }\,t\geq 0,

where
|\cdot|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
In other words, in Theorem
1.3
,
any pair of core sets
\Omega(t)
and
\Omega(t)_{-}
approximately touch for all time
t\geq 0
, and the radius of the approximately touching set remains bounded uniformly for the choice of perturbed initial set
\Omega_{0}
and time
t\geq 0
.
We refer to Remark
3.2
that gives the existence of such a uniform constant
R_{0}>0
(see Definition
3.3
for the relation of
\mathscr{A}
and
S
).

Remark 1.4
.

For the description of the collection
\mathscr{A}
in Theorem
1.3
, see Definition
3.3
in Section
3
, where
\mathscr{A}
is identified as a subset of the set
S
defined in (
3.2
). The traveling speed
W
is determined uniquely in
S
(so in
\mathscr{A}
) by the identity (
3.3
) in Remark
3.1
.

Remark 1.5
.

Near any Sadovskii vortex patch in
\mathscr{A}
, one can find a perturbed set
\Omega_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with its smooth boundary
\partial\Omega_{0}
that experiences linear-in-time growth of perimeter while staying close to
\mathscr{A}
up to translation (see Corollary
3.6
).
In such scenarios, we can choose any of the following two cases:

(a)
\Omega(t)\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
is simply connected with
\partial\Omega(t)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
, or

(b)
\Omega(t)
touches
x_{1}
-axis in the sense that the set
\overline{\Omega(t)\cup\Omega(t)_{-}}
is simply connected with

\partial\Big(\overline{\Omega(t)\cup\Omega(t)_{-}}\Big)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

as illustrated in Figure
1
(see Remark
3.7
for details).

Figure 1
.
In Theorem
1.3
, a perturbed set
\Omega(t)
exhibits linear-in-time filamentation in the manner of (a)
\Omega(t)\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
for all
t\geq 0
, or (b) touching the axis of symmetry for all
t\geq 0
.

1.1.2.
Second result
: existence of regular Sadovskii vortices

Due to the strong symmetry and close interaction in the Sadovskii configuration, it is natural to ask to what extent such a vortex structure can possess regularity.
Understanding the maximal attainable smoothness for these strongly interacting, symmetry-constrained configurations is a delicate but meaningful problem for understanding the mathematical structure of such vortex configurations.

A well-known example is the
Chaplygin–Lamb dipole
, a classical Sadovskii-type vortex that is Lipschitz continuous; see
[
80
]
for its original formulation,
[
20
]
for uniqueness, and
[
1
]
for stability analysis.
Beyond Lipschitz regularity, we establish the existence of a Sadovskii vortex in the class
C^{2,\alpha}
for each
0<\alpha<1
.

Theorem 1.6

(Second result)
.

For each
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists a Sadovskii vortex
\overline{\omega}
that belongs to
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Theorem
1.6
above is contained in Theorem
4.3
, whose proof can be found in Section
4.5.1
.
As in the case of the Sadovskii vortex patch, we establish the stability of a nonempty class of
C^{2,\alpha}
-Sadovskii vortices given in Theorem
1.6
.
See Theorem
4.2
for a precise statement.

Remark 1.7

(Regularity of non-Sadovskii type traveling waves)
.

If we do not restrict attention to the Sadovskii vortex, there exist traveling vortex pair solutions to (
1.1
) with regularity beyond
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
For example,
[
98
, Theorem 11]
constructs such solutions via desingularization of point vortices using the stream function method.
An alternative way of constructing a non-touching traveling wave with higher regularity is discussed in Remark
4.17
.

1.2.
Related topics

The present work is closely related to several existing works on constructing traveling vortex solutions and analyzing their properties.
We highlight a few particularly relevant directions below.

Comparison with the other construction of Sadovskii vortex patch in Huang–Tong
[
74
]
.

The recent paper
[
47
]
used a
variational method
to prove the existence of the Sadovskii vortex patches.
The variational method allows us to obtain a stability result for Sadovskii vortex patches, which is one of the main results of the current paper. Concurrently with
[
47
]
, an independent study,
[
74
]
, also established the existence of the Sadovskii vortex patch.
They proved the existence of an upper-part boundary curve
f:[-1,1]\to[0,\infty)
such that, by defining

\Omega:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mbox{ }x_{1}\in(-1,1)\mbox{ }\mbox{ and }\mbox{ }x_{2}<f(x_{1})\},

the function
\omega_{0}:=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{-}}
solves (
1.4
), and the curve
f
has some additional properties:

(i)
f
is an even function such that
\mbox{ }f(1)=f(-1)=0\mbox{ }
and
\,f\in C[-1,1]\cap C^{1}(-1,1)
.

(ii)
f
is locally analytic in
(-1,1)
.

(iii)
f^{\prime}(0)=0
,
\mbox{ }f^{\prime}(x)<0\mbox{ }
for
x\in(0,1),\mbox{ }
and
\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 1^{-}}f^{\prime}(x)=-\infty
with

-f^{\prime}(x)\simeq x\quad\mbox{ for }\mbox{ }x\in[0,1/2]\quad\mbox{ and }\quad-f^{\prime}(x)\simeq\log\left(1+\frac{1}{1-x}\right)\quad\mbox{ for }\mbox{ }x\in[1/2,1].

Their result contains specific information on the geometric features of the boundary curve. For example, the curve
f
meets
x_{1}
-axis vertically so that the touching set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
has exactly two corners of
90
degrees at the points
(1,0)
and
(-1,0)
. However, the method used to obtain the result above differed from the variational method used in
[
47
]
and the current paper. Simply put,
[
47
]
obtained a set of traveling vortex patch solutions
\omega
in the form of a dipole

\omega=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{-}}

using the variational method
and found a certain condition under which the dipole
touches,

i.e.
,

B_{r}(0,0)\subset\overline{\Omega\cap\Omega_{-}}.

Instead,
[
74
]
characterized the touching set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
by its upper-part boundary
f:[-1,1]\to[0,\infty)
. Then, they constructed a set of functions
\mathbb{D}
and a nonlinear map
{\bf R}:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
such that any fixed point
f\in\mathbb{D}
of
{\bf R}
generates the touching set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
that travels with a constant speed in the Euler equation (
1.3
). In this setting, they obtained the result by proving the existence of such a fixed point
f\in\mathbb{D}
of the map
{\bf R}
.

Here, we compare these two papers
[
74
,
47
]
more concretely.
First,
[
47
]
showed the existence of Sadovskii vortex patch
\omega=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}
where

\Omega:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\mbox{ }\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})-Wx_{2}>0\}

in the following steps:

Step 1.
For each
\mu>0
, construct an admissible set of functions
K_{\mu}
in which each maximizer
\omega\in K_{\mu}
of the kinetic energy
E(\cdot)
has the form

\omega=\mathbf{1}_{\big\{\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma>0\big\}}\quad\mbox{ a.e. in }\mbox{ }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\quad\mbox{ for }\mbox{ }W>0,\mbox{ }\gamma\geq 0.

(We refer to (
1.5
) to remark that such a maximizer
\omega
generates a traveling wave vortex patch solution.)

Step 2.
Prove that, given that
\mu>0
is small enough, the flux constant
\gamma
of each maximizer
\omega
vanishes,
i.e.
,
\gamma=0
.

Step 3.
Prove that the
vanishing of flux constant
is an equivalent condition of
touching
of the set
\Omega
to its mirror image
\Omega_{-}
,
i.e.
,

\gamma=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\mbox{ }r>0,\tau\in\mathbb{R}\quad\mbox{such that}\quad B_{r}(\tau,0)\subset\overline{\Omega\cap\Omega_{-}}.

On the other hand,
[
74
]
proved the existence of a Sadovskii vortex patch in the following steps:

Step I.
Reformulate
the problem of finding the Sadovskii vortex patch
(in their terms, a traveling wave solution in the form of a contiguous vortex-patch dipole) into
a fixed point problem of a nonlinear map

{\bf R}
. Here,
{\bf R}
maps the upper boundary
f:[-1,1]\to[0,\infty)
of the support set
\Omega
of the Sadovskii vortex patch to a certain curve
\widetilde{f}:[-1,1]\to[0,\infty)
.

Step II.
Establish some estimates of
{\bf R}
and construct a proper function set
\mathbb{D}
on which the behavior of
{\bf R}
can be well controlled. Specifically, the properties (i) and (iii) of the resulting fixed point
f
seem to follow from the estimates of
{\bf R}
and the construction of
\mathbb{D}
. (Indeed, the set
\mathbb{D}
is constructed as

\mathbb{D}=\left\{\mbox{ }f\in C[-1,1]:\mbox{ }f\mbox{ }\mbox{ is even in }\mbox{ }[-1,1],\quad f\mbox{ }\mbox{ is non-increasing on }\mbox{ }[0,1],\quad u\leq f\leq\min\{v,M\},\quad\|f\|_{C^{1/2}}\leq C\mbox{ }\right\}

for the functions
u,v:[-1,1]\to[0,\infty)
given as

u(x)=\lambda(1-x^{2})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad v(x)=M\cdot W^{-1}\left(\Lambda(1-|x|)/M\right)\quad\mbox{ for }\mbox{ }W(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{1+\log(1+1/s)}\textrm{d}s.

Here
M,C,\lambda,\Lambda>0
are some constants.)

Step III.
Construct a continuous-in-time dynamical system as

\partial_{t}f={\bf R}(f)-f

and prove the existence of time-periodic solutions
f(t,\cdot)
(with arbitrary period
T
) of the system above using the
Schauder fixed-point theorem.

Step IV.
Obtain a
fixed point
of
{\bf R}
in
\mathbb{D}
by letting the period
T
of the time-periodic solution
f(t,\cdot)
tend to
0
.

Traveling and rotating solutions.

The system (
1.1
), due to its incompressibility and the absence of vortex-stretching terms, admits certain steady motions that preserve their profiles over time.
Two such types of steady motion are traveling wave solutions and rotating solutions.

Traveling motions are typically obtained by imposing odd symmetry and finding relative equilibrium solutions in the half-plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
. Among these, the Chaplygin–Lamb dipole is the only known nontrivial
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
solution with an explicit formula. Most other studies have focused on desingularizations of dipolar configurations—specifically, a pair of counter-rotating point vortices with opposite signs—except for the Sadovskii vortex patches, whose profile consists of a touching pair of counter-rotating vortex patches. For related results, we refer to
[
93
,
100
,
19
,
72
,
66
,
26
,
23
,
4
,
88
,
90
,
63
]
and references therein.

Additionally, there exist several periodic, non-rigid configurations of point vortex systems in a traveling frame. For instance, in two-dimensional leapfrogging motion, two pairs of oppositely signed point vortices undergo a time-periodic motion in that each pair exhibits a repeated leapfrogging motion; see
[
67
,
12
,
61
,
3
,
2
,
99
]
and references therein. For the 3D leapfrogging, we refer to

[
49
,
53
,
24
,
25
]
and references therein.

A classical example of a rotating solution is the V-state, which is a uniformly rotating vortex patch whose support is simply connected;
e.g.
, see
[
52
,
17
,
106
,
92
,
73
,
105
,
69
,
57
,
75
,
32
,
50
,
51
,
71
,
29
,
33
]
and references therein for related results. We also refer to
[
14
]
for the existence of a time quasi-periodic vortex patch solution near a uniformly rotating solution. Other substantial works have explored a co-rotating steady vortex pair;
e.g.
, see
[
95
,
106
,
63
,
72
,
66
,
63
]
and references therein.

Moreover, there also exist self-similarly expanding/contracting configurations composed of 3, 4, or 5 point vortices, known as
vortex collapse
; see
[
56
,
91
,
81
,
7
,
8
]
and references therein.

Perimeter growth of vortex patch.

It has been established that if the boundary of a vortex patch is initially smooth, then the smoothness is preserved for all time (
[
15
,
35
]
), and the perimeter of such a patch can grow at most double exponentially over time (
[
15
,
79
]
).
Corollary
3.6
provides an example of a vortex patch in the upper half-plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
away from the boundary, whose (smooth) perimeter grows at least linearly over time.
This patch remains close to the family of Sadovskii vortex patches while simultaneously experiencing elongation in its trailing region.
This phenomenon can be viewed as a 2D counterpart to the 3D result in
[
45
]
, where it was shown that a perturbed Hill’s spherical vortex undergoes linear-in-time filamentation in an axisymmetric flow without swirl.
See also
[
43
]
for the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole.
Notably, Hill’s spherical vortex
\xi_{H}=\xi_{H}(r,z)
shares structural similarities with the Sadovskii vortex patches when the axisymmetric domain
\Pi=\{(r,z):\mbox{ }r>0,\mbox{ }z\in\mathbb{R}\}
is identified with the half-plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, as its support
\{(r,z)\in\Pi:\mbox{ }r^{2}+z^{2}<1\}
touches the symmetry axis.
For related results,
[
41
]
examines vortex patches with smooth boundaries in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose perimeter grows in time.
In the whole plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
[
55
]
establishes linear-in-time perimeter growth of a vortex patch solution for all time, while
[
46
]
presents similar findings in the half-cylindrical domain
\mathbb{T}\times[0,\infty)
.

Non-uniqueness of planar flows via the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole.

The Chaplygin-Lamb dipole is a classical traveling-wave solution of the two-dimensional incompressible Euler equations
[
80
,
34
]
, and it has recently played a key role in revealing a greater degree of flexibility in the Euler flows than expected.
In
[
16
]
, the authors construct infinitely many non-conservative weak solutions to the Euler equation using localized perturbations modeled after the dipole structure.
This construction demonstrates that the Euler equations can exhibit serious non-uniqueness with integrable vorticity.
The configuration appears to rely on several key features of the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole: Lipschitz regularity of vorticity, connected compact support, separation of core and outer irrotational regions, and shape-preserving moving structures.
These elements provide an effective basis for designing scale-adapted perturbations within a convex integration scheme.

This perspective suggests that explicit stationary solutions can serve as models in analytical studies of weak solutions.
In this context, the Sadovskii vortex family constructed in the present work may also provide opportunities for exploration in a similar direction.
Although the focus of this paper is on a variational framework, the structure introduced here may have relevance for broader aspects of Eulerian dynamics.
Whether this family can provide insight for future studies on coherent structures or the approximation of irregular flows requires further investigation through rigorous analysis.

Variational construction and stability of stationary solutions.

One powerful framework for constructing steady or traveling vortex structures involves formulating them as critical points of the kinetic energy under physically motivated constraints, such as prescribed impulse or bounded circulation.
This perspective originates from the ideas of Kelvin
[
78
]
and was formalized through Arnold’s theory
[
9
,
13
]
.
In the axisymmetric setting, the seminal work of Friedman and Turkington
[
60
]
applied this variational principle to establish the existence of vortex rings.
Their approach maximizes the kinetic energy subject to constraints on total mass and impulse.
In addition, an alternative formulation using rearrangement classes was developed by Turkington
[
100
]
and further advanced by Burton
[
18
,
19
]
.
In particular, Burton
[
20
]
also addressed the uniqueness of circular vortex pairs.

The stability of such variationally constructed vortices has also been extensively studied.
Following Arnold’s formalism
[
9
]
, it has become standard to establish Lyapunov-type stability results for steady flows that are energy-critical under appropriate constraints (see
[
62
]
for an explanation).
Among various results,
[
23
]
proved the orbital stability of symmetric vortex pairs via the vorticity method.
When oppositely signed vortices are in contact,
[
1
]
established Lyapunov stability and variational characterization of the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole, while
[
104
]
refined the earlier work by Burton
[
22
]
.
Stability (with uniqueness characterization due to
[
5
]
) for the Hill’s spherical vortex was also addressed in
[
40
]
, and a variational construction of the Sadovskii patch was proposed in recent work
[
47
]
. For a stronger notion of stability (asymptotic stability) concerning some specialized flows, we refer to
[
11
,
10
,
76
,
77
]
.

Desingularization of point vortices.

In the regime where the two vortex blobs are widely separated, various techniques have been developed to construct smooth traveling dipole solutions by desingularizing singular configurations, particularly those involving point vortex pairs.
These methods aim to replace the singular structure of the idealized dipole
\delta_{(0,1)}-\delta_{(0,-1)}
with a smooth vorticity distribution supported on compact sets.
In the context of our analysis, this corresponds to the high impulse regime
\mu\gg 1
, where the vorticity becomes highly concentrated around two regions and the kinetic energy functional approximates the interaction of two point vortices.

Several mathematical strategies have been proposed for constructing such desingularized dipole solutions.
A classical and widely used approach is the
stream function method
, in which the vorticity is prescribed as a nonlinear function of the stream function, and the resulting semilinear elliptic equation is analyzed either perturbatively or via variational methods
[
90
,
107
,
98
]
.
Another approach involves
bifurcation theory
, where families of traveling or rotating dipoles arise as bifurcations from radial or circularly symmetric configurations
[
102
,
103
,
69
]
.
In certain cases,
KAM theory
has also been employed to construct quasi-periodic vortex structures with strong stability under perturbations
[
68
]
.
These constructions typically impose symmetry constraints, such as reflection or rotational symmetry, to facilitate analysis and control nonlinear interactions.

Recent works have extended these desingularization methods to more general settings; see
[
72
,
30
,
26
,
27
,
48
,
28
,
24
,
53
]
and references therein.
These results highlight the broader applicability of desingularization techniques and provide valuable information on the emergence of coherent structures from singular models.
For further discussions on classical examples of desingularized dipoles and their analogues in vortex ring settings, see also
[
70
,
101
]
.

On the terminology
Sadovskii vortex
.

The term
Sadovskii vortex
originates from Sadovskii’s 1971 work
[
94
]
, where the author studied a vortex patch on a wall—equivalently, a pair of counter-rotating patches in a dipolar configuration—enclosed by a vortex sheet or not.
Following this, similar vortex structures have been analyzed under the same terminology in various contexts
[
89
,
37
,
36
,
6
,
93
,
96
,
47
,
74
]
(see an earlier work
[
38
]
).
In particular,
[
58
,
59
]
used it to describe a generic vortex patch enclosed by a vortex sheet.

While the term
Sadovskii vortex
in literature has been used to describe vortices only of patch-type (with or without vortex sheets), we adopt the term only for vortices without vortex sheets but, in a slightly generalized sense of Definition
1.1
: an odd-symmetric pair of touching vortices in the plane whose vorticity distribution in the upper-half plane extends from characteristic functions (patches) to general non-negative functions having the Steiner symmetry (Definition
1.2
).

The last condition, the Steiner symmetry in Definition
1.2
, is indeed so natural for the energy maximizers that any known traveling wave solution in literature has the Steiner symmetry, up to the authors’ knowledge. We remark that establishing the existence of traveling wave solutions without Steiner symmetry is an interesting open question.

1.3.
Sadovskii vortex: traveling waves in the form of touching dipole

We examine the structure of the Sadovskii vortex in Definition
1.1
. We discuss its three main features in order:
odd-symmetry
,
traveling
, and
touching
. We will see that the other feature,
Steiner symmetry
, is related to the
touching
feature.

•

Odd-symmetry:
We consider the Euler equations (
1.1
) restricted to the upper half-plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
:

(1.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mbox{ }\partial_{t}\omega+{\bf v}\cdot\nabla\omega\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\\
&\mbox{ }{\bf v}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\nabla^{\perp}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}})^{-1}\omega\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\end{aligned}\right.

for a given initial data
\omega_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
. In the half-plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, the stream function
\mathcal{G}[\omega]:=(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}})^{-1}\omega
is given by

\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y},

and the velocity
{\bf v}=\nabla^{\perp}\mathcal{G}[\omega]
satisfies

{\bf v}\cdot{\bf n}\equiv 0\quad\mbox{ on }\mbox{ }\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mbox{ }x_{2}=0\}

where
{\bf n}
denotes the outward unit normal to the boundary
\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
.

•

Traveling waves:
Since the Sadovskii vortex
\overline{\omega}
gives rise to a traveling wave solution of the form

\omega(t,\cdot)=\overline{\omega}(\cdot-(W,0)t),

the equations (
1.3
) leads to the steady Euler equations for the traveling profile
\overline{\omega}
with the moving frame
(W,0)
:

(1.4)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\overline{{\bf v}}\cdot\nabla\overline{\omega}=0\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\\
&\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\overline{{\bf v}}=\nabla^{\perp}\mathcal{G}\big[\overline{\omega}\big]-(W,0)\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}:=\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mbox{ }x_{2}\geq 0\mbox{ }\}.\end{aligned}\right.

Solving the steady Euler equations (
1.4
) is typically reduced to finding
\overline{\omega}
satisfying the following relation:

(1.5)

\overline{\omega}=f\Big(\mathcal{G}\big[\overline{\omega}\big]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\Big)\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}

for some non-decreasing function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
f\equiv 0
in
(-\infty,0]
and for some constant
\gamma\geq 0
. In general,
f
is called the
vorticity function
and
\gamma
is called the flux constant. Two examples of the vorticity function are
f(s):=\mathbf{1}_{\{s>0\}}
and
f(s):=s_{+}=\max\{s,0\}
. Given that
\gamma=0
, the former corresponds to the Sadovskii vortex patch and the latter to the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole. In Theorem
4.2
, by considering the variational problem of
L^{1}\cap L^{p}
case for each
p\in(4/3,\infty)
, we seek for solutions whose vorticity function is given by
f(s)=s_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
,
i.e.
, solution in the form

\overline{\omega}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left(\mathcal{G}\left[\overline{\omega}\right]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}.

•

Touching of dipole:

We arrived at the fact that
every Sadovskii vortex satisfies the relation (
1.5
)
. It remains to deal with the third feature,
touching
. That is, in view of (
1.5
), the support of
\overline{\omega}
given as
\overline{\Omega\cup\Omega_{-}}
, where

\Omega=\left\{\mathcal{G}\left[\overline{\omega}\right]-Wx_{2}-\gamma>0\right\}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Omega_{-}=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\,(x_{1},-x_{2})\in\Omega\},

contains a touching set
\{|{\bf x}|<R\}
,
i.e.
,

\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\,\subset\,\left\{\mathcal{G}\left[\overline{\omega}\right]-Wx_{2}-\gamma>0\right\}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\quad\mbox{ for some }\mbox{ }R>0.

We will observe that the above inclusion holds if and only if the flux constant vanishes,
i.e.
,
\gamma=0
.
This can be deduced from the observations:

\mathcal{G}\big[\overline{\omega}\big]\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }0\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{G}\big[\overline{\omega}\big]\mbox{ }\equiv\mbox{ }0\quad\mbox{ on }\mbox{ }\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\{x_{2}=0\},

together with the relation:

(1.6)

\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}\big[\overline{\omega}\big]}{\partial x_{2}}(0,0)\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }2W,

where the above relation is a consequence of the Steiner symmetry condition in Definition
1.2
, which is satisfied by
\overline{\omega}
in our construction.
See (
4.51
) and (
4.53
) in the proof of Proposition
4.20
for details (see for the patch case:
[
47
, Lemma 4.5]
).

Indeed, as
\partial_{x_{2}}\mathcal{G}=\overline{v}_{1}
where
\overline{v}_{1}
is the first component of velocity
\overline{{\bf v}}=(\overline{v}_{1},\overline{v}_{2})
, the inequality (
1.6
) above is derived from the relation between the traveling speed
W
and center (horizontal) speed
\overline{v}_{1}(0,0)
of the dipolar vortex
\overline{\omega}
. From the observation that interactions in a dipole generate the steady flow
(W,0)
(a moving frame), the center of the dipole (the origin) acts as a focal point of these interactions, provided that the dipole exhibits a coherent symmetry, such as the Steiner symmetry.
See Figure
2
for a simple illustration of the case of a patch dipole.

Figure 2
.
For an odd-symmetric dipolar vortex
\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{+}}-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{-}}
, the upper set
\Omega_{+}
and the lower set
\Omega_{-}
interact to generate the steady flow
(W,0)
by which the dipole travels. Assuming further that each set is symmetrically concentrated to the vertical axis (the Steiner symmetry), two actions (
\Omega_{+}
influences
\Omega_{-}
, and vice versa) overlap at the center
(0,0)
to make the center speed
\overline{v}_{1}(0,0)
greater than
2W
.

1.4.
Key ideas

We present some essential ideas on which our main results and their proofs are based.

Vorticity method and Variational framework.

The
vorticity method
, based on a
variational framework
, is a highly effective approach for identifying steady solutions to the Euler equations and analyzing their properties, such as stability, dynamics, and regularity.
As discussed in Section
1.3
, by appropriately choosing the vorticity function
f
, one can construct
vortex dipoles
exhibiting desired features such as touching behavior and enhanced regularity.

We seek such steady solutions by formulating a
maximization problem
for a suitably designed energy functional over an appropriate admissible class.
More precisely, to construct vortex solutions of the form
\omega=f(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma)
with
f(s)=s_{+}^{2+\alpha}
(or
f(s)=\mathbf{1}_{\{s>0\}}
for patch type solutions
[
47
]
), we consider the penalized energy functional

E_{p}(\omega)=E(\omega)-\frac{1}{p}\|\omega\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}^{p},

where
E(\omega)
is the kinetic energy and
p=1+1/(2+\alpha)>4/3
(see Lemma
4.4
for the lower bound
4/3
, and Subsection
1.5
for the open case
p\leq 4/3
).
Here, the case
p=\infty
for the
Sadovskii vortex patch
can be understood in a limiting sense (with the constraint
\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\leq 1
), while the case
p=2
corresponds to the
Chaplygin-Lamb dipole
.
The admissible class is a subset of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
, subject to constraints on two fundamental conserved quantities of the Euler flow:

\textit{impulse}\,\,\|x_{2}\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\textit{mass}\,\,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}.\\

Using fundamental estimates related to the Green’s function
G({\bf x},{\bf y})
and the kinetic energy
E(\omega)
, together with the fact that Steiner symmetrization increases the kinetic energy, we establish the existence of maximizers of
E_{p}(\cdot)
under the prescribed impulse and mass bound.
Such a variational formulation naturally leads to a framework for analyzing the stability of the set of maximizers, via an adaptation of the Lions concentration–compactness principle
[
86
]
.

From this setup, it follows that any admissible perturbation
\omega+\varepsilon\eta
of a maximizer
\omega
must satisfy

E_{p}(\omega)\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega+\varepsilon\eta),

which yields the variational inequality

\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}\varepsilon}E_{p}(\omega+\varepsilon\eta)\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-\omega^{p-1}\right)\eta\,\textrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq 0.

By selecting a test function
\eta
consistent with the mass and impulse constraints, we derive the relation

(1.7)

\omega=-\Delta\mathcal{G}[\omega]=f\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)\quad\textrm{in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},

for some traveling speed
W=W(\omega)>0
and flux constant
\gamma=\gamma(\omega)\geq 0
.
This representation provides the foundation for the further construction of traveling wave solutions that contact the
x_{1}
-axis, and for characterizing broader aspects of such a structure, as discussed below.

Touching behavior and the role of the impulse and flux constant.

Building on the vorticity formulation (
1.7
), we investigate the conditions under which a dipole configuration makes contact with the
x_{1}
-axis.
While
[
47
, Theorem 4.1]
establishes this touching behavior for vortex patches with Heaviside-type vorticity functions, the core idea—that touching occurs when the impulse is sufficiently small—remains valid in more general settings.
With additional analysis, the argument extends to symmetrically concentrated dipoles associated with non-decreasing vorticity functions.
Here, we outline the main strategy of the proof.

Heuristically, when the averaged vertical center of mass is quite small;

\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}}\mbox{ }\ll\mbox{ }1,

the dipole tends to remain compact and close to the symmetry axis.
In this regime, the two counter-rotating vortex blobs naturally attract each other, resulting in contact with the
x_{1}
-axis—a behavior we refer to as
touching
.
In contrast, when the impulse dominates the mass, the dipole remains concentrated but stays away from the axis, thereby preventing contact.

This intuition is supported by our variational framework.
A key observation is that
touching
necessarily implies that the flux constant vanishes,
i.e.
,
\gamma=0
in (
1.7
).
This follows from the boundary behavior of the stream function:

\mathcal{G}[\omega](x_{1},0)\equiv 0.

Thus, if
\gamma>0
, the support of
\omega
, given by
\{\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma>0\}
, remains strictly away from the
x_{1}
-axis.
In other words,
\gamma>0
prevents contact, so touching is only possible if
\gamma=0
.

To show that small impulse forces
\gamma=0
, we examine how the quantities determined by the maximizer
\omega
—such as traveling speed
W
, flux constant
\gamma
, maximal penalized energy, mass
\|\omega\|_{1}
, and
L^{p}
-norm
\|\omega\|_{p}
—are functionally related.
This relation is formalized in Lemma
4.21
.
In addition, when
p>4/3
, scaling arguments provide insight into how the impulse
\mu
influences these quantities, particularly the maximal energy.
Combining these observations, we analyze the vertical mass distribution of the maximizer
\omega
and conclude that any sufficiently small impulse
\mu
enforces
\gamma=0
.

Although
\gamma=0
is necessary for touching, in general it is not sufficient without further structural assumptions.
We show that if the vortex patch is
symmetrically concentrated
about the
x_{2}
-axis, then
\gamma=0
indeed implies touching.
The main idea is to compare the stream function
\psi[\omega]
with the linear drift term
Wx_{2}
.
Following
[
47
, Lemma 4.5]
, we interpret
W
as the spatial average of
\partial_{x_{2}}\psi[\omega_{-}]
over the support of
\omega_{+}
, where the odd-symmetric dipole
\omega
is decomposed as

\omega=\omega_{+}-\omega_{-},\quad\omega_{+}:=\omega\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{2}>0\}},\quad\omega_{-}:=-\omega\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{2}<0\}}.

Let
\mathbf{u}=(u^{1},u^{2})
be the velocity field induced by
\omega
via the Biot–Savart law.
Due to the odd symmetry of
\omega
, we obtain the identity

u^{1}=2\partial_{x_{2}}\psi[\omega_{-}]\quad\text{on the }x_{1}\text{-axis}.

Since the expression for
\partial_{x_{2}}\psi[\omega_{-}]
is explicit, we can compute that
u^{1}
attains its maximum at the origin when
\omega
is symmetrically concentrated:

u^{1}(0,0)=2\partial_{x_{2}}\psi[\omega_{-}](0,0)>2W\quad\textrm{(See Figure~\ref{fig: center speed}.)}

This inequality implies that the set
\{\psi-Wx_{2}>0\}
reaches the
x_{1}
-axis.
Therefore, under the symmetry assumption, the condition
\gamma=0
is both necessary and sufficient for touching.

In summary, when the impulse
\mu
is sufficiently small relative to mass, the flux constant
\gamma
must vanish.
Moreover, in this regime, the mass constraint is not saturated (
\|\omega\|_{1}
is strictly less than the prescribed mass bound), confirming that the resulting dipole prefers to concentrate near the symmetry axis,
i.e.
,
Sadovskii
vortex.

Stability of Sadovskii vortex.

We establish the Lyapunov stability of a family of Sadovskii vortices (see Theorem
1.3
for patches, and Theorem
4.2
for
C^{2,\alpha}
-regular vortices for
\alpha<1
).
The main tool is the Lions’ concentration–compactness lemma (Lemma
2.4
), applied to variational problems that characterize these vortices as energy maximizers under suitable constraints.

We now briefly explain the underlying mechanism.
The energy maximization problems are formulated under an exact impulse constraint
\|x_{2}\omega\|_{L^{1}}=\mu
for each fixed parameter
\mu>0
, together with an upper bound on the mass
\|\omega\|_{L^{1}}
.
Since both the impulse and the energy
E(\omega)
(or
E_{p}(\omega)
) are conserved under the 2D Euler flow, any solution
\theta(t)
to (
1.3
) that starts sufficiently close—in the impulse norm
|x_{2}(\cdot)|_{L^{1}}
—to the set of energy maximizers remains close to it for all time:

\|x_{2}\theta(t)\|_{L^{1}}\simeq\mu\quad\mbox{ for all }t\geq 0,

and its energy also stays close to the maximal value.

At this point, the Lions’ concentration-compactness lemma
[
86
]
(see Lemma
2.4
) guarantees that any sequence with bounded impulse admits one of three scenarios:
(i) concentration,

(ii) vanishing,
and
(iii) dichotomy(splitting)
.
Here, the fact that the energy of
\theta(t)
is close to the maximal energy allows us to rule out the cases of
(ii) vanishing
and
(iii) dichotomy
.
Indeed, this conclusion exploits the singular structure of the Green’s function

G({\bf x},{\bf y})\sim\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right),

which can blow up when
|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\to 0
or when both
x_{2},y_{2}\to\infty
while keeping
|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|
bounded.
In our setting, the prescribed impulse
\|x_{2}\omega\|_{L^{1}}=\mu
plays a crucial role in preventing such escape to infinity, effectively forcing any maximizing sequence to remain concentrated in a compact region.
This eliminates the possibilities of
(ii) vanishing
and
(iii) dichotomy
in the Lions’ lemma, leaving only
(i) concentration
.
It follows that the solution
\theta(t)
remains close (up to translation) to the set of maximizers for all time
t\geq 0
.

Regularity of Sadovskii vortex.

One of a notable feature of the vorticity method is that, once the condition
\gamma=0
is enforced, one can construct a family of Sadovskii vortex—within the range permitted by the chosen vorticity function
f
—that includes both the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole, which is Lipschitz continuous, and the Sadovskii patch as special cases.
The key is that the variational approach yields a maximizer
\widetilde{\omega}
satisfying the structural relation

\omega=f(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}).

By choosing a vorticity function of the form
f(s)=s_{+}^{\alpha}
, we effectively generate Sadovskii vortices with regularity almost
C^{3}
, thereby extending beyond the patch type or
C^{0,1}
examples.
However, achieving this regularity involves a delicate issue arising from the coupling across the symmetry axis enforced by the odd symmetry of
\omega
, as well as a compatibility between the stream function
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
and the linear function
Wx_{2}
.
This issue lies in controlling how derivatives behave near the touching axis, where the two vortex blobs interact most strongly.
Below, we briefly describe how these aspects are handled in our proof.

As our primary focus is on Sadovskii vortices, we restrict our attention to the case
\gamma=0
in (
1.7
).
On the other hand, when
\gamma>0
, the support of the vorticity in the upper half-plane remains strictly separated from the
x_{1}
-axis, making the regularity analysis more straightforward.
We begin by observing that, for small enough
\mu
and
p>4/3
, the maximizer
\omega\in L^{p}
obtained from the variational problem satisfies the relation above with
f(s)=s_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
, leading to the semilinear elliptic equation for
\psi
(the odd extension of
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
):

\displaystyle-\Delta\psi({\bf x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(\psi\left({\bf x}\right)-Wx_{2}\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\omega(x_{1},x_{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}\geq 0,\\
-\left(-\psi\left({\bf x}\right)+Wx_{2}\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }-\omega(x_{1},-x_{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}<0.\end{array}\right.

Since
\omega\in L^{p}
, classical Calderón-Zygmund theory combined with Sobolev embeddings implies that
\psi
belongs to
C^{0,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
\beta\in(0,1)
.
To improve the regularity of
\psi
, we first analyze derivatives in the
x_{1}
-direction using finite difference quotient, obtaining the regularity determined by the choice of the vorticity function
f(s)=s_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
.
What remains is to extend the
x_{1}
-regularity across the symmetry axis in the
x_{2}
-direction, making use of the elliptic structure and odd symmetry:

\displaystyle-\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\psi({\bf x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(\psi-Wx_{2}\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\psi&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}\geq 0,\\
-\left(Wx_{2}-\psi\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\psi&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}<0.\end{array}\right.

This structure creates a feedback mechanism: improved regularity of
\psi
in
x_{1}
enhances the right-hand side above, which in turn raises regularity in
x_{2}
.
Through this process, we show in Proposition
4.15
that
\psi
becomes a classical solution, and
\widetilde{\omega}=-\Delta\psi
inherits precisely the regularity dictated by the smoothness of
f
, via Schauder theory.
Moreover, by analyzing the nonlocal velocity component
\partial_{x_{2}}\psi
near the origin, one can confirm that the regularity of
\omega
cannot exceed that of
f
, establishing a sharp boundary for possible regularity improvements; see Remark
4.25
for details.

Shift function
\tau(t)
used in stability of Sadovsii patches.

We aim to demonstrate that, in Theorem
1.3
, the perturbed set
\Omega(t)
travels nearly with a velocity
(W,0)
, where
W
is the unique traveling speed of Sadovskii patches in the class
\mathscr{A}
. More precisely, we will prove that the shift function
\tau(t)
, which describes the dominant translation of
\Omega(t)
in Theorem
1.3
, satisfies

(1.8)

\tau(t)\,\simeq\,Wt.

To achieve this, we proceed in two steps:

(i)

Relate
\tau(t)
to the
(horizontal) center of mass of

\Omega(t)
as

\tau(t)\,\simeq\,\fint_{\Omega(t)}x_{1}\,d\mathbf{x}.

(ii)

Show that the
center of mass
grows almost linearly in time with speed
W
,

\fint_{\Omega(t)}x_{1}\,d\mathbf{x}\,\simeq\,Wt.

We now explain these two steps in detail to achieve (
1.8
).

Step (i): Relating
\tau(t)
and the center of mass.

Based on the Lyapunov stability of
\mathscr{A}
in Theorem
1.3
, we choose a solution
\theta(t,\cdot)
that remains sufficiently close to the set
\mathscr{A}
up to a horizontal translation
(-\tau(t),0)
for all time
t\geq 0
,
i.e.
,

(1.9)

\inf_{A\in\mathscr{A}}\left\|\mathbf{1}_{\Omega(t)}-\mathbf{1}_{A^{\tau(t)}}\right\|\ll 1\quad\mbox{ where }\mbox{ }\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}

with the shift operator
A^{\tau(t)}=A+(\tau(t),0)
.

Under this assumption, the centers of mass of
\Omega(t)
and
A^{\tau(t)}
are close. Since each
A\in\mathscr{A}
is centralized (
i.e.
,
\int_{A}x_{1}\,d\mathbf{x}=0
), the center of
A^{\tau(t)}
is
\tau(t)
, and so we find that the center of
\Omega(t)
is close to
\tau(t)
,
i.e.
,

\fint_{\Omega(t)}x_{1}\,\textrm{d}\mathbf{x}\,\simeq\,\tau(t).

Step (ii): Linear growth of the center of mass.

Next, we show that the center of mass moves almost linearly in time with speed
W
. Differentiating it in time and using the Euler equations, we have

(1.10)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(\fint_{\Omega(t)}x_{1}\,\textrm{d}\mathbf{x}\right)\,=\,\fint_{\Omega(t)}v_{1}[\Omega(t)]\,\textrm{d}\mathbf{x},

where
v_{1}[\Omega(t)]
is the first component of the velocity field induced by the set
\Omega(t)
. It is given by

v_{1}[\Omega(t)](\mathbf{x})=\int_{\Omega(t)}G_{x_{2}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\,\textrm{d}{\bf y}

where
G
is the Green’s function of the half plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
. Moreover, since any
A\in\mathscr{A}
is a traveling ansatz of a Sadovskii vortex, its traveling speed
W
is expressed as

(1.11)

W\,=\,\fint_{A^{\tau(t)}}v_{1}[A^{\tau(t)}]\,\textrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

For details, we refer to
[
47
, Remark 3.16]
. (In the above relation (
1.11
), any other number can replace the shift operator
\tau(t)
, due to invariance of the kernel
G_{x_{2}}
under horizontal translations.)

Using the proximity assumption (
1.9
), we deduce that the two right-hand side terms of (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) are close to each other,
i.e.
, we arrive at

\fint_{\Omega(t)}x_{1}\,\textrm{d}\mathbf{x}\mbox{ }\simeq\mbox{ }Wt.

Together with
Step (i)
, we achieve our goal:

\tau(t)\,\simeq\,Wt.

Minimal
L^{1}
-mass condition.

The collection
\mathscr{A}
in our first result (Theorem
1.3
; see Theorem
3.4
for a generalized version) originates from the collection
S
in (
3.2
): the set of maximizers of kinetic energy in a certain admissible class (see Definition
3.3
in Section
3
for details).
To ensure
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
convergence of perturbed solutions near Sadovskii patches, we only consider
the maximizers having the minimal
L^{1}
norm in
S
.
To examine the necessity of such a restriction, let us attempt to prove the stability of
S

without
imposing the minimal mass condition. In the proof below,
L^{1},L^{2}
-norms are often not distinguished, because we concern vortex patches
\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}
.

I.

Assumption.
Suppose a sequence of initial data
\theta_{0,n}
converges to the set
S
in the combined norm:

(1.12)

\inf_{\omega\in S}\left\{\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0,n}-\omega)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}\to 0,

and we desire to obtain the convergence: for each
t\geq 0
,

\inf_{\omega\in S}\left\{\|\theta_{n}(t)-\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{n}(t)-\omega)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}\to 0.

II.

Convergence in impulse norm.
Fix
t\geq 0
. The assumption (
1.12
)
gives the impulse convergence

\|x_{2}\theta_{0,n}\|_{L^{1}}=\|x_{2}\theta_{n}(t)\|_{L^{1}}\to 1

due to the normalized impulse condition in
S
. Applying the Lions’ concentration-compactness lemma
[
86
]
(Lemma
2.4
) to the convergence
\|x_{2}\theta_{n}(t)\|_{L^{1}}\to 1
, the sequence is
concentrated
in the impulse norm,
i.e.
, for some
R>0
,

(1.13)

\int_{|{\bf x}|>R,\,x_{2}>0}x_{2}\theta_{n}^{\tau_{n}}(t)\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}\,\ll\,1\quad\mbox{ for a (horizontal) shift sequence}\mbox{ }\{\tau_{n}\}.

It is a result of excluding
Vanishing
and
Dichotomy(splitting)
in Lemma
2.4
under the impulse norm, in the following way.

•

The energy convergence
E(\theta_{n}(t))=E(\theta_{0,n})\to I\mbox{ (maximal energy)}
implies that
\theta_{n}(t)
is a maximizing sequence, which means there is no
Vanishing
of impulse.

•

The case of
Dichotomy
is ruled out by the strict super-additivity of
I
with respect to the impulse. Indeed, under scaled impulse condition
\int x_{2}\omega=\mu
, one can easily find that the maximal energy
I
is scaled to
I\mu^{4/3}
, and it gives the (strict) super-additivity

\mu^{4/3}\,>\,(\mu-\alpha)^{4/3}+\alpha^{4/3},\quad 0<\alpha<\mu.

In sum, the impulse of maximizing sequence
\theta_{n}^{\tau_{n}}(t)
is (almost) concentrated in a compact set (as in (
1.13
)). It leads to the convergence to
S
under the impulse norm (since the set
S
is invariant under translation
\tau_{n}
).

III.

Convergence in
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
norm.
On the other hand, the Lions’ lemma does not give strong
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
convergence to
S
, due to the possibility of
Dichotomy
in
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
norm (as we do not have any super-additivity of the maximal energy in
L^{1}
norm).

We instead strengthen our assumption (
1.12
) as follows.
We replace
S
with
\mathscr{A}
by imposing the minimal mass condition
(as in Definition
3.3
). Thereby, by denoting the minimal
L^{1}
-mass in
S
as
\nu>0
, we have the norm convergence
\|\theta_{n}(t)\|_{L^{1}}\to\nu\mbox{ }
(minimal mass). Moreover, as a maximizing sequence,
\theta_{n}^{\tau_{n}}(t)
cannot experience mass loss outside the compact domain
\{|{\bf x}|\leq R\}
in which its impulse is concentrated (see (
1.13
)), since any function having less mass than
\nu
is unable to maximize the energy. Therefore
the maximizing sequence is (almost) concentrated in the compact domain
\{|{\bf x}|\leq R\}
in
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
norm.
In this way, we obtain strong
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
convergence to
S
.

Remark 1.8
.

The minimal mass condition is only needed to ensure strong convergence in
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
. Stability in the impulse norm alone can be proved without it (see Theorem
3.20
).

Remark 1.9
.

For the
L^{1}\cap L^{p}
setting of Theorem
4.2
, we instead maximize the penalized energy

E_{p}(\omega):=E(\omega)-\frac{1}{p}\|\omega\|_{L^{p}}^{p}.

In this setting, the energy convergece
E_{p}(\theta_{n}(t))\to I_{\mu}(p)
directly gives strong-
L^{p}
convergence

\theta_{n}(t)\to\omega\quad\text{ in }L^{p}.

This enables us to prove the stability of the maximizer set without requiring the minimal mass condition.

1.5.
Open problems

This section outlines some open questions arising from our study of Sadovskii vortices.

Uniqueness and Geometric features.

While the existence and qualitative properties of Sadovskii vortices are rigorously established in this work, the question of uniqueness remains open.
This issue has also arisen in earlier studies.
In particular, both
[
47
]
and
[
74
]
independently constructed symmetric traveling vortex patches touching the symmetry axis, though by fundamentally different methods.
Whether these constructions yield the same solution is unknown, and
[
47
]
explicitly poses the uniqueness problem as an open question.
For comparison, uniqueness is currently known only in the special case
p=2
, corresponding to the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole; see
[
20
,
21
,
1
,
104
]
.

Even when the analysis is restricted to symmetric configurations, the uniqueness problem remains nontrivial.
For example, in the case of the Chaplygin-Lamb dipole,
[
1
]
proved uniqueness via an isometric transformation of the stream function combined with the moving plane method.
However, no analogous structure is known in our setting, and no principle currently rules out the existence of multiple symmetric maximizers with the same physical parameters.

Moreover, the precise geometry of the vortex core (
i.e.
, the support of the vorticity) is not yet fully understood.
It remains unclear how the core boundary meets the symmetry axis, whether the core is convex, and how its curvature behaves.
These geometric features are closely tied to regularity, uniqueness, and stability, and are expected to play a central role in the broader mathematical theory of Sadovskii-type vortices.

Touching regularity beyond
C^{3}
(
p\leq 4/3
).

In this work, we construct Sadovskii vortices with regularity up to
C^{2,1-}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by maximizing the penalized energy
E_{p}(\cdot)
(
p>4/3
) over the class
K_{\mu}(p)
of vortices with exact impulse
\mu
.
A key step in our approach involves first considering a relaxed admissible class
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}(p)
, where the impulse is bounded above by
\mu
, and then showing that the supremum over
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}(p)
coincides with that over
K_{\mu}(p)
,
i.e.
,
\widetilde{I}_{\mu}(p)=I_{\mu}(p)
.

This allows us to construct non-trivial maximizers when
p>4/3
, due to the strict positivity of the maximal energy
I_{\mu}(p)
derived from scaling arguments; Lemma
4.4
.
Once a non-trivial maximizer
\omega
is obtained, we use the vorticity formulation (
4.13
) to recover a solution of the semilinear elliptic equation and subsequently deduce its regularity and touching behavior.
However, when
p\leq 4/3
, this variational procedure fails to produce maximizers exhibiting contact with the symmetry axis.

The obstruction becomes more transparent when considering a Pohožaev-type identity satisfied by any non-trivial maximizer
\omega\in K_{\mu}
that fulfills the vorticity formulation (
•
‣
1.3
) (see Lemma
4.21
):

I_{\mu}(p)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{3p-4}{4p-4}W\mu\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2}\gamma\|\omega\|_{1}.

For the Sadovskii vortex of interest, we require
\gamma=0
, which simplifies the identity to
I_{\mu}(p)=\frac{3p-4}{4p-4}W\mu
.
When
p=4/3
, this yields
I_{\mu}(p)=0
, indicating a loss of coercivity at this critical threshold.
In such cases, even if a non-trivial maximizer exists in
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}(p)
, it becomes difficult to distinguish it from the trivial solution
\omega=0
through variational methods alone.

Consequently, within the current penalized energy framework, establishing the existence or non-existence of Sadovskii vortices with
C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
or higher regularity remains an open problem.
New ideas or alternative approaches seem necessary to go beyond the regularity threshold achieved here.
We also remark that in non-touching configurations, such as widely separated dipoles, it is often possible to construct traveling wave solutions with higher regularity, see Remark
4.17
.
This contrast highlights not only the special difficulty of the Sadovskii case, where the vortex components are in full contact and the nonlinear interaction is strongest, but also the limitations of current variational methods in handling such singular structures.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.

•

Section
2
presents some preliminary materials, including several lemmas that will be used throughout the paper.

•

In Section
3
, a generalized form of our first main result (Theorem
1.3
) is established in Theorem
3.4
. Section
3
is then mainly focused on the proof of Theorem
3.4
.
One of its applications, the perimeter growth, is also obtained (Corollary
3.6
).

•

In Section
4
, we prove the second main result (Theorem
1.6
).
Section
4
is primarily devoted to establishing Theorem
4.2
and proving Theorem
4.3
(Theorem
1.6
) as an application of Theorem
4.2
.

•

In Appendix
A
, we provide the proof of Lemma
3.26
, which ensures a suitable approximation of a patch-type maximizer.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations

Let
\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the two-dimensional Euclidean space.
We define the upper half-space as

\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:=\left\{\,\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,x_{2}>0\,\right\},

and denote its closure by
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
.
Similarly, we define the lower half-space
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
and its closure
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}}
.
The topological boundary of
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
is the
x_{1}
-axis, denoted by
\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
(which coincides with
\mathbb{R}
).
We write
|A|
to denote the Lebesgue measure of a measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We abbreviate the
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
-norm as

\|f\|_{p}:=\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq p\leq\infty,

along with the weighted
L^{1}
-norm

\|x_{2}f\|_{1}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}|f(\mathbf{x})|\,\textrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

Additionally, we define the combined
L^{p}\cap L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
-norm as

\|f\|_{L^{p}\cap L^{q}}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\|f\|_{p}+\|f\|_{q}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq p,q\leq\infty.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and a positive integer
m
, we denote by
C^{m,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
the space of functions
f
such that
\partial^{\ell}_{\mathbf{x}}f
is uniformly bounded and continuous for
|\ell|\leq m-1
, and
\partial^{\ell}_{\mathbf{x}}f
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for
|\ell|=m
.
The corresponding norm is given by

\|f\|_{C^{m,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\sum_{|\ell|\leq m}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\ell}f\|_{\infty}+\sum_{|\ell|=m}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}\\
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{|\partial_{{\bf x}}^{\ell}f(\mathbf{x})-\partial_{{\bf x}}^{\ell}f(\mathbf{y})|}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{\alpha}},

where
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is a multi-index with integers
\ell_{j}
(
j=1,2
), and

|\ell|=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},\quad\partial_{{\bf x}}^{\ell}f:=\frac{\textrm{d}^{|\ell|}f}{\textrm{d}x_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\textrm{d}x_{2}^{\ell_{2}}}.

We frequently use the function
(\cdot)_{+}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

s_{+}:=\max\{s,0\}.

For a measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, we denote its characteristic function by
\mathbf{1}_{A}
, given by

\mathbf{1}_{A}(\mathbf{x}):=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\mathbf{x}\in A,\\
0,&\text{if }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus A.\end{cases}

For each
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, we define its reflection point across the
x_{1}
-axis as
\mathbf{x}^{*}:=(x_{1},-x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
.
Given a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define its
odd extension

\widetilde{f}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\widetilde{f}(\mathbf{x}):=\begin{cases}f(\mathbf{x}),&\text{if }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\\
-f(\mathbf{x}^{*}),&\text{if }\mathbf{x}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\\
0,&\text{if }\mathbf{x}\in\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}.\end{cases}

The constant
C
in (in)equalities may vary from line to line, with its dependencies understood from the context.
We sometimes write
\lesssim
(or
\simeq
) as shorthand.

2.2.
Common technical lemmas used both in Sections
3
and
4
.

We introduce some lemmas, including inequalities concerning the Green’s function
G({\bf x},{\bf y})
for the Laplacian defined in the half-plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
and kinetic energy
E(\omega)
. We recall that the Green’s function
G
of the half plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
is given by

G({\bf x},{\bf y}):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\quad\mbox{ for }\mbox{ }{\bf x},{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},

and the kinetic energy
E(\omega)
is given by

E(\omega):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf x})\omega({\bf y})\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}\,\textrm{d}{\bf y}.

Lemma 2.1
.

There exists a constant
C=C(p)>0
such that, for each
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
, we have the following:

(1)

for
p>2
,

(2.1)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G\left({\bf x},{\bf y}\right)^{p}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{2},

(2)

for
p\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
,

(2.2)

\int_{y_{2}>0,\,\,|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<\alpha}G\left({\bf x},{\bf y}\right)^{p}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}\alpha,

(3)

for
p\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
,

(2.3)

\int_{0<y_{2}\leq\alpha}G\left({\bf x},{\bf y}\right)^{p}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{1/2}\alpha^{3/2}.

Proof.

(1) We set

J\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }(4\pi)^{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G\left({\bf x},{\bf y}\right)^{p}\textrm{d}{\bf y}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left\{\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\right\}^{p}\textrm{d}{\bf y}.

By changing variables
y_{1}\to x_{1}-x_{2}y_{1}
and
y_{2}\to x_{2}-x_{2}y_{2}
, we see that

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=x_{2}^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left\{\log\left(1+\frac{4(1-y_{2})}{|{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\right\}^{p}\textrm{d}y_{1}\textrm{d}y_{2}=x_{2}^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}+\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\right)

\displaystyle=:x_{2}^{2}\left(J_{1}+J_{2}\right).

Since
0<1-y_{1}<1
for
y_{1}\in(0,1)
and
|y_{1}|\leq|y|
, using the integration by parts, we obtain

J_{1}\leq C\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left\{\log\left(1+\frac{4}{t^{2}}\right)\right\}^{p}\textrm{d}t<\infty

for all
p\geq 1
.
For
J_{2}
, we again decompose it by

J_{2}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left\{\log\left(1+\frac{4(1+y_{2})}{|{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\right\}^{p}\textrm{d}y_{1}\textrm{d}y_{2}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }J_{2,1}+J_{2,2}.

As in the case of
J_{1}
, we have
J_{2,1}<\infty
for any
p\geq 1
.
Regarding
J_{2,2}
, it is clear that

J_{2,2}\leq\int_{\{|{\bf y}|\leq N\}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\textrm{d}{\bf y}+\int_{\{|{\bf y}|>N,y_{2}>1\}}\textrm{d}{\bf y},\quad N>0

and the first term of the above expression is finite for any
p\geq 1
.
For the second term, observe that
y_{2}>1
implies
1+y_{2}\simeq y_{2}
and

\frac{1+y_{2}}{|{\bf y}|^{2}}\mbox{ }\simeq\mbox{ }\frac{y_{2}}{|{\bf y}|^{2}}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{\sin\theta}{r}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{r},

where
{\bf y}=(y_{1},y_{2})=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)
in a polar coordinate system.
Then by the fact that
\log(1+r^{-1})<r^{-1}
, we have

\int_{\{|{\bf y}|>N,y_{2}>1\}}\left|\log\left(1+\frac{4(1+y_{2})}{|{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\right|^{p}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{N}^{\infty}\left|\log\left(1+\frac{C}{r}\right)\right|^{p}r\textrm{d}r\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\int_{N}^{\infty}r^{-p+1}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\infty

provided that
-p+1<-1
, that is,
p>2
. By collecting the above estimates for
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
, we get (
2.1
).

(2) To prove (
2.2
), observe that by changing variables as above,

\displaystyle\int_{y_{2}>0,\,\,|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<\alpha}G\left({\bf x},{\bf y}\right)^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }x_{2}^{2}\int_{|{\bf y}|<\alpha/x_{2}}\left|\log\left(1+\frac{4(1-y_{2})}{|{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\right|^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\simeq\mbox{ }x_{2}^{2}\int_{0}^{\alpha/x_{2}}r\left|\log\left(1+\frac{4(r+1)}{r^{2}}\right)\right|^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\alpha x_{2},

where the last inequality is due to the fact that

r\left|\log\left(1+\frac{4(r+1)}{r^{2}}\right)\right|^{p}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }1\quad\textrm{on}\,\,\mathbb{R}_{+}

for any
p\geq 1
.

(3) One obtains (
2.3
) by direct calculations on logarithmic function.
More precisely, we have

\int_{0<y_{2}\leq\alpha}G\left({\bf x},{\bf y}\right)^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\int_{0<y_{2}\leq\alpha}\left|\log\left(1+\frac{4\alpha x_{2}}{|{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\right|^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\alpha^{3/2}x_{2}^{1/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\log\left(1+\frac{1}{t^{2}}\right)\right|^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}t.

The lemma is proved.
∎

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and

\theta\in\left(\max\left\{0,\frac{p-2}{2p-2}\right\},1\right).

There is a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(p,\theta)
satisfying

(2.4)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\mathrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{1}x_{2}^{2(1-\theta)(1-1/p)}||\omega||_{1}^{\theta}||\omega||_{p}^{1-\theta},

for any
\omega,\eta\in L^{p}\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
such that
x_{2}\omega,x_{2}\eta\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
.
Furthermore, we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf x})\eta({\bf y})\mbox{ }\mathrm{d}{\bf x}\mathrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{2}||\omega||_{1}^{1/2}||\omega||_{p}^{1/2}||x_{2}\eta||_{1}^{1-1/p}||\eta||_{1}^{1/p},

(2.6)

\displaystyle E(\omega)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{2}||x_{2}\omega||_{1}^{1-1/p}||\omega||_{1}^{1/2+1/p}||\omega||_{p}^{1/2},

(2.7)

\displaystyle\left|E(\omega)-E(\eta)\right|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{2}\|\omega-\eta\|_{1}^{1/2}\|\omega-\eta\|_{p}^{1/2}\|x_{2}(\omega+\eta)\|_{1}^{1-1/p}\|\omega+\eta\|_{1}^{1/p},

for some
C_{2}=C_{2}(p)>0
.

Proof.

We first prove (
2.4
).
If
p\leq 2
, for
q\in(1,p)
and
1/q=\theta+(1-\theta)/p
with
\theta\in(0,1)
, then by denoting the left-hand side of (
2.4
) as
\psi=\psi({\bf x})
, we see

\displaystyle\psi({\bf x})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{q^{\prime}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right)^{1/q^{\prime}}||\omega||_{q}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{2/q^{\prime}}||\omega||_{q}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{2(1-\theta)(1-1/p)}||\omega||_{1}^{\theta}||\omega||_{p}^{1-\theta},

where the second inequality is due to (
2.1
) of Lemma
2.1
and the fact that
q^{\prime}>2
.
This implies (
2.4
) for
p\in(1,2]
.
Similarly, if
p>2
, by taking
q\in(1,2)
and
\theta\in((p-2)/(2p-2),1)
such that
1/q=\theta+(1-\theta)/p
, we have

\displaystyle\psi({\bf x})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{2/q^{\prime}}||\omega||_{q}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{2(1-\theta)(1-1/p)}||\omega||_{1}^{\theta}||\omega||_{p}^{1-\theta}.

Thus, we obtain (
2.4
) for
p>2
and
\theta
satisfying
(p-2)(2p-2)<\theta<1
.

We now prove (
2.5
) and (
2.6
).
Since
(p-2)/(2p-2)\leq 1/2
, taking
\theta=1/2
in (
2.4
) implies

\displaystyle\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf y})\eta({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int\psi({\bf x})\eta({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mbox{ }C||\omega||_{1}^{1/2}||\omega||_{p}^{1/2}\int x_{2}^{1-1/p}\eta({\bf x})\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C||\omega||_{1}^{1/2}||\omega||_{p}^{1/2}||x_{2}\eta||_{1}^{1-1/p}||\eta||_{1}^{1/p}.

Thus (
2.5
) is obtained.
The estimate (
2.6
) follows from (
2.5
) by taking
\eta=\omega
.

Finally, we prove (
2.7
).
By the symmetry
G({\bf x},{\bf y})=G({\bf y},{\bf x})
, we directly check that

E(\omega)-E(\eta)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2}\iint G\left({\bf x},{\bf y}\right)\cdot\left(\omega-\eta\right)\left({\bf x}\right)\cdot\omega\left({\bf y}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}+\frac{1}{2}\iint G\left({\bf x},{\bf y}\right)\cdot\left(\omega-\eta\right)\left({\bf x}\right)\cdot\eta\left({\bf y}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y},

and this with (
2.6
) implies (
2.7
).
The lemma is proved.
∎

We also give a lemma which tells us that any nonnegative
L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
function with
1<p<\infty
admits its Steiner rearrangement,
i.e.
, a rearrangement having the same distributions and satisfying the Steiner symmetry condition. It is quite a classical argument, and one can find similar statements in several references,
e.g.
, we refer to
[
1
, Proposition 3.1]
.

Lemma 2.3

(Steiner symmetrization)
.

For
\omega\geq 0
satisfying
\omega\in L^{p}\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
with
1<p<\infty
and
x_{2}\omega\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
, there exists a Steiner symmetrization
\omega^{*}\geq 0
of
\omega
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle||\omega^{*}||_{q}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }||\omega||_{q}\quad(1\leq q\leq p),

\displaystyle||x_{2}\omega^{*}||_{1}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }||x_{2}\omega||_{1},

\displaystyle E(\omega^{*})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }E(\omega).

We also recall the Lions’ concentration-compactness lemma, which is one of the crucial arguments used in this paper. It can be found in Lions’ work
[
86
, Lemma I.1]
for the whole domain
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, which has been followed by some of its slight variants (
e.g.
,
[
23
,
1
]
). Below is the one given in
[
1
]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

[
1
, Lemma 4.1]
Let
0<\mu<\infty
. Let
\{\rho_{n}\}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
satisfy

\rho_{n}\geq 0\quad\mbox{ for }n\geq 1,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\rho_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=:\mu_{n}\to\mu\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }\mbox{ }n\to\infty.

There exists a subsequence
\{\rho_{n_{k}}\}
satisfying the one of the following:

(i) (Compactness) There exists a sequence
\{{\bf y}_{k}\}\subset\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
such that
\rho_{n_{k}}(\cdot+{\bf y}_{k})
is tight, i.e., for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
there exists
R>0
such that

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}_{k}|<R}\rho_{n_{k}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\mu-\varepsilon.

(ii) (Vanishing) For each
R>0
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left[\sup_{{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R}\rho_{n_{k}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right]=0.

(iii) (Dichotomy) There exists
\alpha\in(0,\mu)
such that for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
there exist
k_{0}\geq 1
and
\{\rho_{k}^{1}\},\{\rho_{k}^{2}\}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
such that
{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\rho^{1}_{k}}\cap{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\rho^{2}_{k}}=\emptyset
and
0\leq\rho^{1}_{k},\rho^{2}_{k}\leq\rho_{n_{k}}
for
k\geq k_{0}
, together with

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\left\{\mbox{ }\|\mbox{ }\rho_{n_{k}}-(\rho^{1}_{k}+\rho^{2}_{k})\mbox{ }\|_{1}+\big|\mbox{ }\|\rho^{1}_{k}\|_{1}-\alpha\mbox{ }\big|+\big|\mbox{ }\|\rho^{2}_{k}\|_{1}-(\mu-\alpha)\mbox{ }\big|\mbox{ }\right\}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ and}

\mathrm{dist}\,({\mathrm{supp}}\,{\rho^{1}_{k}},{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\rho^{2}_{k}})\to\infty\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }k\to\infty.

3.
Stability of Sadovskii vortex patches

This section is devoted to establishing Theorem
1.3
, our first main result, which concerns the stability of a nonempty collection of Sadovskii vortex patches. We also rigorously justify the perimeter growth near this collection, as mentioned in Remark
1.5
.

3.1.
Technical statements for first result: Theorem
1.3

We review the variational setting given in
[
47
, Theorem 1.2]
and we specify the collection
\mathscr{A}
in Theorem
1.3
. First, we define the admissible class
K\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as

K\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\Bigl\{\mbox{ }\omega\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\mbox{ }\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{A_{-}}\mbox{ }\mbox{a.e. in}\mbox{ }\mathbb{R}^{2}\quad\mbox{for some bounded open set }\mbox{ }A\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\mbox{ }\mbox{ satisfying }\int_{A}x_{2}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=1\mbox{ }\Bigl\}

where the set
A_{-}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
denotes the
mirror image
of
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
. We also define the kinetic energy functional
E(\cdot)
as

(3.1)

E(\omega)\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|{\bf u}|^{2}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\quad\mbox{ for the velocity }\mbox{ }{\bf u}=\nabla^{\perp}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}})^{-1}\omega.

We formulate the maximization problem by

I\mbox{ }:=\sup_{\omega\in K}E(\omega)

and define the set of maximizers as

(3.2)

S:=\big\{\mbox{ }\omega\in K:\,E(\omega)=I\mbox{ }\big\}.

Remark 3.1
.

By
[
47
, Theorem 1.2]
, the maximal energy
I
is finite, and the maximizer set
S
is nonempty, while every maximizer
\omega\in S
is a Sadovskii vortex patch up to
x_{1}
-translation. Moreover, the traveling speed
W>0
is unique in
S
with the relation

(3.3)

W=\frac{4}{3}I,

and there exists a uniform constant
C>1
such that every maximizer
\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{A_{-}}\in S
satisfies

\frac{1}{C}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }|A|\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C

where
|\cdot|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Remark 3.2
.

[
47
, Remark 1.6]
