says that, for the Sadovskii vortex patches in
S
, we have a uniform lower bound for the touching radius
R>0
in Definition
1.1
-(iii). Indeed, it is due to the center speed estimate (see (
1.6
) and Figure
2
):

v_{1}(0,0)>2W

and the fact that the continuity of the velocity
{\bf v}=(v_{1},v_{2})
is uniform under the choice of vortices in
S
.

We restrict our interest only to the maximizers having the minimal
L^{1}
mass.

Definition 3.3
.

We define the collection of measurable sets as

\mathscr{A}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\left\{\,A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\mbox{ }\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}-\mathbf{1}_{A_{-}}\in S,\mbox{ }\int_{A}x_{1}\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0,\quad|A|=\inf_{\eta\in S}\|\eta\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\,\right\}

where
|\cdot|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In other words,
\mathscr{A}
is the collection of the core sets of Sadovskii vortex patches in
S
that are centered in the
x_{1}
-direction and attain the smallest
L^{1}
-norm in
S
.

By the way of defining
\mathscr{A}
from
S
, we note that every element of
\mathscr{A}
is the core set of a Sadovskii vortex patch (since they are centralized in
x_{1}
variable). We now establish its stability theorem:

Theorem 3.4

(Stability of
\mathscr{A}
)
.

The collection
\mathscr{A}
is nonempty. Moreover, it is stable in the following sense:

For each
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
such that, for any odd-symmetric initial data
\theta_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
which is nonnegative in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
and satisfies

\inf_{A\in\mathscr{A}}\left\{\|\theta_{0}-\mathbf{1}_{A}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|\theta_{0}-\mathbf{1}_{A}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0}-\mathbf{1}_{A})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}<\delta,

there exists a shift function
\tau:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\tau(0)=0
, for which the corresponding solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.1
) satisfies

(3.4)

\inf_{A\in\mathscr{A}}\left\{\big\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\mathbf{1}_{A^{\tau(t)}}\big\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\big\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\mathbf{1}_{A^{\tau(t)}}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\big\|x_{2}\big(\theta(t,\cdot)-\mathbf{1}_{A^{\tau(t)}}\big)\big\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0,

where the shift operator is given as

\mbox{ }A^{\tau}:=\big\{\,{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\,{\bf x}-(\tau,0)\in A\,\big\}\mbox{ }
for each
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, assuming further that the support of
\theta_{0}
is bounded, it holds that

(3.5)

\Big|\mbox{ }\tau(t)-Wt\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon(t+1)\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0

for some constant

C=C\left(\mbox{ }\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})},\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\theta_{0}}|x_{1}|\mbox{ }\right)>0.

Theorem
3.4
above follows directly from Theorem
3.19
, which establishes the stability of the set
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
that will be defined in Definition
3.17
. It is based on the fact that
\mathscr{A}
is an alternative form of
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
for small
\mu>0
(see Remark
3.18
for details).

Remark 3.5
.

The shift estimate (
3.5
) can be considered optimal in the sense that the power exponent of
\varepsilon
on the right-hand side of (
3.5
) matches that in (
3.4
). Indeed, we may fix any
A\in\mathscr{A}

and, for each
\varepsilon>0
, consider the solution
\theta_{\varepsilon}(t)
corresponding to the perturbed initial data
\theta_{\varepsilon}(0)=(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{1}_{A}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
(i.e., a traveling vortex of non-unit strength). Since the traveling speed of
\theta_{\varepsilon}(t)
is exactly
(1+\varepsilon)W
and the stability estimate (
3.4
) is of order
\varepsilon^{1}
, the shift
\tau(t)
should be close to
\varepsilon^{1}Wt
.

In Theorem
3.4
above, a perturbed initial data
\theta_{0}
can be taken to be in a characteristic function form
\theta_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{0}}
with the smooth set-boundary
\partial\Omega_{0}\in C^{\infty}
. Moreover, one can find a perturbed set
\Omega_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
with
\partial\Omega_{0}\in C^{\infty}
which experiences
linear-in-time
growth of perimeter. Corollary
3.6
below describes such a scenario.

Corollary 3.6

(Perimeter growth near
\mathscr{A}
)
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a vortex patch solution
\omega:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
to the Euler equations (
1.1
) in the odd- symmetric form

\omega\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mathbf{1}_{\Omega(t)}-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega(t)_{-}}\quad\mbox{ }\mbox{ where }\quad\Omega(t)\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Omega(t)_{-}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mbox{ }(x_{1},-x_{2})\in\Omega(t)\},

satisfying the following properties:

(i) Simply-connectedness:
The set
\Omega(t)\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
is open, bounded, simply-connected, and satisfies
\mbox{ }\partial\Omega(t)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
.

(ii) All-time proximity to Sadovskii vortex patches:
It holds that

\inf_{A\in\mathscr{A},\,\tau\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Omega(t)\triangle A}(x_{2}+1)\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ for any}\mbox{ }t\geq 0.

(iii) Linear-in-time growth of perimeter:
We have
\left(\,\mbox{Perimeter of }\mbox{ }\partial\Omega(0)\,\right)<\infty
, while

\left(\,\mbox{Perimeter of }\mbox{ }\partial\Omega(t)\,\right)>Ct\quad\mbox{ for any }\mbox{ }t\geq 0

for some uniform constant
C>0
.

The proof of Corollary
3.6
above follows directly from the perimeter growth result near
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
(Lemma
3.27
) and the stability of
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
(Theorem
3.19
), both of which are assuming the smallness of
\mu>0
. It is also based on Remark
3.18
, which says that
\mathscr{A}
is an alternative form of
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
for small
\mu>0
.

Remark 3.7
.

In Corollary
3.6
above, the initial perturbed set
\Omega(0)\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
can be replaced with a set
\widetilde{\Omega(0)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
that touches the
x_{1}
axis as the Sadovskii vortex patch did, while having a smooth boundary in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the sense that

\partial\Bigg(\,\overline{\widetilde{\Omega(t)}\cup\widetilde{\Omega(t)}_{-}}\,\Bigg)\in C^{\infty}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\mbox{for any }t\geq 0,

as demonstrated in Figure
1
(b). For details, we refer to Appendix
A
for the construction of a set
\Omega(0)\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where one can easily modify the method there to find a way of replacing
\Omega(0)
with a touching set
\widetilde{\Omega(0)}
.

3.2.
Background

We now recall some results about
Sadovskii vortex patches
established in
[
47
]
based on a variational method, which will be frequently used later. Indeed, in
[
47
]
, the maximization problem for the admissible class
K
is solved by first considering the class
K
under an
L^{1}
bound, and later removing that constraint (see Remark
3.9
for that). Hence, we now recall the variational setting established in
[
47
, Section 3]
where the boundedness of
K
in
L^{1}
is imposed, and establish several results which will be used in the sequel.

Given
\mu,\nu,\lambda>0,
we define an admissible class

\displaystyle K_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\Bigl\{\mbox{ }\omega\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}):\mbox{ }\omega=\lambda\cdot\mathbf{1}_{A}

a.e.

\displaystyle\mbox{where }A\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\mbox{ is (Lebesgue) measurable, }

\displaystyle\|x_{2}\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}=\mu,\quad\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\leq\nu\mbox{ }\Bigl\},

on which the kinetic energy functional
E(\cdot)
of (
3.1
) takes a form of

E(\omega)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf y})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\textrm{d}{\bf x}

with the Green’s function
G
of the half plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
given by

G({\bf x},{\bf y}):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\quad\mbox{ for }\mbox{ }{\bf x}\neq{\bf y}.

We formulate the maximization problem by

I_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}\mbox{ }:=\sup_{\omega\in K_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}}E(\omega)

and define the set of maximizers as

S_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}:=\left\{\mbox{ }\omega\in K_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}:\mbox{ }E(\omega)=I_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}\mbox{ }\right\}.
We recall
[
47
, Remark 3.1-(v)]
to reduce the maximization problem to the case where
\nu=\lambda=1
by some scaling. We put

K_{\mu}:=K_{\mu,1,1},\quad I_{\mu}:=I_{\mu,1,1},\quad S_{\mu}:=S_{\mu,1,1}.

Remark 3.8
.

In
[
47
]
, it was shown that there exists a uniform constant
\mu_{0}>0
such that, for each
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, every maximizer in
S_{\mu}
is a Sadovskii vortex patch up to
x_{1}
-translation
(see Lemma
3.10
-(iii) and Lemma
3.14
-(i) for details).

Remark 3.9
.

Indeed, the set
S
previously defined in (
3.2
) is related to
S_{\mu}
in the following way. For each
\mu>0
, we define the admissible set
K_{\mu,\infty}
by

\displaystyle K_{\mu,\infty}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\left\{\mbox{ }\omega\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}):\mbox{ }\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}\quad\mbox{a.e.}\quad\mbox{where }\mbox{ }A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }\mbox{ is (Lebesgue) measurable, }\quad\|x_{2}\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}=\mu\mbox{ }\right\}

\displaystyle\mbox{with}\quad I_{\mu,\infty}:=\sup_{\omega\in K_{\mu,\infty}}E(\omega)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad S_{\mu,\infty}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\left\{\mbox{ }\omega\in K_{\mu,\infty}:\mbox{ }E(\omega)=I_{\mu,\infty}\mbox{ }\right\}.

We note that the set
S_{1,\infty}
is equated with
S
(by the odd extension of each element in
S_{1,\infty}
). Moreover, one can easily find in
[
47
, Subsection 5.3]
that

S_{1,\infty}=S_{1,\mbox{ }\mu^{-2/3},1}\quad\mbox{ for small }\,\mu>0.

From the bijection

S_{1,\mbox{ }\mu^{-2/3},1}\simeq S_{\mu}
under the scaling map
\omega\mapsto\omega(\mu^{-1/3}(\cdot))
, we obtain the scaling relation

(3.6)

S_{1,\infty}\,\simeq\,S_{\mu}\quad\mbox{ for small }\mbox{ }\mu>0,

which implies that
S
is an alternative form of
S_{\mu}
given that
\mu>0
is small enough.

We first refer to one of the fundamental results concerning the above variational problem: for any
\mu>0
, each maximizer in
S_{\mu}
generates a traveling wave solution.

Lemma 3.10
.

[
47
, Theorem 3.3]

For any
\mu>0
, we have the following properties.

(i) The set
S_{\mu}
is nonempty.

(ii) For each
\omega\in S_{\mu}
, there exist some constants
W>0,\mbox{ }\gamma\geq 0
that are uniquely determined by
\omega
satisfying

\omega\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mathbf{1}_{\left\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})-Wx_{2}-\gamma>0\right\}}\quad\mbox{a.e.}\quad\mbox{in }\mbox{ }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}.

(iii) Each
\omega\in S_{\mu}
has a bounded support and satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition in Definition
1.2
up to
x_{1}
-translation.

It is also shown that each element in
S_{\mu}
is a maximizer in a larger admissible set than
K_{\mu}
. Indeed, for the larger admissible set
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}\supset K_{\mu}
defined by

\widetilde{K}_{\mu}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\Bigl\{\mbox{ }\omega\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}):\mbox{ }0\leq\omega\leq 1\mbox{ }\mbox{ a.e. }\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},\quad\|x_{2}\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\leq\mu,\quad\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\leq 1\mbox{ }\Bigl\}

together with

\widetilde{I}_{\mu}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\sup_{\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}}E(\omega)\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }I_{\mu}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\widetilde{S}_{\mu}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\left\{\mbox{ }\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}:\mbox{ }E(\omega)=\widetilde{I}_{\mu}\mbox{ }\right\},

we have the following lemma:

Lemma 3.11
.

[
47
, Lemma 3.10]

For each
\mu>0
, we have

\widetilde{S}_{\mu}=S_{\mu}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\widetilde{I}_{\mu}=I_{\mu}.

Lemma
3.11
above says that maximizers of the kinetic energy in the set of bounded functions
\omega:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to[0,1]
turn out to be characteristic functions
\omega:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\{0,1\}
attaining the full impulse
\|x_{2}\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}=\mu
.
This fact is one of the most important tools for the proof of the stability of maximizer sets.
Indeed, a useful result from Lemma
3.11
is the monotonicity of the maximial energy
I_{\mu}
:

Lemma 3.12
.

The maximal energy
I_{\mu}
increases strictly in
\mu>0
.

Proof.

For any
\mu>0
and
\tau>1
, we choose any
\omega\in K_{\mu}
and consider the scaling

\omega_{\tau}({\bf x}):=\tau^{-2}\omega(\tau^{-1}{\bf x}).

Then we note that
\mbox{ }E(\omega_{\tau})=E(\omega),\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\|x_{2}\omega_{\tau}\|_{1}=\tau\mu,\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\|\omega_{\tau}\|_{1}=\|\omega\|_{1}\leq 1,\mbox{ }
and
\mbox{ }0\leq\omega_{\tau}\leq\tau^{-2}<1.\mbox{ }
Therefore we get
\omega_{\tau}\in\widetilde{K}_{\tau\mu}
and the relation

E(\omega)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }I_{\tau\mu}.

As the choice of
\omega\in K_{\mu}
is arbitrary, we obtain

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }I_{\tau\mu}.

It remains to be shown that the equality never holds. For any
\eta\in S_{\mu}
, it holds
\mbox{ }\|x_{2}\eta\|_{1}=\mu\mbox{ }
and
\mbox{ }\eta\in\widetilde{K}_{\tau\mu}
. If
I_{\mu}=I_{\tau\mu}
, then we have

E(\eta)=I_{\tau\mu}=\widetilde{I}_{\tau\mu},

which implies
\eta\in\widetilde{S}_{\tau\mu}=S_{\tau\mu}
. It means
\|x_{2}\eta\|_{1}=\tau\mu
, which contradicts to the assumption
\tau>1
. Therefore we have

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }I_{\tau\mu}.

∎

Indeed, the set
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
is a proper admissible class to deal with weak limits of energy-maximizing sequences. We establish the following lemma:

Lemma 3.13
.

For any
\mu\in(0,\infty)
, suppose a sequence
\{\eta_{n}\}\subset L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
and a function
\eta\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
satisfy

\eta_{n}\geq 0,\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\leq 1,\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\eta_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\leq\mu,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|(\eta_{n}-1)_{+}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}=0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\eta_{n}\rightharpoonup\eta\quad\mbox{in }\mbox{ }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}).

Then we have
\eta\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
.

Proof.

Let
\mu>0
. Suppose a sequence
\{\eta_{n}\}\subset L^{1}\cap L^{2}
and a function
\eta\in L^{2}
satisfies every assumption in the statement.

(Claim 1)
We have
0\leq\eta\leq 1
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
.

For any bounded set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, we observe that

\int_{U}\eta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{U}\eta_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\geq 0

and

\int_{U}(\eta-1)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{U}(\eta_{n}-1)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\leq\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\mbox{ }\int_{U}(\eta_{n}-1)_{+}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0.

It completes the proof of the claim.

(Claim 2)
We have
\|\eta\|_{1}\leq 1
and
\|x_{2}\eta\|_{1}\leq\mu
.

Suppose
\|\eta\|_{1}>1
. We choose large
R>0
such that

\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}\eta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }1.

Then observe that

1\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}\left(\eta-\eta_{n}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}\eta_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,

which is a contradiction. Similarly, one can easily prove that
\|x_{2}\eta\|_{1}\leq\mu.
This completes the proof.

∎

Under the assumption that
\mu>0
is small enough, we can obtain various properties of the maximizers in
S_{\mu}
that will be used in a variety of ways in this paper. The most important fact is that, for small
\mu>0
, each maximizer in
S_{\mu}
is a
Sadovskii vortex patch
. Moreover, the set
S_{\mu}
enjoys some uniform estimates with respect to small
\mu>0
.

Lemma 3.14
.

There exists a uniform constant
\mu_{0}>0
such that, for each
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, the following properties hold:

(i) For each
\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}\in S_{\mu}
satisfying
\int x_{1}\omega\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0,\mbox{ }
there exist
R>0
and
l:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
l\in C([0,\infty))
and

\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\mbox{ }|{\bf x}|<R\mbox{ }\}\mbox{ }\subset\mbox{ }A\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\mbox{ }x_{1}<l(x_{2})\mbox{ }\}.

Moreover, the radius of the touching set
R>0
has a lower bound depending only on
\mu>0
.

(ii)
[
47
, Lemma 4.6]
For some uniform constant
C_{1}>1
, we have

(3.7)

\frac{1}{C_{1}}\cdot\mu^{2/3}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{1}\cdot\mu^{2/3}\quad\mbox{ for each }\omega\in S_{\mu}.

(iii)
[
47
, Lemma 4.7]
There exists some uniform constant
C_{2}>0
such that, for each
\omega\in S_{\mu}
satisfying
\int x_{1}\omega\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0,\mbox{ }
we have

(3.8)

\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\omega}|{\bf x}|\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{2}\cdot\mu^{1/3}.

(iv) The traveling speed
W_{\mu}
of
S_{\mu}
is uniquely determined by the relation

(3.9)

I_{\mu}=\frac{3}{4}W_{\mu}\cdot\mu.

(v) There exist some uniform constants
W,\mbox{ }I>0
such that, for each
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, we have the relations

(3.10)

W_{\mu}=W\cdot\mu^{1/3}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad I_{\mu}=I\cdot\mu^{4/3}.

Proof.

For completeness, we give the proofs of (i), (iv), and (v). The proof of (i) follows from Theorem 4.1 and Theorem 4.2 in
[
47
]
.

[
47
, Theorem 4.1]
asserts that, for small enough
\mu>0
, each maximizer
\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}\in S_{\mu}
that satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition must touch the axis in the sense that

\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\mbox{ }|{\bf x}|<R\mbox{ }\}\mbox{ }\subset\mbox{ }A.

[
47
, Theorem 4.1]
implies that the Steiner symmetry condition of each maximizer
\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}
makes the set
A
characterized by a curve
l\in C([0,\infty))
under the relation

A=\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:\mbox{ }x_{1}<l(x_{2})\mbox{ }\}.

For the uniform lower bound of
R>0
, we refer to
[
47
, Remark 1.6]
.

We now prove (iv). Note that
[
47
, Lemma 3.21]
gives us a Pohožaev-type identity

I_{\mu}=\frac{3}{4}W\mu+\frac{1}{2}\gamma\|\omega\|_{1}

which holds for each
\omega\in S_{\mu}
and for the constants
W=W(\omega)>0
,
\gamma=\gamma(\omega)\geq 0
given in Lemma
3.10
.
From
[
47
, Lemma 4.4]
we obtain that
\gamma=0
by assuming
\mu>0
is small enough.

The proof of (v) is induced from Remark
3.9
. Indeed, the scaling relation (
3.6
):

S_{1,\infty}\,\simeq\,S_{\mu}\quad\mbox{ for small }\,\mu>0

given in Remark
3.9
implies the relation

S_{\mu,\infty}=S_{\mu}\quad\mbox{ for small }\,\mu>0

(due to the exact impulse condition
\int x_{2}\omega=\mu
both in
S_{\mu,\infty}
and
S_{\mu}
).
Hence we have
I_{\mu}=I_{\mu,\infty}
. By the scaling map, we get

I_{\mu}=I_{\mu,\infty}=I_{1,\infty}\cdot\mu^{4/3},

and thus

W_{\mu}=\frac{4}{3\mu}I_{\mu}=\frac{4}{3}I_{1,\infty}\cdot\mu^{1/3}

(see
[
47
, Remark 5.3]
).

∎

3.3.
Compactness of maximizing sequences

We next apply Lions’ concentration-compactness lemma (Lemma
2.4
) to obtain a localization property of maximizing sequences, up to translations. Roughly speaking, any maximizing sequence of the kinetic energy is localized up to translations. We recall from Section
3.2
that
I_{\mu}
in Proposition
3.15
below denotes the maximal kinetic energy in the admissible class
K_{\mu}
.

Proposition 3.15
.

For any
\mu\in(0,\infty)
, suppose a sequence
\{\eta_{n}\}\subset(L^{1}\cap L^{2})(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
satisfies
\mbox{ }\eta_{n}\geq 0\mbox{ }
,
E(\eta_{n})\to I_{\mu}
, and
\|x_{2}\eta_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\to\mu
as
n\to\infty
, together with

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\leq 1,\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}<\infty,\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\|(\eta_{n}-1)_{+}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}=0.

Then there exist a subsequence
\{\eta_{n_{k}}\}
and a sequence
\{y_{k}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that, for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
there exists
R>0
such that

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}x_{2}\eta_{n_{k}}\left({\bf x}+(y_{k},0)\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\mu-\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
\mu>0
. Choose any sequence
\{\eta_{n}\}\subset(L^{1}\cap L^{2})(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
satisfying
\eta_{n}\geq 0\mbox{ }
for
n\geq 1
,
E(\eta_{n})\to I_{\mu}
and
\|x_{2}\eta_{n}\|_{1}\to\mu
as
n\to\infty
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{1}\leq 1,\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{2}=:C_{0}<\infty,\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\|(\eta_{n}-1)_{+}\|_{1}=0

for some constant
C_{0}>0
.
We now apply Lemma
2.4
for
\rho_{n}:=x_{2}\eta_{n}
to choose a subsequence of
{\eta_{n}}
(still denoted by
\{\eta_{n}\}
) satisfying one of (i)
Compactness
, (ii)
Vanishing
, and (iii)
Dichotomy
holds. Assuming that (i) is true, we have two cases for the sequence
\{{\bf y}_{n}=(y^{1}_{n},y^{2}_{n})\}\subset\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
:

\limsup_{n\to\infty}y^{2}_{n}=\infty\quad\mbox{ or }\quad\sup_{n\geq 1}y^{2}_{n}<\infty.

Suppose
\limsup_{n\to\infty}y^{2}_{n}=\infty
,
i.e.
, there exists a subsequence of
\{y_{n}^{2}\}
(still denoted by
\{y_{n}^{2}\}
) which tends to
\infty
as
n\to\infty
. Then we will see that
E(\eta_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, which leads to a contradiction. Let
\varepsilon>0
. From (i), we choose
R>0
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\eta_{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{n}^{c})}\leq\varepsilon

where
B_{n}:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }|{\bf x}-{\bf y}_{n}|<R\}
. Then we have

2E(\eta_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{G}[\eta_{n}]\cdot\eta_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=\int_{B_{n}}+\int_{B_{n}^{c}}

where, by the estimates (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) in Proposition
2.2
for the cases
p=2
and
\theta=1/2
, we get

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{B_{n}^{c}}\mathcal{G}[\eta_{n}]\cdot\eta_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{1}\|\eta_{n}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|x_{2}\eta_{n}\|_{L^{1}(B_{n}^{c})}^{1/2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{0}^{1/2}\varepsilon^{1/2}

and

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{B_{n}}\mathcal{G}[\eta_{n}]\cdot\eta_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{1}^{1/2}\|\eta_{n}\|_{2}^{1/2}\int_{B_{n}}x_{2}^{1/2}\eta_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{0}^{1/2}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{B_{n}}\frac{x_{2}}{(y_{n}^{2}-R)^{1/2}}\eta_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0

due to
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\eta_{n}\|_{L^{1}(B)}\leq\mu
. Let
\varepsilon\searrow 0
and we obtain that
E(\eta_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, which contradicts to
I_{\mu}>0
.
Therefore, it holds that

\sup_{n\geq 1}y^{2}_{n}<\infty,

and we can rewrite the statement of (i) in Lemma
2.4
by putting
y_{n}^{2}=0
and
y_{n}:=y_{n}^{1}
as follows:
there exists a sequence
\{y_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that, for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
R>0
satisfying

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}x_{2}\eta_{n}\left({\bf x}+(y_{n},0)\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\mu-\varepsilon.

It gives the conclusion in the statement of Proposition
3.15
. Therefore, it remains to show that the cases (ii)
Vanishing
and (iii)
Dichotomy
cannot occur.

(Claim 1)
For the sequence
\rho_{n}=x_{2}\eta_{n}
, the case (ii)
Vanishing
never holds.

Let
R>0
. We suppose the case (ii)
Vanishing
holds. Then we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[\sup_{{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R}x_{2}\eta_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right]=0.

We will derive a contradiction by showing that
E(\eta_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. We split the double integral as

2E(\eta_{n}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\eta_{n}({\bf x})\eta_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}=\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R}+\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq R}.

First, we obtain that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq R}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }R^{-2}\cdot\limsup_{n\to\infty}\iint x_{2}y_{2}\eta_{n}({\bf x})\eta_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }R^{-2}\mu^{2}\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }R\to\infty

by the relation

G({\bf x},{\bf y})\leq\frac{x_{2}y_{2}}{\pi|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}.

For the case
|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R
, we split the integral once again such that

\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R}=\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\mbox{ }G({\bf x},{\bf y})\geq Rx_{2}y_{2}}+\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\mbox{ }G({\bf x},{\bf y})<Rx_{2}y_{2}}

where

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\mbox{ }G({\bf x},{\bf y})<Rx_{2}y_{2}}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }R\cdot\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}y_{2}\eta_{n}({\bf y})\left[\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R}x_{2}\eta_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right]\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }R\cdot\mu\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[\sup_{{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R}x_{2}\eta_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right]=0.

Lastly,

\displaystyle\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\mbox{ }G({\bf x},{\bf y})\geq Rx_{2}y_{2}}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R^{-1/2}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\eta_{n}({\bf x})\eta_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\|\eta_{n}\|_{1}^{1/2}\|\eta_{n}\|_{2}^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\eta_{n}({\bf y})\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\cap\{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R^{-1/2}\}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{4}\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)^{1/4}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

due to the Hölder inequality and the relation

G({\bf x},{\bf y})\geq Rx_{2}y_{2}\mbox{ }\Rightarrow\mbox{ }R\leq\frac{1}{x_{2}y_{2}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\leq\frac{1}{\pi|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}.

From (
2.2
) of Lemma
2.1
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\cap\{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R^{-1/2}\}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{4}\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }R^{-1/2}y_{2}

and so

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\mbox{ }G({\bf x},{\bf y})\geq Rx_{2}y_{2}}\leq\mbox{ }R^{-1/8}\cdot\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{1}^{5/4}\|\eta_{n}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|x_{2}\eta_{n}\|^{1/4}_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{0}^{1/2}R^{-1/8}\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }R\to\infty.

This completes the proof of the claim.

(Claim 2)
For the sequence
\rho_{n}=x_{2}\eta_{n}
, the case (iii)
Dichotomy
never holds.

We suppose that the case (iii)
Dichotomy
holds. Let
\alpha\in(0,\mu)
be the constant satisfying that, for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
there exist
k_{0}\geq 1
and
\{x_{2}\eta_{n,1}\},\{x_{2}\eta_{n,2}\}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
such that
{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\eta_{n,1}}\cap{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\eta_{n,2}}=\emptyset
and
0\leq\eta_{n,1},\eta_{n,2}\leq\eta_{n}
for
n\geq k_{0}
, and that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\{\mbox{ }\|\mbox{ }x_{2}\eta_{n}-(x_{2}\eta_{n,1}+x_{2}\eta_{n,2})\mbox{ }\|_{1}+\big|\mbox{ }\|x_{2}\eta_{n,1}\|_{1}-\alpha\mbox{ }\big|+\big|\mbox{ }\|x_{2}\eta_{n,2}\|_{1}-(\mu-\alpha)\mbox{ }\big|\mbox{ }\right\}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon,

d_{n}:=\mathrm{dist}\,({\mathrm{supp}}\,{\eta_{n,1}},{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\eta_{n,2}})\to\infty\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }n\to\infty.

First, we put
\eta_{n,3}:=\eta_{n}-(\eta_{n,1}+\eta_{n,2})
to have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n,3}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }3\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}<\infty\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\eta_{n,3}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon.

Then we have

\displaystyle E(\eta_{n})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E(\eta_{n,1})\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }E(\eta_{n,2})\mbox{ }+\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\eta_{n,1}({\bf x})\eta_{n,2}({\bf y})\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }E(\eta_{n,3})\mbox{ }+\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\left(\eta_{n,1}({\bf x})+\eta_{n,2}({\bf x})\right)\eta_{n,3}({\bf y})\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}.

Among the five terms on the right-hand side, the last two terms satisfy

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Bigg|\mbox{ }E(\eta_{n,3})+\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\left(\eta_{n,1}({\bf x})+\eta_{n,2}({\bf x})\right)\cdot\eta_{n,3}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\Bigg|\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\varepsilon^{1/2}

by the estimate (
2.5
) in Proposition
2.2
for the cases
p=2
and
\theta=1/2
, and the third term satisfies

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left|\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\eta_{n,1}({\bf x})\eta_{n,2}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right|\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}d_{n}^{-2}\|x_{2}\eta_{n}\|_{1}\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }n\to\infty

by the relation

G({\bf x},{\bf y})\leq\frac{x_{2}y_{2}}{\pi|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}.

Thus, for some uniform constant
C>0
, we have

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(E(\eta_{n,1})+E(\eta_{n,2})\right)+C\varepsilon^{1/2}.

For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, based on Lemma
2.3
we denote the Steiner symmetrization of
\eta_{n,i}
as
\eta^{*}_{n,i}
which satisfies the relation

E(\eta_{n,i})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E(\eta^{*}_{n,i}).

while preserving each norm

\|\eta^{*}_{n,i}\|_{1}=\|\eta_{n,i}\|_{1},\quad\|\eta^{*}_{n,i}\|_{2}=\|\eta_{n,i}\|_{2},\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\|x_{2}\eta^{*}_{n,i}\|_{1}=\|x_{2}\eta_{n,i}\|_{1},

together with

\|(\eta^{*}_{n,i}-1)_{+}\|_{1}=\|(\eta_{n,i}-1)_{+}\|_{1}.

Therefore we have

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(E(\eta^{*}_{n,1})+E(\eta^{*}_{n,2})\right)+C\varepsilon^{1/2}

with the conditions
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\eta^{*}_{n,1}\|_{1}\leq\alpha+\varepsilon,\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\eta^{*}_{n,1}\|_{2}\leq\mu-\alpha+\varepsilon\mbox{ }
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Big[\|\eta^{*}_{n,1}\|_{1}+\|\eta^{*}_{n,2}\|_{1}\Big]\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Big[\|\eta_{n,1}\|_{1}+\|\eta_{n,2}\|_{1}\Big]\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,

and

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta^{*}_{n,i}\|_{2}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{2}<\infty,\quad\|(\eta^{*}_{n,i}-1)_{+}\|_{1}\leq\|(\eta_{n}-1)_{+}\|_{1}\to 0\quad\mbox{ for each }\mbox{ }i\in\{1,2\}.

Note that, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, we obtain a function
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{i}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
such that a subsequence of
\{\eta^{*}_{n,i}\}
(still denoted by
\{\eta^{*}_{n,i}\}
) converges weakly to
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{i}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
. Then by Lemma
3.13
, we have

\eta^{\varepsilon}_{1}\in\widetilde{K}_{\alpha+\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\eta^{\varepsilon}_{2}\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu-\alpha+\varepsilon}.

Moreover, borrowing Lemma
3.16
below, we obtain

\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\eta^{*}_{n,i})=E(\eta^{\varepsilon}_{i})\quad\mbox{ for each }\mbox{ }i\in\{1,2\}.

Lemma 3.16
.

[
1
, Lemma 3.5]
Let
\{\omega_{n}\}
be a sequence such that

\sup_{n\geq 1}\left\{\|\omega_{n}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}\omega_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}<\infty,

\omega_{n}\rightharpoonup\omega\quad\mbox{ in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\quad\mbox{ as }n\to\infty.

Assume that each
\omega_{n}
satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition. Then,

E(\omega_{n})\to E(\omega)\mbox{ }\mbox{ as }n\to\infty.

In sum, we have

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E(\eta^{\varepsilon}_{1})+E(\eta^{\varepsilon}_{2})+C\varepsilon^{1/2}

with

\|\eta^{\varepsilon}_{1}\|_{1}+\|\eta^{\varepsilon}_{2}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Big[\|\eta^{*}_{n,1}\|_{1}+\|\eta^{*}_{n,2}\|_{1}\Big]\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1.

For each
\varepsilon>0
, we see that

\|\eta^{\varepsilon}_{i}\|_{2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\eta^{*}_{n,i}\|_{2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta^{*}_{n,i}\|_{2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n}\|_{2}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }C_{0},

which means that
\|\eta^{\varepsilon}_{i}\|_{2}
is bounded by a constant which does not depend on
\varepsilon>0
.
Then there exist two weak subsequential limits
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
such that
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{i}\rightharpoonup\eta_{i}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in
L^{2}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. By Lemma
3.13
and Lemma
3.16
again, we obtain

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E(\eta_{1})+E(\eta_{2})

with the conditions

\|\eta_{1}\|_{1}+\|\eta_{2}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,\quad\|x_{2}\eta_{1}\|_{1}\leq\alpha,\quad\|x_{2}\eta_{2}\|_{1}\leq\mu-\alpha,

and

\eta_{1}\in\widetilde{K}_{\alpha},\quad\eta_{2}\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu-\alpha}.

It is impossible to have
\eta_{1}\equiv\eta_{2}\equiv 0
due to
I_{\mu}>0
. We assume
\|\eta_{1}\|_{1}>0
. By
\|\eta_{1}\|_{1}\leq 1-\|\eta_{2}\|_{1},
we replace
\eta_{1}
with any maximizer
\omega_{1}\in\widetilde{S}_{\alpha,1-\|\eta_{2}\|_{1},1}
to obtain

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E(\omega_{1})+E(\eta_{2})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }I_{\alpha}+E(\eta_{2})

with the conditions

\|\omega_{1}\|_{1}+\|\eta_{2}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,\quad\|x_{2}\omega_{1}\|_{1}=\alpha,\quad\|x_{2}\eta_{2}\|_{1}\leq\mu-\alpha.

If
\eta_{2}\equiv 0
, then we have
I_{\mu}\leq I_{\alpha}
which contradicts to the monotonicity of
I_{(\cdot)}
(see Lemma
3.12
). So we get
\|\eta_{2}\|_{1}>0
. By
\|\eta_{2}\|_{1}\leq 1-\|\omega_{1}\|_{1}
, we replace
\eta_{2}
with any maximizer
\omega_{2}\in\widetilde{S}_{\mu-\alpha,1-\|\omega_{1}\|_{1},1}
to obtain

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E(\omega_{1})+E(\omega_{2})

with the conditions

\|\omega_{1}\|_{1}+\|\omega_{2}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,\quad\|x_{2}\omega_{1}\|_{1}=\alpha,\quad\|x_{2}\omega_{2}\|_{1}=\mu-\alpha.

We now assume that
{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\omega_{1}}\cap{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\omega_{2}}=\emptyset
by some suitable
x_{1}
-translation (recalling the fact that each maximizer
\omega_{i}
has a compact support). Then our previous conditions on
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
imply

0\leq\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\leq 1,\quad\|\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\|_{1}\leq 1,\quad\mbox{and }\quad\|x_{2}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\|_{1}=\mu

which gives
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
. Then we get
\mbox{ }E(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\leq\widetilde{I}_{\mu}\mbox{ }
. Then by the relation
\widetilde{I}_{\mu}=I_{\mu}
in Lemma
3.11
, we get

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E(\omega_{1})+E(\omega_{2})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{1}({\bf x})\omega_{2}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }I_{\mu}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{1}({\bf x})\omega_{2}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y},

which gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{1}({\bf x})\omega_{2}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}=0.

It implies that
\omega_{1}\equiv 0
or
\omega_{2}\equiv 0
, which contradicts to

\|x_{2}\omega_{1}\|_{1}=\alpha,\quad\|x_{2}\omega_{2}\|_{1}=\mu-\alpha.

It completes the proof.

∎

3.4.
Stability of energy maximizers with shift estimate and application

In Section
3.2
before, we introduced an admissible class (
K_{\mu}
) having an
L^{1}
-mass bound and collected the energy maximizers (
S_{\mu}
). We now define a subset
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}\subset S_{\mu}
by imposing the minimal
L^{1}
-norm condition, similarly to the definition of
\mathscr{A}
in Definition
3.3
. Our stability result for
\mathscr{A}
(Theorem
3.4
) and its application (Corollary
3.6
) will follow from those for
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
.

Definition 3.17
.

For each
\mu>0
, we define

\mathscr{S}_{\mu}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\left\{\mbox{ }\omega\in S_{\mu}:\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}=\inf_{\eta\in S_{\mu}}\|\eta\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\omega\mbox{ }\mathrm{d}{\bf x}=0\mbox{ }\right\}

which is the set of centralized maximizers that attain the smallest
L^{1}
-norm in
S_{\mu}
. (We have either
\displaystyle\inf_{\eta\in S_{\mu}}\|\eta\|_{L^{1}}=1
or
\displaystyle\inf_{\eta\in S_{\mu}}\|\eta\|_{L^{1}}<1
where the latter is the only case when the smallest
L^{1}
-norm condition becomes meaningful.)

Remark 3.18
.

We note that each of the two sets
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
, given in Definition
3.3
and Definition
3.17
respectively, is an alternative form of the other, given that
\mu>0
is small enough. Indeed, Remark
3.9
says that there is a bijection

S\mbox{ }\simeq\mbox{ }S_{\mu}
under a certain scaling map using small
\mu>0
.
Then, by the similarity in the derivations of
\mathscr{A},\,\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
from
S,\,S_{\mu}
, the set
\mathscr{A}
is also bijectively related to
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
using small
\mu>0
as a scaling parameter.

The rest of Section
3
will be devoted to obtaining the theorem below.
Its proof will be given in Section
3.4.4
.

Theorem 3.19
.

For each
\mu\in(0,\infty)
, the set
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
is nonempty. Moreover, there exists a constant
\mu_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for each
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, there exists
\varepsilon_{\mu}\in(0,\infty)
satisfying the following properties:

For each
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
, there exists
\delta=\delta(\mu,\varepsilon)>0
such that, for any initial data
\theta_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
which is nonnegative and

\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}}\left\{\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0}-\omega)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}<\delta,

there exists a shift function
\tau:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\tau(0)=0
such that the unique global weak solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.3
) satisfies

\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}}\left\{\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega^{\tau(t)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega^{\tau(t)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega^{\tau(t)})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}<\varepsilon,\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0

where

\omega^{\tau}(\cdot)\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\omega\left(\cdot-(\tau,0)\right)\quad\mbox{ for each }\tau\in\mathbb{R}.

Moreover, assuming further that the support of
\theta_{0}
is bounded, it holds that

\Big|\mbox{ }\tau(t)-W_{\mu}t\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon(t+1)\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0

for some constant

C=C\left(\mbox{ }\mu,\mbox{ }\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})},\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\theta_{0}}|x_{1}|\mbox{ }\right)>0,

where
W_{\mu}>0
is the traveling speed of Sadovskii vortex patches in
S_{\mu}
(uniquely determined by
\mu>0
by Lemma
3.14
-(iv)).

3.4.1.
Stability of
S_{\mu}
based on weighted norm

Before we go for the subset
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}\subset S_{\mu}
, we first prove the stability of
S_{\mu}
based on the weighted norm
\|x_{2}(\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}
.

Theorem 3.20
.

For each
\mu,\varepsilon,M\in(0,\infty)
, there exists
\delta=\delta(\mu,\varepsilon,M)>0
such that, for any initial data
\theta_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
satisfying
\theta_{0}\geq 0,\mbox{ }\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}<M,\mbox{ }\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}<1+\delta,\mbox{ }\|(\theta_{0}-1)_{+}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}<\delta,\mbox{ }
and

\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\|x_{2}(\theta_{0}-\omega)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}<\delta,

its unique global weak solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.3
) satisfies

\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\|x_{2}(\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}<\varepsilon,\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0.

Proof.

We suppose the contrary,
i.e.
, there exists
\mu,\varepsilon_{0},M_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for each
n\geq 1
, there exist
t_{n}\geq 0
and the initial data
\theta_{0,n}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
satisfying

\displaystyle\theta_{0,n}\geq 0,\quad\|\theta_{0,n}\|_{2}

\displaystyle<M_{0},\quad\|\theta_{0,n}\|_{1}<1+\frac{1}{n},\quad\|(\theta_{0,n}-1)_{+}\|_{1}<\frac{1}{n},

\displaystyle\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\|x_{2}(\theta_{0,n}-\omega)\|_{1}<\frac{1}{n},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\|x_{2}(\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\omega)\|_{1}\geq\varepsilon_{0}

where
\theta_{n}
denotes the solution of the Euler equations (
1.3
) corresponding to each initial data
\theta_{0,n}
.
Hereafter, we denote
\overline{\theta}_{n}:=\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)
. We will see that we can apply Proposition
3.15
for the sequence
\eta_{n}=\overline{\theta}_{n}
by showing that

\mbox{(i)}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }E(\overline{\theta}_{n})\to I_{\mu},\quad\mbox{(ii)}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\|x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{1}\to\mu,\quad\mbox{(iii)}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{1}\leq 1,\quad\mbox{(iv)}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{2}<M_{0},\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mbox{(v)}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\|(\overline{\theta}_{n}-1)_{+}\|_{1}\to 0.

We first obtain (ii), (iii), and (iv) directly from the assumptions. For (i), by choosing a sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}\subset S_{\mu}
satisfying

\|x_{2}(\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n})\|_{1}<\frac{1}{n},

we observe that

(3.11)

\big|E(\theta_{0,n})-I_{\mu}\big|\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\big|E(\theta_{0,n})-E(\omega_{n})\big|\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\|\theta_{0,n}+\omega_{n}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}\cdot\|x_{2}(\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n})\|_{1}^{1/2}\cdot\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n}\|_{1}^{1/2}\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }n\to\infty

which follows from the estimate (
2.7
)
in Proposition
2.2
for the case
p=2
.
Therefore we have
E(\overline{\theta}_{n})\to I_{\mu}
as
n\to\infty
by the conservation

(3.12)

E(\overline{\theta}_{n})=E(\theta_{0,n}).

For (v), the measure-preserving property of the Euler equations gives the proof. Then we apply Proposition
3.15
to find a subsequence of
\{\overline{\theta}_{n}\}
(still denoted by
\{\overline{\theta}_{n}\}
) and a sequence
\{y_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that, for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
there exists
R>0
such that

(3.13)

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\left({\bf x}+(y_{n},0)\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\mu-\varepsilon.

For convenience, we write
\overline{\theta}_{n}=\overline{\theta}_{n}(\cdot+(y_{n},0))
by suppressing the translation
\{y_{n}\}
. (Note that we still have
E(\overline{\theta}_{n})\to I_{\mu}
and
\|x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{1}\to\mu
by the invariance of kinetic energy and impulse in
x_{1}
-translation. Moreover, each norm of
\overline{\theta}_{n}
and
(\overline{\theta}_{n}-1)_{+}
also remains unchanged.) From the bound
\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{2}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\theta_{0,n}\|_{2}<M_{0},
there exists
\theta\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
such that a subsequence of
\{\overline{\theta}_{n}\}
(still denoted by
\{\overline{\theta}_{n}\}
) converges weakly to
\theta
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
. By Lemma
3.13
, we have
\theta\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
. We then give a claim below that, indeed,
\theta
is a maximizer in
S_{\mu}
.

(Claim 1)
We have
E(\overline{\theta}_{n})\to E(\theta)
as
n\to\infty
.
In other words, we obtain
E(\theta)=I_{\mu}
.
(It implies
\theta\in S_{\mu}
.
Indeed, by recalling Lemma
3.11
saying that

I_{\mu}=\widetilde{I}_{\mu},\quad S_{\mu}=\widetilde{S}_{\mu},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\theta\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu},

we obtain
\mbox{ }E(\theta)=\widetilde{I}_{\mu}\mbox{ }
and then
\theta\in S_{\mu}
.)

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists
R>1/\varepsilon
such that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\mu-\varepsilon,\quad\mbox{ and thus }\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq R}x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\leq\varepsilon

(recall (
3.13
)). We denote
B:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }|{\bf x}|<R\}
and split
\overline{\theta}_{n}
as

\overline{\theta}_{n}=\overline{\theta}_{n}^{1}+\overline{\theta}_{n}^{2}\quad\mbox{ where }\quad\overline{\theta}_{n}^{1}:=\overline{\theta}_{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{B},\quad\overline{\theta}_{n}^{2}:=\overline{\theta}_{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{B^{c}}.

Observe that

E(\overline{\theta}_{n})=E(\overline{\theta}_{n}^{1})+E(\overline{\theta}_{n}^{2})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\overline{\theta}_{n}^{1}({\bf x})\overline{\theta}_{n}^{2}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}.

Here, by

E(\overline{\theta}^{2}_{n})\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{1}\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}^{2}\|_{1}^{1/2}

and by

\Big|\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\overline{\theta}_{n}^{1}({\bf x})\overline{\theta}_{n}^{2}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\Big|\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{1}\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}^{2}\|_{1}^{1/2}

which are deduced from (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) in Proposition
2.2
, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\big|E(\overline{\theta}_{n})-E(\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n})\big|\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}^{2}\|_{1}^{1/2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon^{1/2}.

Similarly, we split
\theta
as
\theta=\theta^{1}+\theta^{2}
where
\theta^{1}:=\theta\cdot\mathbf{1}_{B}
and
\theta^{2}:=\theta\cdot\mathbf{1}_{B^{c}}
to obtain that

\big|E(\theta)-E(\theta^{1})\big|\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\|x_{2}\theta^{2}\|_{1}^{1/2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\left(\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq 1/\varepsilon}x_{2}\theta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)^{1/2}.

Lastly, observe that we have

\displaystyle|E(\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n})-E(\theta^{1})|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2}\int_{B}\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n}\cdot\Big|\mathcal{G}[\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n}]-\mathcal{G}[\theta^{1}]\Big|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg|\int_{B}\mathcal{G}[\theta^{1}]\cdot(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\mbox{(I)}+\mbox{(II)}.

We note that (II)
\to 0
as
n\to\infty
by the weak
L^{2}
convergence

\overline{\theta}_{n}\rightharpoonup\theta
and the fact that
\mathcal{G}[\theta^{1}]\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
. For (I), we have the pointwise convergence

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{G}[\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n}]({\bf x})-\mathcal{G}[\theta^{1}]({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{B}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\cdot[\overline{\theta}_{n}({\bf y})-\theta({\bf y})]\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0\quad\mbox{ for each }{\bf x}\in B

by the fact that
G({\bf x},\cdot)\in L^{2}(B)
for each
{\bf x}
and the weak
L^{2}
convergence
\overline{\theta}_{n}\rightharpoonup\theta
. Moreover, we note that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(\mbox{ }\|\mathcal{G}[\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n}]\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\mathcal{G}[\theta^{1}]\|_{L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }\right)\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\infty

which gives

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mbox{(I)}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(\mbox{ }\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(B)}\cdot\|\mathcal{G}[\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n}]-\mathcal{G}[\theta^{1}]\|_{L^{2}(B)}\mbox{ }\right)=0

where we used the Lebesgue-dominated convergence theorem to obtain the convergence
\mathcal{G}[\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n}]\to\mathcal{G}[\theta^{1}]
in
L^{2}(B)
. In sum, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\big|E(\overline{\theta}_{n})-E(\theta)\big|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\big|E(\overline{\theta}_{n})-E(\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n})\big|+\lim_{n\to\infty}\big|E(\overline{\theta}^{1}_{n})-E(\theta^{1})\big|+\big|E(\theta)-E(\theta^{1})\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\varepsilon^{1/2}+\left(\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq 1/\varepsilon}x_{2}\theta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)^{1/2}.

As the choice of
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary and
x_{2}\theta\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
, letting
\varepsilon\to 0
gives the convergence
E(\overline{\theta}_{n})\to E(\theta)
as
n\to\infty
. It means that
E(\theta)=I_{\mu}
. Together with the previous result
\theta\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
, we conclude
\theta\in S_{\mu}
by Lemma
3.11
which says that

I_{\mu}=\widetilde{I}_{\mu}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\widetilde{S}_{\mu}=S_{\mu}.

It finishes the proof of the claim.

(Claim 2)
It holds that
x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\to x_{2}\theta
strongly in
L^{1}
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Again, there exists
R>1/\varepsilon
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq R}x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\leq\varepsilon.

similarly to the proof of
(Claim 1)
. Put
\theta=\mathbf{1}_{A}
for a bounded open set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
. We have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}+\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq R}

where

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq R}x_{2}|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq R}x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq R}x_{2}\theta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon+\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq 1/\varepsilon}x_{2}\theta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}.

For the domain
\{|{\bf x}|<R\}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}x_{2}|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }R\int_{|{\bf x}|<R}|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }R\underbrace{\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A^{c}}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}}_{(I)}+R\underbrace{\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A}|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}}_{(II)}

where
(I)\to 0
by the weak
L^{2}
convergence
\overline{\theta}_{n}\rightharpoonup\theta
and

\displaystyle(II)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A}|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A\cap\{\theta\geq\overline{\theta}_{n}\}}(\theta-\overline{\theta}_{n})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A\cap\{\overline{\theta}_{n}>\theta\}}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A}(1-\min\{\overline{\theta}_{n},1\})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-1)_{+}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A}\left(1-\underbrace{\left(\min\{\overline{\theta}_{n},1\}+(\overline{\theta}_{n}-1)_{+}\right)}_{=\overline{\theta}_{n}}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+2\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-1)_{+}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A}(1-\overline{\theta}_{n})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+2\int_{\{|{\bf x}|<R\}\cap A}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-1)_{+}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }n\to\infty

by the weak
L^{2}
convergence
\overline{\theta}_{n}\rightharpoonup\theta
and the strong
L^{1}
convergence
(\overline{\theta}_{n}-1)_{+}\to 0
. In sum, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon+\int_{|{\bf x}|\geq 1/\varepsilon}x_{2}\theta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}.

Since the choice of
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary and we have
x_{2}\theta\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
, letting
\varepsilon\to 0
gives the strong
L^{1}
convergence
x_{2}\overline{\theta}_{n}\to x_{2}\theta.
It finishes the proof of the claim.

From
(Claim 1)
and
(Claim 2)
, we have
\theta\in S_{\mu}
and
\|x_{2}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\|_{1}\to 0
as
n\to\infty.

Up to now, without considering the re-indexing, we have

\overline{\theta}_{n}=\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot+(y_{n},0)\right)

and therefore

\varepsilon_{0}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\|x_{2}(\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\omega)\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\|x_{2}(\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\theta(\cdot-(y,0)))\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|x_{2}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\|_{1}\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }n\to\infty,

which is a contradiction. It finishes the proof of Theorem
3.20
.
∎

3.4.2.
Stability of
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}

By narrowing the scope of our discussion from
S_{\mu}
to its subset
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
by imposing the smallest
L^{1}
mass condition as in Definition
3.17
, we can obtain the stability based on the combined norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}+\|x_{2}(\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}
. For convenience, we denote the minimal mass as

\mbox{ }\nu_{\mu}^{min}:=\inf_{\eta\in S_{\mu}}\|\eta\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}.

We now give a result that the set
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
is nonempty and stable.
The proofs for the following lemma and theorem are in order.

Lemma 3.21
.

For each
\mu\in(0,\infty)
, the set
\mbox{ }\mathscr{S}_{\mu}\mbox{ }
is nonempty.

Theorem 3.22
.

For each
\mu,\varepsilon\in(0,\infty)
, there exists
\delta=\delta(\mu,\varepsilon)>0
such that, for any initial data
\theta_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
which is nonnegative and satisfies

\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}}\left\{\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0}-\omega)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}<\delta,

there exists a shift function
\tau:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\tau(0)=0
such that the unique global weak solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.3
) satisfies

\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}}\left\{\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega^{\tau(t)}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega^{\tau(t)})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0

where

\omega^{\tau}(\cdot)\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\omega\left(\cdot-(\tau,0)\right)\quad\mbox{ for each }\tau\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof of Lemma
3.21
.

Let
\mu>0
and choose a sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}\subset S_{\mu}
such that we have
\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}\searrow\nu^{min}_{\mu}
as
n\to\infty
.
As
\|\omega_{n}\|_{2}=\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}^{1/2}\leq 1
, there exists a function
\omega\in L^{2}
and a subsequence of
\{\omega_{n}\}
(still denoted by
\{\omega_{n}\}
) such that
\omega_{n}
converges weakly to
\omega
in
L^{2}
,
i.e.
,
\omega_{n}\rightharpoonup\omega
in
L^{2}
.
It remains to show that
\omega\in S_{\mu}
and
\|\omega\|_{1}=\nu^{min}_{\mu}.
Due to
\omega_{n}\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
, we have
\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
by Lemma
3.13
.
By suitable
x_{1}
-translations,
\omega_{n}
satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition.
Then we have
E(\omega_{n})\to E(\omega)
as
n\to\infty
by Lemma
3.16
, and therefore
E(\omega)=I_{\mu}
.
By Lemma
3.11
, we have
E(\omega)=\widetilde{I}_{\mu}
, and thus the previous result
\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
gives
\omega\in\widetilde{S}_{\mu}
, which leads to
\omega\in S_{\mu}
.

Moreover, we can easily show that
\|\omega\|_{1}=\nu^{min}_{\mu}
as follow:

\|\omega\|_{1}=\|\omega\|_{2}^{2}=\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega\cdot(\omega-\omega_{n})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}}_{\to\mbox{ }0\mbox{ }\mbox{ as }n\to\infty}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega\cdot\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

where

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega\cdot\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{2}\|\omega_{n}\|_{2}=\|\omega\|_{1}^{1/2}\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}^{1/2}\to\|\omega\|_{1}^{1/2}\sqrt{\nu^{min}_{\mu}}\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }n\to\infty

which implies that
\|\omega\|_{1}\leq\nu^{min}_{\mu}
.
Then we have
\|\omega\|_{1}=\nu^{min}_{\mu}
by the definition of
\nu^{min}_{\mu}
, and it means that
\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
by some
x_{1}
-translation.
It completes the proof of Lemma
3.21
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
3.22
.

Based on Lemma
3.21
, we suppose the contrary of the the statement given in Theorem
3.22
,
i.e.
, there exists
\mu,\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that, for each
n\geq 1
, there exist
t_{n}\geq 0
and the initial data
\theta_{0,n}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
satisfying

\displaystyle\theta_{0,n}

\displaystyle\geq 0,\quad\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}}\Big\{\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0,n}-\omega)\|_{1}\Big\}<\frac{1}{n},\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu},\mbox{ }y\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\big\|\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\omega\big(\cdot-(y,0)\big)\big\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}+\big\|x_{2}\Big(\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\omega\big(\cdot-(y,0)\big)\Big)\big\|_{1}\right\}\geq\varepsilon_{0},

where
\theta_{n}
denotes the solution of the Euler equations (
1.3
) corresponding to each initial data
\theta_{0,n}
.
Hereafter we denote
\overline{\theta}_{n}:=\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)
.
By choosing a sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}\subset\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
satisfying

\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n})\|_{1}<\frac{1}{n},

we have

\|\theta_{n}\|_{1}=\|\theta_{0,n}\|_{1}\leq\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n}\|_{1}+\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}<1+\frac{1}{n},\quad\|\theta_{n}\|_{2}=\|\theta_{0,n}\|_{2}\leq\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n}\|_{2}+\|\omega_{n}\|_{2}<1+\frac{1}{n}

and

\|(\theta_{n}-1)_{+}\|_{1}=\|(\theta_{0,n}-1)_{+}\|_{1}\leq\|(\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n})_{+}\|_{1}\leq\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n}\|_{1}<\frac{1}{n},

together with

E(\theta_{n})\to I_{\mu}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|x_{2}\theta_{n}\|_{1}=\|x_{2}\theta_{0,n}\|_{1}\to\mu

by using the same arguments with (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) in the proof of Theorem
3.20
.
Then we can repeat all the processes of the proof of Theorem
3.20
to find a subsequence of
\{\overline{\theta}_{n}\}
(still denoted by
\{\overline{\theta}_{n}\}
), a sequence
\{y_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
, and a maximizer
\theta\in S_{\mu}
such that, by suppressing the translation
\{y_{n}\}
to write
\overline{\theta}_{n}=\overline{\theta}_{n}(\cdot+(y_{n},0))
, we have

\overline{\theta}_{n}\rightharpoonup\theta\quad\mbox{in}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }L^{2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|x_{2}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\|_{1}\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }n\to\infty.

It remains to show that
\theta\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
(by some
x_{1}
-translation) and the strong
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
convergence
\overline{\theta}_{n}\to\theta
, which would derive a contradiction.
We note that

\|\theta\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\theta\|^{2}_{2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\overline{\theta}_{0,n}\|_{2}^{2}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\nu^{min}_{\mu},

which gives
\|\theta\|_{1}=\nu^{min}_{\mu}
by the definition of
\nu^{min}_{\mu}
.
Therefore we have
\theta(\cdot-(y,0))\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
for some
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, by
\overline{\theta}_{n}\rightharpoonup\theta
in
L^{2}
and
\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{2}\to\sqrt{\nu^{min}_{\mu}}=\|\theta\|_{2}
, we obtain the strong
L^{2}
convergence,
i.e.
,

\overline{\theta}_{n}\to\theta\quad\mbox{in}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }L^{2}.

For the strong
L^{1}
convergence, we denote
\theta=\mathbf{1}_{A}
for a bounded open set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
. Then by

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\overline{\theta}_{0,n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\nu^{min}_{\mu}=|A|

and

\int_{A}\overline{\theta}_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=\int\mathbf{1}_{A}\cdot(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{A}\theta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\to\int_{A}\theta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=|A|,

we have

\int_{A^{c}}|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=\int_{A^{c}}\overline{\theta}_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\overline{\theta}_{n}\|_{1}-\int_{A}\overline{\theta}_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }n\to\infty

implying that
\overline{\theta}_{n}\to\theta
in
L^{1}(A^{c})
. In
L^{1}(A)
, observe that we have

\displaystyle\int_{A}|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\int_{A\cap\{\overline{\theta}_{n}>\theta\}}\left(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{A\cap\{\overline{\theta}_{n}\leq\theta\}}\left(\theta-\overline{\theta}_{n}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=2\int_{A\cap\{\overline{\theta}_{n}>\theta\}}\left(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{A\cap\{\overline{\theta}_{n}>\theta\}}\left(\theta-\overline{\theta}_{n}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{A\cap\{\overline{\theta}_{n}\leq\theta\}}\left(\theta-\overline{\theta}_{n}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }2\|(\overline{\theta}_{n}-1)_{+}\|_{L^{1}(A)}+\int_{A}(1-\overline{\theta}_{n})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }n\to\infty

by the weak
L^{2}
convergence
\overline{\theta}_{n}\rightharpoonup\theta
. It gives the strong
L^{1}
convergence,
i.e.
,

\overline{\theta}_{n}\to\theta\quad\mbox{in}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }L^{1}.

In sum, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu},\mbox{ }y\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg\{\big\|\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\omega(\cdot-(y,0))\big\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}+\big\|x_{2}\big(\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\omega(\cdot-(y,0))\big)\big\|_{1}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\quad\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\mbox{ }\bigg\{\big\|\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\theta(\cdot-(y,0))\big\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}+\big\|x_{2}\big(\theta_{n}(t_{n},\cdot)-\theta(\cdot-(y,0))\big)\big\|_{1}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\quad\|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}}+\|x_{2}(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\|_{1}\to 0\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }n\to\infty,

which is a contradiction. It finishes the proof of Theorem
3.22
.
∎

3.4.3.
Estimate on shift function

It remains to obtain the shift function estimate given in Theorem
3.19
. We start with any shift function given in Theorem
3.22
, that is, any function
\tau:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the relation

\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}}\left\{\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega^{\tau(t)}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega^{\tau(t)})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right\}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0

in Theorem
3.22
.
Even though the choice of the shift function
\tau(t)
is allowed to vary to some extent at each time
t>0
and the uniqueness (up to translation) of Sadovskii vortex patches in
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}\subset S_{\mu}
is unknown, we can roughly investigate the speed of the shift function
\tau
using some uniform estimates given in Lemma
3.14
. For convenience, for any perturbed solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.3
) satisfying
\theta(t,\cdot)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
with a bounded support for each
t\geq 0
, we denote its horizontal center of mass as
a:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
such that

a(t)\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}.

For later use, we define the width of the set
\mbox{ }{\mathrm{supp}}\,\theta(t,\cdot)\mbox{ }
as
b(t)
,
i.e.
,

b(t)\mbox{ }:=\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\theta(t,\cdot)}|x_{1}|.

Then we obtain the following lemma:

Lemma 3.23
.

There exists a constant
\mu_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that the following holds:

For any
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, there exist some constants
\varepsilon_{\mu},\mbox{ }C_{\mu}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
with a corresponding constant
\delta=\delta(\mu,\varepsilon)\in(0,\varepsilon)
in Theorem
3.22
and for any initial data
\theta_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
having a bounded support and satisfying the assumptions in Theorem
3.22
,
its solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.3
) and any shift function
\tau:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
given in Theorem
3.22
satisfy

\left|\mbox{ }\tau(t)-W_{\mu}t\mbox{ }\right|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot C_{\mu}\Big[\left(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right)t+b(0)+1\Big]\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0,

where
W_{\mu}>0
is the traveling speed of Sadovskii vortex patches in
S_{\mu}
uniquely determined by
\mu>0
.

Lemma
3.23
above is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.24
and Lemma
3.25
, which will follow in order. Lemma
3.24
deals with
|a(t)-W_{\mu}t|
, while Lemma
3.25
is for
|\tau(t)-a(t)|
. In their proofs, we use some uniform estimates given in Lemma
3.14
. For example, we note that the estimate (
3.8
) implies

\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\omega}|{\bf x}|\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C\cdot\mu^{1/3}\quad\mbox{ for each }\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}

for a uniform constant
C>0
, given that
\mu>0
is small enough.

[I] Estimate on horizontal center of mass

We start with an estimate of the horizontal center of mass of the perturbed solution
\theta
. We observe that as long as the solution stays close enough to the set
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}\subset S_{\mu}
up to translation, the center
a(\cdot)
of the solution will travel nearly at the speed
W_{\mu}
. We have the following lemma:

Lemma 3.24
.

There exists a constant
\mu_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that the following holds:

For any
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, there exists some constants

\varepsilon_{\mu},\mbox{ }C_{\mu}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
with a corresponding constant
\delta=\delta(\mu,\varepsilon)>0
in Theorem
3.22
and for any initial data
\theta_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
having a bounded support and satisfying the assumptions in Theorem
3.22
, its solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.3
) satisfies

\Big|\mbox{ }a(t)-W_{\mu}t\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot C_{\mu}\Big[\left(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right)t+b(0)+1\Big]\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0

where
W_{\mu}>0
is the traveling speed of Sadovskii vortex patches in
S_{\mu}
uniquely determined by
\mu>0
.

Proof.

Let
\mu>0
be small enough such that the traveling speed
W_{\mu}
is uniquely determined by the relation (
3.9
) in Lemma
3.14
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and
t>0
. By the assumption, we have

\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega\|_{1}<\varepsilon\mbox{ }
for some
\omega\in S_{\mu}
satisfying that
\omega(\cdot-(y,0))\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
for some
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Observe that, using integration by parts and the Euler equation

\partial_{t}\theta+{\bf u}\cdot\nabla\theta=0,

we get

\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}a(t)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}u_{1}(t,{\bf x})\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[\frac{-x_{2}+y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}+\frac{x_{2}+y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}^{*}|^{2}}\right]\theta(t,{\bf y})\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\textrm{d}{\bf x}

where
{\bf y}^{*}=(y_{1},-y_{2})
. Moreover, we have the formula of traveling speed
W_{\mu}
:

W_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\omega\|_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[\frac{-x_{2}+y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}+\frac{x_{2}+y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}^{*}|^{2}}\right]\omega({\bf y})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\textrm{d}{\bf x}

(given in
[
47
, Remark 3.16]
; see also
[
100
, p.1062]
)
Then we have

(3.14)

\displaystyle\left|\mbox{ }\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}a(t)-W_{\mu}\mbox{ }\right|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\Bigg|1-\frac{\|\omega\|_{1}}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}}\Bigg|W_{\mu}\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{1}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|}\Big|\theta(t,{\bf y})\theta(t,{\bf x})-\omega({\bf y})\omega({\bf x})\Big|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\varepsilon\mbox{ }\frac{W_{\mu}}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}}\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}}\left[\Big\|\theta(t,\cdot)*|\cdot|^{-1}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}}+\Big\|\omega*|\cdot|^{-1}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}}\right]\cdot\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}}\left(W_{\mu}+\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}+\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}}\left(W_{\mu}+\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}+1\right)

where we used the facts that
\|\omega\|_{1}\leq 1
and
\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}=1
for the last inequality. Furthermore, we will simplify the last line in (
3.14
) by estimating
\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}
and
W_{\mu}
using
\mu>0
in that

(3.15)

\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}\simeq\mu^{2/3}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad W_{\mu}\simeq\mu^{1/3}.

First, we recall Lemma
3.14
-(v):

W_{\mu}=W\cdot\mu^{1/3}\quad\mbox{ for some uniform constant }W>0.

Moreover, we recall Lemma
3.14
-(ii) to observe

\frac{1}{C_{0}}\cdot\mu^{2/3}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{0}\cdot\mu^{2/3}

for some uniform constant
C_{0}>1
. We put
\varepsilon_{\mu}<\mu^{2/3}\big/2C_{0}
and choose an arbitrary
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
to obtain
\mbox{ }\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}\simeq\mu^{2/3}\mbox{ }
in (
3.15
) by

(3.16)

\frac{1}{2C_{0}}\mu^{2/3}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{1}-\varepsilon\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{1}+\varepsilon\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2C_{0}\mu^{2/3}\quad\mbox{ for each }t\geq 0.

Then, using (
3.15
), we simplify the last line in (
3.14
) by

\Big|\mbox{ }\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}a(t)-W_{\mu}\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot(1+\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}})=\varepsilon\cdot(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}),

which leads to

(3.17)

\Big|\mbox{ }a(t)-W_{\mu}t\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})\,t+|a(0)|.

It remains to estimate
|a(0)|
. From the assumption of Theorem
3.22
:

\|\theta_{0}-\eta\|_{1}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ for some }\mbox{ }\eta\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu},

we obtain

(3.18)

\displaystyle|a(0)|\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\underbrace{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}^{-1}}_{\simeq\mbox{ }\mu^{-2/3}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\theta_{0}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}(\theta_{0}-\eta)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right|+\bigg|\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\eta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}}_{=0}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}|\theta_{0}-\eta|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot\Bigg(\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\theta_{0}}|x_{1}|\ +\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\eta}|x_{1}|\Bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot\big(b(0)+\mu^{1/3}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mu}\varepsilon\cdot\big(b(0)+1\big)

where we used Lemma
3.14
-(ii) to get

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\eta}|{\bf x}|\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\mu^{1/3}.
Applying (
3.18
) to (
3.17
) gives

\big|\mbox{ }a(t)-W_{\mu}t\mbox{ }\big|\mbox{ }\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot\Big[(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})\,t+b(0)+1\Big]

which is the desired result.
It completes the proof.
∎

[II] Shift function
\tau
and horizontal center of mass

We are now ready to prove that any shift function
\tau(\cdot)
and the center of mass
a(\cdot)
of a given perturbed solution
\theta
travel with a similar speed.

Lemma 3.25
.

There exists a constant
\mu_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that the following holds:

For any
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, there exist some constants
\varepsilon_{\mu},\mbox{ }C_{\mu}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
with a corresponding constant
\delta=\delta(\mu,\varepsilon)\in(0,\varepsilon)
in Theorem
3.22
and for any initial data
\theta_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
having a bounded support and satisfying the assumptions in Theorem
3.22
,
its solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.3
) and any shift function
\tau:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
given in Theorem
3.22
satisfy

\Big|\mbox{ }\tau(t)-a(t)\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot C_{\mu}\Big[\left(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right)\,t+b(0)+1\Big]\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0.

Proof.

Let
\mu_{0}>0
be the constant in Lemma
3.14
and Lemma
3.24
.
We choose
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
and let
\varepsilon_{\mu}>0
be the constant in Lemma
3.24
.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
and
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,\mu)\in(0,\varepsilon)
be the constant in Theorem
3.22
.
We choose an initial date
\theta_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
satisfying the assumption in Theorem
3.22
.
Recalling (
3.16
) in the proof of Lemma
3.24
, the choice of
\varepsilon_{\mu}>0
guarantees for the solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of the Euler equations (
1.3
) to satisfies

(3.19)

\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\simeq_{C_{0}}\mbox{ }\mu^{2/3}\quad\mbox{ for each }t\geq 0.

for the uniform constant
C_{0}>1
given in (
3.7
) and (
3.8
). Then, by denoting its velocity as
{\bf u}:[0,\infty)\times\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

(3.20)

\|{\bf u}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\|\theta(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}

which leads to

(3.21)

b(t)-b(0)\mbox{ }\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }t\cdot(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0.

Therefore, for any
t\geq 0
, we define
\mbox{ }A_{\tau(t)}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\left\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }|x_{1}-\tau(t)|\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{0}\cdot\mu^{1/3}\mbox{ }\right\}\mbox{ }
to put

a(t)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\left[\int_{A_{\tau(t)}}+\int_{A_{\tau(t)}^{c}}\right]\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }I+II.

We will obtain

a(t)\simeq\tau(t)

by showing that

\big|I\big|\ll 1\quad\mbox{ and }\quad II\simeq\tau(t).

First, the estimate (
3.21
) gives the smallness of
(I)
in that

(3.22)

\displaystyle I\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\Bigg|\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\int_{A^{c}_{\tau(t)}}\mbox{ }\Bigg|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\int_{A_{\tau(t)}^{c}}|x_{1}|\mbox{ }\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }b(t)\cdot\frac{\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)}^{c})}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\left(C_{1}(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})\cdot t+b(0)\right)\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}

for some constant
C_{1}\in(0,\infty)
depending only on
\mu>0
.
Here we used the estimate (
3.8
) in Lemma
3.14
to derive

\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)}^{c})}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\inf_{\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}}\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega^{\tau(t)}\|_{1}<\varepsilon.

For
(II)
, we choose some
\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
satisfying

\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\omega(\cdot-(\tau(t),0))\|_{1}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ for a shift function }\mbox{ }\tau(\cdot),

we can obtain
\mbox{ }II\mbox{ }\simeq\mbox{ }\tau(t)\mbox{ }
by

(3.23)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mbox{ }II-\frac{\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)})}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\cdot\tau(t)\mbox{ }\Bigg|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\Bigg|\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\int_{A_{\tau(t)}}x_{1}\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}-\frac{\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)})}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\cdot\tau(t)\mbox{ }\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\Bigg|\int_{A_{\tau(t)}}(x_{1}-\tau(t))\cdot\mbox{ }\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\Bigg|\int_{A_{\tau(t)}}(x_{1}-\tau(t))\cdot\mbox{ }\Big[\theta(t,{\bf x})-\omega({\bf x}-(\tau(t),0))\Big]\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\int_{A_{\tau(t)}}|x_{1}-\tau(t)|\cdot\big|\theta(t,{\bf x})-\omega({\bf x}-(\tau(t),0))\big|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{C_{0}\cdot\mu^{1/3}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\cdot\varepsilon.

Hence, together with (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) we obtain

\displaystyle\big|\mbox{ }a(t)-\tau(t)\mbox{ }\big|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\Bigg|1-\frac{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}{\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)})}}\Bigg|\cdot|a(t)|+\frac{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}{\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)})}}\cdot\Bigg|\mbox{ }a(t)-\frac{\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)})}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\cdot\tau(t)\mbox{ }\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}-\varepsilon}\cdot|a(t)|+\frac{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}-\varepsilon}\cdot\Bigg|\mbox{ }a(t)-\frac{\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)})}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\cdot\tau(t)\mbox{ }\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}-\varepsilon}\cdot|a(t)|+\frac{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}-\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\mbox{ }\big|(I)\big|+\bigg|\mbox{ }(II)-\frac{\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(A_{\tau(t)})}}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\cdot\tau(t)\mbox{ }\bigg|\mbox{ }\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}-\varepsilon}\cdot|a(t)|+\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}-\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\mbox{ }C_{1}(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})\cdot t+b(0)+C_{0}\mu^{1/3}\mbox{ }\right).

Based on the estimate (
3.19
), we re-take a smaller
\varepsilon_{\mu}>0
to have
\varepsilon<\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}/2
. Then we get a simplified estimate

|\mbox{ }a(t)-\tau(t)\mbox{ }|\mbox{ }\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot\left(\mbox{ }|a(t)|+(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})\cdot t+b(0)+1\mbox{ }\right).

Lastly, as we have

|a(t)|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}|x_{1}|\mbox{ }\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }b(t)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{1}(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})\cdot t+b(0),

we have

|\mbox{ }a(t)-\tau(t)\mbox{ }|\mbox{ }\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }\varepsilon\cdot\Big[(1+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})\cdot t+b(0)+1\Big].

It completes the proof.
∎

3.4.4.
Proof of Theorem
3.19

We now prove Theorem
3.19
based on Lemma
3.21
, Theorem
3.22
, and Lemma
3.23
.

Proof of Theorem
3.19
.

Let
\mu>0
be small enough such that Lemma
3.23
holds with the corresponding constants
C_{\mu},\varepsilon_{\mu}>0
. By Lemma
3.21
, the set
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
is nonempty. Then for each
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
, we apply Theorem
3.22
to obtain the stability of the set
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
with the estimate on the shift function
\tau
given in Lemma
3.23
. It completes the proof.
∎

3.4.5.
Application: Linear growth of perimeter of perturbed set near
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}

Based on Theorem
3.19
, we will now prove that there exists a patch-type perturbed solution of the Sadovskii vortex patch whose diameter and perimeter are initially finite and grow linearly in time. Indeed, suppose a perturbed initial data possesses an (arbitrarily) long, thin tail that extends in the opposite direction of the traveling velocity
(W_{\mu},0)
of the Sadovskii vortex patches (see Lemma
3.26
). Then, as time goes on, its tail continues to lag and elongate backward, while most of its body goes forward nearly with the velocity of Sadovskii vortex patches
(W_{\mu},0)
(see Lemma
3.27
).

In Lemma
3.26
below, we show that there exists a patch-type perturbed initial set of each Sadovskii vortex patch in
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
which has a smooth boundary of finite length and has an arbitrarily long tail. (Its proof will be given in Appendix
A
.)

Lemma 3.26

(Initial data having long tail)
.

There exist a constant
\mu_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that the following holds:

For any
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, there exists some constant
\varepsilon_{\mu},C_{\mu}\in(0,\infty)\mbox{ }
such that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu}),\mbox{ }L\in(C_{\mu},\infty),\mbox{ }\mbox{and}\mbox{ }\omega\in S_{\mu}
satisfying

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0,

there exists a set
\Omega\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
satisfying the following properties.

(i)
\Omega
is an open, bounded, and simply connected set satisfying the Steiner symmetry condition.

(ii) The boundary
\partial\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
is smooth and its length is finite.

(iii) It holds that

\sup_{{\bf x}\in\Omega}x_{2}<1,\quad\sup_{{\bf x}\in\Omega}|x_{1}|=L,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|\omega-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}<\varepsilon.

Based on the stability of
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
(Theorem
3.19
) together with Lemma
3.26
above, we get the following lemma establishing the linear-in-time perimeter growth near
\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
, using bootstrap arguments.

Lemma 3.27

(Elongation of tail)
.

There exists a constant
\mu_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that the following holds:

For any
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, there exist some constants
\varepsilon_{\mu},C_{\mu}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
and
\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
, there exists a set
\Omega\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
satisfying the following properties.

(i)
\Omega
is open, bounded, simply-connected.

(ii) The boundary
\partial\Omega
is smooth and has a finite length.

(iii) The initial data defined by
\theta_{0}:=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}
satisfies
