\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0}-\omega)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\varepsilon

and its solution
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
to the Euler equations (
1.3
) satisfies

\left(\mbox{ Diameter of }\mbox{ }{\mathrm{supp}}\,\theta(t,\cdot)\mbox{ }\right)\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }(W_{\mu}-C_{\mu}\varepsilon^{1/3})\cdot t+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\quad\mbox{ for any }\mbox{ }t\geq 0

where
W_{\mu}>0
is the traveling speed of Sadovskii vortex patches in
S_{\mu}
uniquely determined by
\mu>0
.

Proof of Lemma
3.27
.

Let
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
where
\mu_{0}>0
is the uniform constant given in Lemma
3.14
, Theorem
3.19
, and Lemma
3.26
. We choose
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mu})
, where
\varepsilon_{\mu},C_{\mu}>0
are the constant given in Theorem
3.19
and Lemma
3.26
, and let
\delta\in(0,\varepsilon)
be the corresponding constant given in Theorem
3.19
. (In the proof, we will assume further the smallness of
\varepsilon>0
in the way of allowing dependences only on
\mu>0
.)
Let
\omega\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
be any maximizer. Note that the estimate (
3.8
) implies that

(3.24)

\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\omega}}x_{2}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }1

if we assume that
C\mu^{1/3}<1
in (
3.8
). By Lemma
3.26
, we obtain a set
\Omega
which is open, bounded, simply-connected, and satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition with

\sup_{{\bf x}\in\Omega}x_{2}<1,\quad\sup_{{\bf x}\in\Omega}|x_{1}|=\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}>C_{\mu},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|\omega-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}\|_{1}\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}

for which we may re-take
\varepsilon_{\mu}
to guarantee
3/\varepsilon^{1/2}>C_{\mu}
. In addition, Lemma
3.26
tells us that its boundary
\partial\Omega
is in
C^{\infty}
and of finite length. Let
x_{\varepsilon}\in[0,\infty)
be a number satisfying

\left(-\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{1/2}},\mbox{ }x_{\varepsilon}\right)\in\overline{\Omega}.

By defining

\theta_{0}:=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega},

we have
\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{2}=\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{1}^{1/2}
and
\|x_{2}(\theta_{0}-\omega)\|_{1}\leq\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{1}\mbox{ }
by the assumption (
3.24
), and thus by assuming
\varepsilon\leq 1
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{1}+\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{2}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0}-\omega)\|_{1}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }2\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{1}+\|\theta_{0}-\omega\|_{1}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }2\cdot\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}+\frac{\delta}{2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\delta

implying that
\theta_{0}
satisfies the assumptions in Theorem
3.19
. Let
\theta:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be the solution from the initial data
\theta_{0}
and we denote
\tau:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a shift function given in Theorem
3.19
to obtain that

(3.25)

\tau(t)\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }(W-2C_{\mu}\varepsilon)\cdot t-C_{\mu}\left(\varepsilon\cdot(b+1)+|a|\right)\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }(W_{\mu}-2C_{\mu}\varepsilon)\cdot t-4C_{\mu}\varepsilon^{1/2}\quad\mbox{ for any }\mbox{ }t\geq 0

by the relations

a=\frac{1}{\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\theta_{0}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad b=\sup_{{\bf x}\in\Omega}|x_{1}|=\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}

which are induced by the assumptions on the set
\Omega
. We observe that

\tau(t)\geq 0\quad\mbox{ for any }\mbox{ }t\geq\frac{4C_{\mu}\varepsilon^{1/2}}{W_{\mu}-2C_{\mu}\varepsilon}=:T_{\varepsilon}>0

when we assume that

\varepsilon<\frac{W_{\mu}}{4C_{\mu}}.

Then, by denoting the flow map as
\phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2}):[0,\infty)\times\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
and the velocity as

{\bf u}:[0,\infty)\times\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\left(t,(-3/\varepsilon^{1/2},x_{\varepsilon})\right)\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }-\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}+\sup_{s\leq t}\|{\bf u}(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\cdot t

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }-\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}+C_{0}\cdot\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}\cdot t\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0

for some uniform constant
C_{0}>0
, similarly to (
3.20
). Based on the observation that
T_{\varepsilon}\searrow 0
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, we have

(3.26)

\phi_{1}\left(t,(-3/\varepsilon^{1/2},x_{\varepsilon})\right)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }-\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\quad\mbox{ for any }\mbox{ }t\in[0,T_{\varepsilon}]

if we assume that
\varepsilon>0
is small enough to satisfy

C_{0}\cdot\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}\cdot T_{\varepsilon}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}.

Here we can use the facts that
\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}=1
and
\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}\leq\|\omega\|_{1}+\varepsilon\leq C_{1}\cdot\mu^{1/3}+\varepsilon_{\mu}\lesssim_{\mu}1
for some uniform constant
C_{1}>0
so that, in the above assumption, the smallness of
\varepsilon>0
depends only on
\mu>0
.
By denoting

{\bf z}(t)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }(z_{1}(t),z_{2}(t))\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\phi\left(t,(-3/\varepsilon^{1/2},x_{\varepsilon})\right)

and using the estimates (
3.26
), for some
\eta\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
we have

(3.27)

\displaystyle u_{1}(t,{\bf z}(t))\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{1}{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}(t)|}\theta(t,{\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{1}{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}(t)|}|\theta(t,{\bf y})-\eta^{\tau(t)}({\bf y})|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{1}{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}(t)|}\eta^{\tau(t)}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\left[\int_{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}(t)|\geq 1}+\int_{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}(t)|<1}\right]\mbox{ }\frac{1}{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}(t)|}|\theta(t,{\bf y})-\eta^{\tau(t)}({\bf y})|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}+\frac{1}{\displaystyle\inf_{{\bf y}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\eta^{\tau(t)}}|{\bf y}-{\bf z}(t)|}\|\eta\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\eta^{\tau(t)}\|_{1}+\|\theta(t,\cdot)-\eta^{\tau(t)}\|_{3}+\frac{C_{1}\mu^{1/3}}{-z_{1}(t)-C_{1}\mu^{1/3}+\tau(t)}

\displaystyle<\mbox{ }\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{1/3}+\frac{C_{1}\mu^{1/3}}{2\varepsilon^{-1/2}-C_{1}\mu^{1/3}-4C_{\mu}\varepsilon^{1/2}}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{1/3}+C_{1}\mu^{1/3}\varepsilon^{1/2}\mbox{ }\lesssim_{\mu}\mbox{ }\varepsilon^{1/3}\quad\mbox{ for each }t\in[0,T_{\varepsilon}]

assuming that
\varepsilon>0
is small enough to

2\varepsilon^{-1/2}-C_{1}\mu^{1/3}-4C_{\mu}\varepsilon^{1/2}\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }\varepsilon^{-1/2}.

(We note that the choice of
\eta\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
depends on each
t>0
, but the resulting estimate only depends on
\mu>0
and
\varepsilon>0
.) In the last two lines in (
3.27
), we used the fact that
\tau(t)\geq-4C_{\mu}\varepsilon^{1/3}
, the estimate (
3.26
), and Hölder’s inequality. In sum, we have

u_{1}(t,{\bf z}(t))\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/3}\quad\mbox{ for any }t\in[0,T_{\varepsilon}]

for some constant
C_{2}>0
depending only on
\mu>0
. We define

T\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\sup\left\{\mbox{ }t>0\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }u_{1}(s,{\bf z}(s))\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/3}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\mbox{ for any }s\in[0,t]\mbox{ }\right\}\mbox{ }\in\mbox{ }[T_{\varepsilon},\infty].

If
T<\infty,
observe that we have

(3.28)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mbox{ }\mbox{ }u_{1}(t,{\bf z}(t))\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/3}\quad\mbox{ for any }t\in[0,T),\\
&\mbox{ }\mbox{ }u_{1}(T,{\bf z}(T))\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }C_{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/3}\end{aligned}\right.

from the continuity of
{\bf u}
and
\phi
. Hence we have

(3.29)

\displaystyle z_{1}(T)\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }z_{1}(T_{\varepsilon})+\int_{T_{\varepsilon}}^{T}u_{1}(t,{\bf z}(t))dt

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }-\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}+C_{2}(T-T_{\varepsilon})\cdot\varepsilon^{1/3}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }-\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}+(T-T_{\varepsilon})\cdot(W_{\mu}-2C_{\mu}\varepsilon)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }-\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}+\tau(T)

using (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) under the smallness assumption on
\varepsilon>0
:

\varepsilon^{1/3}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }(W_{\mu}-2C_{\mu}\varepsilon)\cdot C_{2}^{-1}.

Using (
3.29
), we now repeat the processes of (
3.27
) in that, for some
\eta\in\mathscr{S}_{\mu}
we have

\displaystyle u_{1}(T,{\bf z}(T))\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\lesssim\mbox{ }\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{1/3}+\frac{1}{\displaystyle\inf_{{\bf y}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\eta^{\tau(T)}}\big|{\bf y}-{\bf z}(T)\big|}\|\eta\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{1/3}+\frac{C_{1}\mu^{1/3}}{2\varepsilon^{-1/2}-C_{1}\mu^{1/3}}

\displaystyle<\mbox{ }\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{1/3}+\frac{C_{1}\mu^{1/3}}{2\varepsilon^{-1/2}-C_{1}\mu^{1/3}-4C_{\mu}\varepsilon^{1/2}},

which would lead to

u_{1}(T,{\bf z}(T))\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/3}.

It contradicts to (
3.28
), and therefore
T=\infty
. In other words, we have

u_{1}(t,{\bf z}(t))\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/3}\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0.

We now introduce the horizontal center of mass of the solution
\theta(t,\cdot)
given by

a(t):=\frac{1}{\|\theta(t,0)\|_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\theta(t,{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}.

We note that Lemma
3.24
gives the estimate

a(t)\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }(W_{\mu}-C_{3}\varepsilon)t\quad\mbox{ for any }\mbox{ }t\geq 0,

where
a(0)=a=0
(recall (
3.25
)) and
C_{3}>0
is a constant depending only on
\mu>0
.
It gives us

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{ }\mbox{ Diameter of }\mbox{ }{\mathrm{supp}}\,\theta(t,\cdot)\mbox{ }\right)\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }a(t)-z_{1}(t)\mbox{ }\Big|=a(t)-z_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }(W_{\mu}-C_{3}\varepsilon)\cdot t-\left(z_{1}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}u_{1}(s,{\bf z}(s))ds\mbox{ }\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }(W_{\mu}-C_{3}\varepsilon-C_{2}\varepsilon^{1/3})\cdot t+\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }\left(W_{\mu}-(C_{3}+C_{2})\varepsilon^{1/3}\right)\cdot t+\frac{3}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\quad\mbox{ for any }t\geq 0.

It completes the proof.
∎

4.
Existence and stability of regular Sadovskii vortex

This section is devoted to the proof of our second main result, Theorem
1.6
, which concerns the existence of a Sadovskii vortex with higher regularity.
More precisely, we construct traveling vortex pair solutions to (
1.1
) (or equivalently, (
1.3
) in the half-plane) that belong to the class
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

4.1.
Technical statements for second result: Theorem
1.6

To address the regularity question, we adopt a variational approach inspired by the framework introduced in
[
1
]
.
We formulate a
penalized energy
functional
E_{p}(\omega)
and maximize it over a class of odd-symmetric functions with prescribed impulse and mass.
This framework is flexible enough to accommodate a wide range of regularities, depending on the choice of the vorticity function
f(t)=t_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
with
p>4/3
in (
1.5
).
In what follows, we introduce the precise variational setup and state the main results.

Let
0<\mu,\nu,\lambda<\infty
as before and consider only
p\in(4/3,\infty)
unless otherwise specified.
To establish a variational framework, we define the space of admissible functions as

\displaystyle K_{\mu,\nu}(p)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left\{\mbox{ }\omega\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }\ \omega\geq 0,\mbox{ }\|x_{2}\omega\|_{1}=\mu,\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\leq\nu\mbox{ }\right\}.

The stream function
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
is expressed in terms of
G({\bf x},{\bf y})
, satisfying the Dirichlet boundary condition in the half-plane:

(4.1)

\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}.

Here, we recall that

G({\bf x},{\bf y}):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\quad\mbox{ for }\mbox{ }{\bf x}\neq{\bf y}.

We then consider an alternative maximization problem of the following
L^{p}
-penalized energy functional on
K_{\mu,\nu}(p)
:

\displaystyle E_{p,\lambda}(\omega)\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }E(\omega)-\frac{\lambda}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left(\frac{\omega}{\lambda}\right)^{p}\textrm{d}{\bf x}.

The supremum of
E_{p,\lambda}
over the admissible set
K_{\mu,\nu}(p)
is denoted by

(4.2)

\displaystyle I_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}(p)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\sup_{\omega\in K_{\mu,\nu}(p)}E_{p,\lambda}(\omega).

We define
S_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}(p)
as the set of maximizers attaining (
4.2
),
i.e.
,

\omega\in S_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}(p)\quad\textrm{if and only if}\quad E_{p,\lambda}(\omega)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }I_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}(p),\quad\omega\in K_{\mu,\nu}(p).

It turns out that the maximizer
\omega
satisfies

\omega=\lambda f(\psi-Wx_{2}-\gamma)

for some vorticity function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with constants
W>0
(traveling speed) and
\gamma\geq 0
(flux).

Before proceeding, we record some notational conventions for the remainder of this section.
We omit the explicit dependence on
p\in(4/3,\infty)
in the notation for the admissible class, maximal energy, and set of maximizers.
That is, we write

K_{\mu,\nu}(p)=K_{\mu,\nu},\quad I_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}(p)=I_{\mu,\nu,\lambda},\quad\text{and}\quad S_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}(p)=S_{\mu,\nu,\lambda}.

We also omit the dependence on
p
in the constants appearing in inequalities, unless ambiguity arises.
However, we continue to include the subscript
p
in the penalized energy functional
E_{p}(\omega)
, for notational clarity, even though other objects considered here also depend on
p
.

Remark 4.1
.

We reduce the problem (
4.2
) to the case
\nu=\lambda=1
via a scaling

(4.3)

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}({\bf x})=\frac{1}{\lambda\nu^{1/(p-1)}}\omega\left(\frac{\nu^{(p-2)/(2p-2)}{\bf x}}{\lambda^{1/2}}\right).

If
\omega\in K_{\mu,\nu}
,
\hat{\omega}\in K_{M,1}
for
M=\mu\nu^{-(p-2)/(2p-2)-1}\lambda^{1/2}
and
E_{p,1}(\hat{\omega})=\nu^{-2}E_{p,\lambda}(\omega)
.
When
\nu=\lambda=1
, we abbreviate the notation as
K_{\mu}=K_{\mu,1}
,
I_{\mu}=I_{\mu,1,1}
,
E_{p}(\omega)=E_{p,1}(\omega)
, and
S_{\mu}=S_{\mu,1,1}
.

Theorem 4.2

(Existence and stablity of energy-maximizers)
.

For each
p>4/3
and
\mu\in(0,\infty)
, the set
S_{\mu}=S_{\mu}(p)
of maximizers is nonempty, and every maximizer satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition up to a
x_{1}
-translation.
In addition, each
\omega\in S_{\mu}
is given recursively by

\omega=\left(\mathcal{G}\left[\omega\right]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)},

where
\mathcal{G}[\omega]=(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}})^{-1}\omega
is the stream function and
W>0
,
\gamma\geq 0
are constants depending on
\omega
.

Furthermore,
S_{\mu}
is stable in the following sense:
For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta=\delta(p,\mu,\varepsilon)>0
such that for any initial data
0\leq\theta_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
satisfying
\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}\leq 1+\delta
and

\displaystyle\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\left\{\left\|\theta_{0}-\omega\right\|_{p}+\left\|x_{2}\left(\theta_{0}-\omega\right)\right\|_{1}\right\}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\delta,

the unique global-in-time weak solution
\theta(t)
to (
1.3
) with
\theta(0)=\theta_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\left\{\left\|\theta\left(t,\cdot\right)-\omega\right\|_{p}+\left\|x_{2}\left(\theta\left(t,\cdot\right)-\omega\right)\right\|_{1}\right\}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon

for all
t\geq 0
.

We note that Theorem
4.2
above establishes the existence (and stability) of traveling waves in the form

\omega=\left(\mathcal{G}\left[\omega\right]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}.

for each
p>4/3
, as an energy-maximizer in the admissible class
K_{\mu}=K_{\mu}(p)
. It leads to the existence of Sadovskii vortices that lie in
C^{2,\alpha}
, for each
0<\alpha<1
.

Theorem 4.3

(Regular Sadovskii vortices as energy-maximizers)
.

There exists a constant
\widetilde{\mu}>0
such that, for any
\mu\in(0,\widetilde{\mu})
, the following holds:

For each
\alpha\in(0,1)
, by fixing
p=1+1/(2+\alpha)>4/3
, each maximizer
\omega
in the set
S_{\mu}=S_{\mu}(p)
satisfies

\omega=\left(\mathcal{G}\left[\omega\right]-Wx_{2}\right)_{+}^{2+\alpha},

and its odd-extension
\widetilde{\omega}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
in the
x_{2}
-variable is a Sadovskii vortex belonging to
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, defined by

\widetilde{\omega}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\begin{cases}\,\omega(x_{1},x_{2}),&\text{if }x_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\;x_{2}>0\\
\,-\omega(x_{1},-x_{2}),&\text{if }x_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\;x_{2}<0,\\
\,0&\text{if }x_{2}=0.\end{cases}

In short, the above
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
Sadovskii vortex arises as a maximizer of the variational problem (
4.2
) for some
p=p(\alpha)>4/3
and small
\mu>0
.
We will later see that the smallness of
\mu>0
forces the flux constant in Theorem
4.2
to vanish,
i.e.
,
\gamma=0
, which yields the
touching of dipole
structure (as discussed in Subsection
1.3
).
Moreover, choosing
p=1+1/(2+\alpha)>4/3
ensures
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity of each maximizer for
0<\alpha<1
.

4.2.
Existence of penalized energy maximizers

We begin by establishing the existence of maximizers using a standard variational approach,
e.g.

[
60
,
1
]
. Recall that

K_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left\{\omega\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\ \middle|\ \omega\geq 0,\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=\mu,\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\leq 1\ \right\}.

and

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\sup_{\omega\in K_{\mu}}E_{p}(\omega),\qquad E_{p}(\omega)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E(\omega)-\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\quad(\omega\in K_{\mu}).

From the definition of
I_{\mu}
, it is clear that
I_{\mu}=0
if
\mu=0
.
As a preliminary step in the variational approach, we first establish the monotonicity of the energy supremum
I_{\mu}
with respect to the parameter
\mu>0
.

Lemma 4.4
.

I_{\mu}
is a strictly increasing positive function of
\mu
on
(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

We first prove that
0<I_{\mu}<\infty
for
\mu\in(0,\infty)
, that is,
I_{\mu}
is a well-defined positive function.
From (
2.6
) of Proposition
2.2
with
\|\omega\|_{1}\leq 1
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle E_{p}(\omega)\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C||x_{2}\omega||_{1}^{1-1/p}||\omega||_{p}^{1/2}-\frac{1}{p}||\omega||_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\left(\varepsilon^{-1/p}||x_{2}\omega||_{1}^{1-1/p}\right)^{2p/(2p-1)}+\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2p}-\frac{1}{p}\right)||\omega||_{p}^{p}.

If we choose
\varepsilon
sufficiently small,

E_{p}(\omega)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\mu^{(2p-2)/(2p-1)}

and this implies
I_{\mu}<\infty
.

To verify the strict positivity
I_{\mu}>0
, we fix a positive function
\omega_{1}
satisfying
\|x_{2}\omega_{1}\|_{1}=\mu
.
Then by a scaling
\omega_{\sigma}({\bf x})=\sigma^{3}\omega_{1}(\sigma{\bf x})
with
\sigma>0
, we see that
\|x_{2}\omega_{\sigma}\|_{1}=\|x_{2}\omega_{1}\|_{1}=\mu
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega_{\sigma}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega_{1}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}<1,

that is,
\omega_{\sigma}\in K_{\mu}
for sufficiently small
\sigma<1
.
Moreover, if we further reduce
\sigma
, then

E_{p}(\omega_{\sigma})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\sigma^{2}\left(E(\omega_{1})-\frac{\sigma^{3p-4}}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega^{p}_{1}\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }0

since
p>4/3
, and this means that
I_{\mu}\geq E_{p}[\omega_{\sigma}]>0
.

For the last, we show
I_{\alpha}<I_{\mu}
for
0<\alpha<\mu
,
i.e.
, the strict monotonicity.
It is enough to prove
I_{\tau\alpha}>I_{\alpha}
for
\alpha>0
and
\tau>1
since the case
\tau=\mu/\alpha
provides
I_{\alpha}<I_{\mu}
for
0<\alpha<\mu
.
For given
\omega\in K_{\alpha}
, we rescale it by
\omega_{\tau}({\bf x})=\tau^{-2}\omega(\tau^{-1}{\bf x})
with
\tau=\mu/\alpha>1
.
By direct calculation, one may see that
\omega_{\tau}\in K_{\mu}
;
\|x_{2}\omega_{\tau}\|_{1}=\tau\|x_{2}\omega\|_{1}=\mu

and
\|\omega_{\tau}\|_{1}=\|\omega\|_{1}\leq 1
.
In particular,
\omega_{\tau}
satisfies

(4.4)

\displaystyle I_{\mu}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }E(\omega)-\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}-\frac{1}{p\tau^{2p-2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega)+\frac{1}{p}\left\{1-\left(\frac{\alpha}{\mu}\right)^{2p-2}\right\}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle>\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega)

due to the relation
\mu>\alpha
.
Thus we get

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }I_{\alpha}.

Now, suppose
I_{\mu}=I_{\alpha}
and take a maximizing sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}\subset K_{\alpha}
such that
E_{p}(\omega_{n})\to I_{\alpha}
.
Then from the inequality (
4.4
), it must be

\frac{1}{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{\tau^{2p-2}}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega_{n}^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0,\quad\textrm{that is},\quad\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{in}\quad L^{p}.

By (
2.6
) of Proposition
2.2
again, it follows that
E(\omega_{n})\to 0
and

0\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }I_{\alpha}=\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{p}[\omega_{n}]\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})-\frac{1}{p}\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0,

which is a contradiction. Therefore,
I_{\mu}>I_{\alpha}
if
\mu>\alpha
. The lemma is proved.
∎

In order to control maximizing sequences, we establish an
a priori

L^{p}
bound.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\{\omega_{n}\}
be a sequence such that
\omega_{n}\in K_{\mu_{n}}
,
\mu_{n}\to\mu
and
E_{p}[\omega_{n}]\to I_{\mu}>0
.
Then
\{\omega_{n}\}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{p}
.
In particular, we have

(4.5)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}||\omega_{n}||_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\mu^{(2p-2)/(2p-1)}

for some definite constant
C>0
.

Proof.

By (
2.6
) of Proposition
2.2
and Young’s inequality, we have that for
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\mu_{n}^{1-1/p}\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}^{1/2}-E_{p}(\omega_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon^{-1/(2p-1)}\mu_{n}^{(2p-2)/(2p-1)}+\varepsilon||\omega_{n}||_{p}^{p}-E_{p}(\omega_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon^{-1/(2p-1)}\mu_{n}^{(2p-2)/(2p-1)}+\varepsilon||\omega_{n}||_{p}^{p}

for sufficiently large
n
since
I_{\mu}>0
and
E(\omega_{n})\to I_{\mu}
.
Thus, by taking
\varepsilon=1/(2p)

and absorbing the last term to the left-hand side, the sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}
satisfies

\limsup_{n\to\infty}||\omega_{n}||_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon^{-1/(2p-1)}\mu^{(2p-2)/(2p-1)}.

We obtain the desired result.
∎

The following estimates for stream function
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
will be used to prove the convergence
E(\omega_{n})\to E(\omega)
for some maximizing sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}
in Lemma
4.8
.
The corresponding results for the case of
p=2
can be found in
[
1
, Propositions 3.3 and 3.4]
, and the proof is parallel, but we introduce the detailed proof for completeness.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\omega\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
be a non-negative function with
x_{2}\omega\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
.
Suppose that
\omega
satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition in Definition
1.2
, and we fix
A\geq 2
and
R>0
.
For the stream function
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
of
\omega
(see (
4.1
)), the following holds:

(1)

for
x_{2}\leq 2|x_{1}|/A
,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\left(\left(\frac{x_{2}}{A}\right)^{\theta}||\omega||_{1}^{\theta}||\omega||_{p}^{1-\theta}+\frac{1}{A}||\omega||_{1}+x_{2}\left(\frac{A}{x_{1}}\right)^{2}||x_{2}\omega||_{1}\right),

(2)

for
Q(A,R)=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\ |\ |x_{1}|<AR,\ x_{2}<R\}
,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\backslash Q}\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{C}{\min\{A^{\theta},R^{1/p}\}}\left(||\omega||_{1}+||\omega||_{p}+||x_{2}\omega||_{1}\right)^{2},

where
\theta=(2-2/p)/(3-2/p)\in(0,1)
and
C=C(p)>0
.

Proof.

(1) Owing to the Steiner symmetry assumption, it suffices to consider the case
x_{1}>0
.
For any
A\geq 2
, we see that

(4.8)

\displaystyle\int_{|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{x_{1}-x_{1}/A}^{x_{1}+x_{1}/A}\|\omega(y_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{1-1/A}^{1+1/A}x_{1}\|\omega(x_{1}s,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}s

(4.9)

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\left(1+\frac{1}{A-1}\right)\int_{1-1/A}^{1+1/A}x_{1}s\cdot\|\omega(x_{1}s,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}s

(4.10)

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\left(1+\frac{1}{A-1}\right)\frac{2}{A}\|\omega\|_{1}\leq\frac{4}{A}\|\omega\|_{1},

where we use the fact that

x_{1}s\|\omega(x_{1}s,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{0}^{x_{1}s}\|\omega(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\textrm{d}x\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{1}

since the function
y_{1}\mapsto\|\omega(y_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}
is non-increasing for
y_{1}\geq 0
.

To prove the pointwise estimate (
4.6
), we decompose the stream function
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
into two parts:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<x_{2}/2}+\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq x_{2}/2}\mbox{ }=:\mbox{ }\mathcal{G}_{1}+\mathcal{G}_{2}.

Since the assumption
x_{2}\leq 2x_{1}/A
and the condition
|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<x_{2}/2
together imply
|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A
, we apply Hölder’s inequality with exponents satisfying
1/q=\theta+(1-\theta)/p
and
1/q+1/q^{\prime}=1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}({\bf x})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<x_{2}/2,\\
|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A\end{subarray}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\left(\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<x_{2}/2}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{q^{\prime}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right)^{1/q^{\prime}}\left(\int_{|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A}\omega^{q}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{2/q^{\prime}}||\omega||_{L^{1}(|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A)}^{\theta}||\omega||_{L^{p}(|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A)}^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{2(1-\theta)(1-1/p)}||\omega||_{L^{1}(|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A)}^{\theta}||\omega||_{L^{p}(|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A)}^{1-\theta}.

In the second inequality, we have used (
2.2
) of Lemma
2.1
with
\alpha=x_{2}/2
.
Since
\theta=(2-2/p)/(3-2/p)=2(1-\theta)(1-1/p)
, we have

\mathcal{G}_{1}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C(x_{2}/A)^{\theta}||\omega||_{1}^{\theta}||\omega||_{p}^{1-\theta}.

Next, we estimate the second part
\mathcal{G}_{2}({\bf x})
by further splitting the domain of integration according to the horizontal distance:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq x_{2}/2,\\
|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A\end{subarray}}+\int\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq x_{2}/2,\\
|x_{1}-y_{1}|\geq x_{1}/A\end{subarray}}\mbox{ }=:\mbox{ }\mathcal{G}_{2,1}+\mathcal{G}_{2,2}.

For points
{\bf x}
and
{\bf y}
satisfying
|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq x_{2}/2
, we observe that

\frac{x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2\frac{y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|}.

We now fix any constant
m\geq 1
.
If
y_{2}\leq mx_{2}
,

\frac{y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }m\frac{x_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2m.

On the other hand, if
y_{2}>mx_{2}
, then

y_{2}(m-1)=my_{2}-y_{2}<my_{2}-mx_{2}=m(y_{2}-x_{2}),

which implies

\frac{y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\frac{y_{2}}{y_{2}-x_{2}}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\frac{m}{m-1}.

By choosing
m=3/2
, we deduce the uniform bound

\frac{x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2\frac{y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }6

whenever
|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq x_{2}/2
.

Combining this with the upper bound
G({\bf x},{\bf y})\leq x_{2}y_{2}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{-2}
, we estimate the first term as follows:

\mathcal{G}_{2,1}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq x_{2}/2,\\
|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }6\int_{|x_{1}-y_{1}|<x_{1}/A}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{24}{A}||\omega||_{1},

where the last inequality is due to (
4.8
).
On the other hand, we see that

\mathcal{G}_{2,2}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq x_{2}/2,\\
|x_{1}-y_{1}|\geq x_{1}/A\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq x_{2}/2,\\
|x_{1}-y_{1}|\geq x_{1}/A\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{2}y_{2}}{|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{2}}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }x_{2}\left(\frac{A}{x_{1}}\right)^{2}||y_{2}\omega||_{1}.

Putting together the estimates for
\mathcal{G}_{1}
,
\mathcal{G}_{2,1}
, and
\mathcal{G}_{2,2}
yields (
4.6
).

(2) We now prove the integral estimate (
4.7
).
We decompose the domain of integration outside the set
Q(A,R)
into two parts:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\backslash Q}\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{x_{2}\geq R}+\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{2}<R,\\
|x_{1}|\geq AR\end{subarray}}.

We first estimate the contribution from the region
x_{2}\geq R
.
By applying inequality (
2.4
) of Proposition
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{x_{2}\geq R}\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C||\omega||_{1}^{1/2}||\omega||_{p}^{1/2}\int_{x_{2}\geq R}x_{2}^{1-1/p}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}x\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{C}{R^{1/p}}\left(||\omega||_{1}^{2}+||\omega||_{p}^{2}+||x_{2}\omega||_{1}^{2}\right).

Next, we consider the region where
|x_{1}|\geq AR
and
x_{2}<R
.
Since
x_{2}\leq|x_{1}|/A
holds in this case, applying the pointwise bound (
4.6
) yields

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{2}<R,\\
|x_{1}|\geq AR\end{subarray}}\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\int\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{2}<R,\\
|x_{1}|\geq AR\end{subarray}}\left(\left(\frac{x_{2}}{A}\right)^{\theta}||\omega||_{L^{p}\cap L^{1}}+\frac{1}{A}||\omega||_{1}+x_{2}\frac{1}{R^{2}}||x_{2}\omega||_{1}\right)\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{C}{\min\{A^{\theta},R^{1/p}\}}\left(||\omega||_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}}^{2-\theta}||x_{2}\omega||_{1}^{\theta}+||\omega||_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}}^{2}+||x_{2}\omega||_{1}^{2}\right),

where
\theta=(2-2/p)/(3-2/p)
.
Therefore, (
4.7
) follows from the Young’s inequality. The lemma is proved.
∎

4.2.1.
Proof of existence

The existence of maximizers is established below, together with a description of their symmetry.

Proposition 4.7

(Existence of maximizer)
.

There exists
\omega\in K_{\mu}
such that
E_{p}(\omega)=I_{\mu}
, i.e.,
S_{\mu}\neq\emptyset
.
Moreover, every maximizer
\omega\in S_{\mu}
, up to a translation in the
x_{1}
-direction, satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition in Definition
1.2
.

Before proceeding to the proof of Proposition
4.7
, we need to establish several lemmas.
A key step is to ensure that the energy behaves well under weak convergence, which is achieved through the Steiner symmetry condition as demonstrated in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\{\omega_{n}\geq 0\}
be a sequence of non-negative functions in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
satisfying

\sup_{n}\mbox{ }\left\{\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}+\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}+\|x_{2}\omega_{n}\|_{1}\right\}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\infty.

If
\omega_{n}
satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition and
\omega_{n}\rightharpoonup\omega
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
as
n\to\infty
, then

E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }E(\omega)\quad\textrm{as}\ n\to\infty.

Proof.

Take
Q=Q(A,R)
from Proposition
4.6
.
Note that

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\int_{Q}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{Q}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}-\int_{Q}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\backslash Q}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }\int_{Q}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\backslash Q}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x},

where the last inequality is due to the symmetry
G({\bf x},{\bf y})=G({\bf y},{\bf x})
and
\omega_{n}\geq 0
.
Then, since

\displaystyle 2E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{Q}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\backslash Q},

(
4.7
) implies

\displaystyle\left|2E(\omega_{n})-\int_{Q}\int_{Q}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }2E(\omega_{n})-\int_{Q}\int_{Q}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\backslash Q}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}x\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{C}{\min\{A^{\theta},R^{1/p}\}}

with
\theta=(2-2/p)/(3-2/p)
.
By the same calculation for
E(\omega)
, we reach

\displaystyle 2\left|E(\omega_{n})-E(\omega)\right|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\left|\int_{Q}\int_{Q}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\left(\omega({\bf x})\omega({\bf y})-\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right|+\frac{C}{\min\{A^{\theta},R^{1/p}\}}.

Since
G({\bf x},{\bf y})\in L^{p^{\prime}}(Q\times Q)
(
1/p^{\prime}=1-1/p
) and
\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\rightharpoonup\omega({\bf x})\omega({\bf y})
in
L^{p}(Q\times Q)
, sending
n\to\infty
and
A,R\to\infty
imply the convergence
E(\omega_{n})\to E(\omega)
.
The lemma is proved.
∎

To prove Proposition
4.7
, we introduce an enlarged admissible class
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}\supset K_{\mu}
for each
\mu>0
, defined by

(4.11)

\widetilde{K}_{\mu}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\left\{\omega\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\ \middle|\ \omega\geq 0,\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\leq\mu,\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\leq 1\ \right\},

where the class
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
relaxes the impulse constraint from an equality to an inequality.
We also define a corresponding maximizing problem

\widetilde{I}_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\sup_{\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}}E_{p}(\omega).

The following lemma asserts that the relaxed problem
\widetilde{I}_{\mu}
admits a maximizer satisfying the desired symmetry.

Lemma 4.9
.

There exists
\omega\in\widetilde{K}{\mu}
such that
E_{p}(\omega)=\widetilde{I}\mu
.
Moreover, up to a translation in the
x_{1}
-direction, every such
\omega
satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition in Definition
1.2
.

Proof.

Let
\{\omega_{n}\}\subset\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
be a maximizing sequence of
E_{p}
,
i.e.
,

E_{p}(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }\sup_{\eta\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}}E_{p}(\eta)=\widetilde{I}_{\mu}

as
n\to\infty
.
By Lemma
2.3
and Lemma
4.5
, we may assume that
\omega_{n}
satisfies the Steiner symmetry, and
\{\omega_{n}\}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{p}
.
It means that there is a subsequence, still denoted by
\{\omega_{n}\}
, such that
\omega_{n}\rightharpoonup\omega
in
L^{p}
and

||\omega||_{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}||\omega_{n}||_{p}

for some
\omega\in L^{p}
.
We claim that
\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
.
It is clear that
\omega\geq 0
.
Take a sufficiently smooth and compactly support function
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
.
Since
\omega_{n}\rightharpoonup\omega
in
L^{p}
, by the property of weak convergence, it holds that

\omega_{n}\,\phi\mbox{ }\rightharpoonup\mbox{ }\omega\,\phi\quad\textrm{and}\quad x_{2}\omega_{n}\,\phi\mbox{ }\rightharpoonup\mbox{ }x_{2}\omega\,\phi\quad\textrm{in}\quad L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}).

Thus, we have

||\omega\cdot\phi||_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}||\omega_{n}\cdot\phi||_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1

and

||x_{2}\omega\cdot\phi||_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}||x_{2}\omega_{n}\cdot\phi||_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\mu

for any
\phi
, and then, monotone convergence theorem implies
\|\omega\|_{1}\leq 1
and
\|x_{2}\omega\|_{1}\leq\mu
.

On the other hand, since
\{\omega_{n}\}
satisfies the assumption of Lemma
4.8
, we see that

\sup_{\eta\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}}E_{p}(\eta)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{p}(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})-\frac{1}{p}\liminf_{n\to\infty}||\omega_{n}||_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E(\omega)-\frac{1}{p}||\omega||_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega).

Thus
\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
is a maximizer.
It is clear that
\omega
satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition, i.e, (i)
\omega_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})=\omega_{0}(-x_{1},x_{2})
and (ii)
\omega_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})
is non-increasing in
x_{1}\geq 0
, for each
x_{2}>0
.

It remains to show that every such maximizer
\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
has the Steiner symmetry, up to a translation in the
x_{1}
-direction.
We note that the Steiner symmetrization
\omega^{*}\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
(as introduced in Lemma
2.3
) of a given
\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
attains strictly larger energy,

E(\omega^{*})>E(\omega),

unless
\omega
already possesses Steiner symmetry up to translation.
See, for instance,
[
84
, Theorem 3.9]
.
This completes the proof.
∎

With the existence of a maximizer in the relaxed class
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
established, we now turn to the proof of Proposition
4.7
, which shows that the maximizer obtained in Lemma
4.9
in fact belongs to the original admissible class
K_{\mu}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.7
.

Let
\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
be the one in Lemma
4.9
, that is,
\omega
satisfies
E_{p}(\omega)=\widetilde{I}_{\mu}
and the Steiner symmetry condition.
To prove the existence of maximizer in
K_{\mu}
, we show
\|x_{2}\omega\|_{1}=\mu
by a contradiction argument.
Suppose
\|x_{2}\omega\|_{1}<\mu
.
We set
\omega_{\delta}(x):=\omega(x_{1},x_{2}-\delta)
for some
\delta>0
.
Observe that

\displaystyle E(\omega_{\delta})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\omega(x_{1},x_{2}-\delta)\omega(y_{1},y_{2}-\delta)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\log\left(1+\frac{4(x_{2}+\delta)(y_{2}+\delta)}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\omega({\bf x})\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}y

\displaystyle>\mbox{ }\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\omega({\bf x})\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E(\omega)

provided that
\omega\nequiv 0
, and it is clear that
\|\omega_{\delta}\|_{p}=\|\omega\|_{p}
.
Thus for sufficiently small
\delta>0
, we are led that
\omega_{\delta}\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
and

E_{p}(\omega_{\delta})\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega),

which is a contradiction to the fact that
\omega
is a maximizer in
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
.
Hence,
\|x_{2}\omega\|_{1}=\mu
and the lemma is proved.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

In the proof of Proposition
4.7
, we verify that

(4.12)

\widetilde{I}_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }I_{\mu},

that is, every maximizer
\omega
in the admissible set
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
must attain the prescribed impulse constraint:
\|x_{2}\omega\|_{1}=\mu
.

We conclude this subsection with a central structural property of maximizers by showing that their stream functions solve semilinear elliptic equations.
Beyond its interpretation as traveling wave formulations, it will be instrumental in establishing the regularity and touching properties in the following sections.

Proposition 4.11
.

For
\mu>0
and for each maximizer
\omega\in S_{\mu}
of
E_{p}(\omega)
, there exist non-negative constants
W
and
\gamma
satisfying

(4.13)

\omega\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\Big(\mathcal{G}\left[\omega\right]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\Big)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}

for almost every
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, where
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
is the stream function of
\omega
defined by (
4.1
).
Moreover, such
W
and
\gamma
are uniquely determined by the maximizer
\omega
.

Proof.

We follow the standard argument in
[
60
]
.
See
[
1
]
, also.
We set a domain (which is a Banach space)

D\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\{\zeta\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}):\zeta,\,\,x_{2}\zeta\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\}.

equipped with a finite norm
\|\zeta\|_{D}:=\|\zeta\|_{1}+\|\zeta\|_{p}+\|x_{2}\zeta\|_{1}
.
We claim that for each
\omega,\eta\in D
, a functional derivative
\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}\varepsilon}E_{p}[\omega+\varepsilon\eta]
is well-defined for
\varepsilon\in[0,\infty)

and satisfies

F_{\omega}(\eta)\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}\varepsilon}E_{p}(\omega+\varepsilon\eta)\Bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-\omega^{p-1}\right)\eta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

and then

\left|F_{\omega}(\eta)\right|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\|\omega\|_{D}\|\eta\|_{D}

for some
C>0
.
To show this, first note that by (
2.5
) and (
2.7
) in Proposition
2.2
, the kinetic energy
E(\omega)
is a continuous functional defined on
D
.
Clearly, the same also holds true for the penalized energy

E_{p}(\omega)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E(\omega)-\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}.

Then, a straightforward calculation

\displaystyle\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}\varepsilon}E_{p}(\omega+\varepsilon\eta)\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}\varepsilon}\left[E(\omega)+\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G(x,y)\omega({\bf x})\eta({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}+\varepsilon^{2}E(\eta)-\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}(\omega+\varepsilon\eta)^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right]

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\eta({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}+2\varepsilon E(\eta)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}(\omega+\varepsilon\eta)^{p-1}\eta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

implies the claim.
Here, regarding the last term, we use (
2.5
) of Proposition
2.2
and an algebraic inequality for
p\geq 1

\left|\left|a\right|^{p}-\left|b\right|^{p}\right|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }p\left(|a|^{p-1}+|b|^{p-1}\right)|a-b|,\quad a,b\in\mathbb{R}

to justify limit calculations.
We fix an arbitrary
\omega\in S_{\mu}\subset D
.
For a given constant
\delta_{0}>0
satisfying

\left|\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\ :\ \omega({\bf x})\geq\delta_{0}\}\right|>0,

we fix compactly supported functions
h_{1},h_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
such that

\operatorname{supp}h_{i}\subset\{\omega\geq\delta_{0}\},\quad i=1,2,

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}h_{1}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}h_{2}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }1,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}h_{2}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}h_{1}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0.

Indeed, such functions can be constructed using linear combinations of characteristic functions supported on appropriate subsets of the set
\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\ :\ \omega({\bf x})\geq\delta_{0}\}
.
For example, since
|\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\ :\ \omega({\bf x})\geq\delta_{0}\}|>0
, there exist constants
0<a<b
such that the following holds: for any sufficiently small
\ell>0
, the sets

A:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\ :\ \omega({\bf x})\geq\delta_{0},\mbox{ }a+\ell>x_{2}\geq a\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad B:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\ :\ \omega({\bf x})\geq\delta_{0},\mbox{ }b+\ell>x_{2}\geq b\}

have positive measures.
Moreover, we may choose bounded subsets
A^{\prime}\subset A
and
B^{\prime}\subset B
with
|A^{\prime}|,|B^{\prime}|>0
.
For
\alpha,\beta>0
, define

h^{(\ell)}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\alpha\cdot\mathbf{1}_{A^{\prime}}-\beta\cdot\mathbf{1}_{B^{\prime}}.

Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}h^{(\ell)}({\bf x})\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\alpha\int_{A^{\prime}}x_{2}\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}-\beta\int_{B^{\prime}}x_{2}\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}\quad(=:\alpha m_{1}-\beta m_{2})

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }0

provided that
\alpha/\beta=m_{2}/m_{1}
.
If
\int h^{(\ell)}\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0
also holds, then we must have
m_{2}/m_{1}=\alpha/\beta=|B^{\prime}|/|A^{\prime}|
.
However, since
0<a<b
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{|A^{\prime}|}\mbox{ }<\frac{b^{-1}\int_{B^{\prime}}x_{2}\textrm{d}{\bf x}}{(a+\ell)^{-1}\int_{A^{\prime}}x_{2}\textrm{d}{\bf x}}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{a+\ell}{b}\cdot\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}\mbox{ },

and it means that for small enough
\ell>0
, we always have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}h^{(\ell)}({\bf x})\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}\neq 0.

Hence, the function

h_{1}({\bf x}):=\frac{h^{(\ell)}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}h^{(\ell)}\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}}

satisfies all the required properties by choosing
\ell>0
sufficiently small.
In a similar manner, one can also construct
h_{2}
.

Given such fixed
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
, and
\delta_{0}>0
, we now consider an arbitrary
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
and any function
h\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
having compact support such that

h({\bf x})\geq 0\quad\textrm{on}\quad\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:0\leq\omega({\bf x})\leq\delta\}.

By setting

(4.14)

\displaystyle\eta\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }h-\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}h\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)h_{1}-\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}h\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)h_{2}\in L^{p},

Also, using the properties of
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
and
h
, we show that
\omega+\varepsilon\eta\in K_{\mu}
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, as follows.
We first verify
\omega+\varepsilon\eta\geq 0
.
Observe that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\,\omega+\varepsilon\eta=\omega+\varepsilon h\geq 0\quad&\textrm{on}&\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\{0\leq\omega\leq\delta\},\\
&\,\omega+\varepsilon\eta>\delta-\varepsilon\|\eta\|_{\infty}\geq 0\quad&\textrm{on}&\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\{\omega>\delta\}\end{aligned}\right.

provided that
\varepsilon>0
is suffciently small.
Then, we compute

\|\omega+\varepsilon\eta\|_{1}=\int\omega+\varepsilon\eta\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}=\int\omega\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}\leq 1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|x_{2}(\omega+\varepsilon\eta)\|_{1}=\int x_{2}(\omega+\varepsilon\eta)\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}=\int x_{2}\omega\,\textrm{d}{\bf x}=\mu,

where we used the identities
\int\eta=\int x_{2}\eta=0
.
Therefore, we conclude that
\omega+\varepsilon\eta\in K_{\mu}
.

Then, since
\omega
is a maximizer and
F_{\omega}(\cdot)
is linear,

\displaystyle 0\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }F_{\omega}\left(\eta\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }F_{\omega}(h)-F_{\omega}(h_{1})\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}h\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)\mbox{ }-\mbox{ }F_{\omega}(h_{2})\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}h\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right).

Here we take
\gamma=F_{\omega}(h_{1})
and
W=F_{\omega}(h_{2})
, and then we rewrite the above inequality by

(4.15)

\displaystyle 0\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }F_{\omega}(h)-\gamma\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}h\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)-W\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}h\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma-\omega^{p-1}\right)h\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{0\leq\omega\leq\delta}+\int_{\omega>\delta}.

Since
h
is an arbitrary function satisfying
h\geq 0
on
\{0\leq\omega\leq\delta\}
and
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
is also an arbitrary number, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma-\omega^{p-1}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{on}\,\,\{\omega>0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{on}\,\,\{\omega=0\}.

This implies (
4.13
).

We now show the non-negativity of
W
and
\gamma
.
Due to the integrability of
\omega
and the continuity from relation (
4.13
), there exists a sequence of points
{\bf x}_{n}=(a_{n},b_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
such that
0<a_{n}\nearrow\infty
,
0<b_{n}\searrow 0
, and
\omega({\bf x}_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Then since
\mathcal{G}[\omega]\geq 0
,

\gamma\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]\left({\bf x}_{n}\right)-Wb_{n}\right)\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }0.

Similarly, for
{\bf y}_{n}=(b_{n},a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, we see that
\omega({\bf x}_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
and

W\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x}_{n})}{a_{n}}-\frac{\gamma}{a_{n}}\right)\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }0.

Finally, we prove the uniqueness of
W
and
\gamma
.
Suppose there are constants
\overline{W},\bar{\gamma}\geq 0
satisfying (
4.13
) for
\omega\in S_{\mu}
.
By following the above argument, it should be satisfied that

0\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }F_{\omega}(h)-\bar{\gamma}\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}h\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)-\overline{W}\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}h\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)

for any compactly supported
h\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
satisfying
h\geq 0
on
\{0\leq\omega\leq\delta\}
.
Then by taking
h=\pm h_{1},\pm h_{2}
, it follows that
\gamma=F_{\omega}(h_{1})=\bar{\gamma}
and
W=F_{\omega}(h_{2})=\overline{W}
. The proposition is proved.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

As noted in
[
47
]
, by using the fact that the each maximizer
\omega\in K_{\mu}
produces a traveling wave solution to (
1.3
) with traveling speed
W
, one obtain the identity

(4.16)

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{1}\cdot W\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{x_{2}+y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}^{*}|^{2}}\omega({\bf x})\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\bf y}^{*}=(y_{1},-y_{2}),

using integration by parts.

4.3.
Regularity of maximizers

In what follows, we consider the odd extension
\psi=\psi[\omega]
of the stream function
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
from
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
to the entire plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
From (
4.13
), we know that
\psi({\bf x})
satisfies the following elliptic equation:

(4.17)

-\Delta\psi({\bf x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}f\left(\psi\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\omega(x_{1},x_{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }0,\\
-f\left(-\left(\psi\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)-Wx_{2}\right)-\gamma\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }-\omega(x_{1},-x_{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }0,\end{array}\right.

where
f(s)=s_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
with
p>4/3
.
Note that the right-hand side reflects the odd symmetry across the
x_{1}
-axis.

Lemma 4.13
.

For each
\omega\in S_{\mu}
, we have
\psi\in C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
\beta=\beta(p)>0
.
In particular, there exists a constant
C=C\bigl(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\bigr)>0
such that

(4.18)

\|\psi\|_{C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C.

Proof.

(Case 1:
p<2
)

We first consider the case
p<2
, which corresponds to
1/(p-1)>1
.
In this regime, the regularity of
f(s)=s_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
has regularity higher than Lipschitz.
We fix any
q\in(1,2)
.
Since
\omega\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
, the Calderón-Zygmund theory implies that
\partial_{\bf x}^{2}\psi\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\omega
satisfies the estimate

(4.19)

\|\partial_{\bf x}^{2}\psi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}),

where we define
|\partial_{{\bf x}}\psi|=|\partial_{x_{1}}\psi|+|\partial_{x_{2}}\psi|
.
Since
q\in(1,2)
, the Sobolev embedding theorem yields

(4.20)

\|\partial_{\bf x}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})})\quad\textrm{for}\quad q^{*}=\frac{2q}{2-q}>2,\quad\textit{i.e.},\,\,\frac{1}{q^{*}}=\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2},

and by applying the embedding theorem again, we obtain

(4.21)

\|\psi\|_{C^{0,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})})\quad\textrm{for}\quad\beta=1-\frac{2}{q^{*}}\in(0,1).

(
Claim 1
)   For any
{\bf x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have

(4.22)

\left\|\partial_{x_{1}}\left(\partial_{\bf x}^{2}\psi\right)\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }\leq\|\partial_{\bf x}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}).

Fix an arbitrary point
{\bf x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Consider the finite difference quotient with respect to
x_{1}
-variable

D_{h}\psi({\bf x})\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\frac{\psi(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-\psi(x_{1},x_{2})}{h}\quad(h\neq 0)

which satisfies

-\Delta(D_{h}\psi)({\bf x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}f_{h}^{1}({\bf x})&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}\geq 0,\\
f_{h}^{2}({\bf x})&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}<0,\end{array}\right.

where the functions
f_{h}^{1}
and
f_{h}^{2}
are given by

f_{h}^{1}({\bf x})\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\frac{f(\psi(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-Wx_{2}-\gamma)-f(\psi(x_{1},x_{2})-Wx_{2}-\gamma)}{h}

and

f_{h}^{2}({\bf x})\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }-\frac{f(-\psi(x_{1}+h,x_{2})+Wx_{2}-\gamma)-f(-\psi(x_{1},x_{2})+Wx_{2}-\gamma)}{h}.

Then, by the classical interior
L^{p}
estimates from Sobolev space theory (see
[
64
, Theorem 9.13]
or
[
54
, Lemma 1]
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{\bf x}^{2}\left(D_{h}\psi\right)\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }\lesssim_{p}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\left\|f_{h}^{1}\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\left\|f_{h}^{2}\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-})}+\left\|D_{h}\psi\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}.

Observe that, due to the Lipschitz continuity of
f
and the boundedness of
\psi
(recall that
\psi\in C^{0,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
)

\displaystyle\left|f_{h}^{1}\right|\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\left|\frac{f(\psi(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-Wx_{2}-\gamma)-f(\psi({\bf x})-Wx_{2}-\gamma)}{h}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{ }\left|\frac{f(\psi(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-Wx_{2}-\gamma)-f(\psi({\bf x})-Wx_{2}-\gamma)}{\psi(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-\psi({\bf x})}\right|\cdot\left|\frac{\psi(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-\psi({\bf x})}{h}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mbox{ }\left|\frac{\psi(x_{1}+h,x_{2})-\psi({\bf x})}{h}\right|.

Hence,

\left\|f_{h}^{1}\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\mbox{ }\lesssim_{p}\mbox{ }\left\|D_{h}\psi\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}.

Similarly,

\left\|f_{h}^{2}\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-})}\lesssim_{p}\mbox{ }\left\|\mbox{ }D_{h}\psi\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}.

Therefore, we deduce that

\left\|D_{h}\left(\partial_{\bf x}^{2}\psi\right)\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left\|\partial_{\bf x}^{2}\left(D_{h}\psi\right)\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }\lesssim_{p}\mbox{ }\left\|D_{h}\psi\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\|\partial_{\bf x}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where the last inequality is due to the property of the finite difference in
L^{p}
space
[
64
, Lemma 7.23]
.
Together with (
4.20
), this completes the proof of (
4.22
) in (
Claim 1
).

(
Claim 2
)   For any
{\bf x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have

(4.23)

\|\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}).

To prove (
Claim 2
), observe that from (
4.22
) in
(Claim 1)
,

\left\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}\psi\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}+\left\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\partial_{x_{2}}\psi\right\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})})

for any
{\bf x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then, applying (
4.20
) together with an interpolation inequality (
e.g.

[
82
, Theorem 13.51]
),

(4.24)

\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }\lesssim\|\partial_{x_{1}}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}+\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\leq\mbox{ }C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }\lesssim\|\partial_{x_{2}}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}+\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\partial_{x_{2}}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\leq\mbox{ }C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}).

Regarding the remaining term
\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\psi
, we use the equation (
4.17
):

-\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\psi=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}f(\psi-Wx_{2}-\gamma)+\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\psi&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}\geq 0,\\
-f\left(-\left(\psi-Wx_{2}\right)-\gamma\right)+\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\psi&\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}<0.\end{array}\right.

Observe that, due to the monotonicity of
f(s)=s_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
,

\displaystyle\left|f(\psi({\bf x})-Wx_{2}-\gamma)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|f(\psi({\bf x}))|\leq\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{1/(p-1)}\quad\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}\geq 0

\displaystyle\left|-f(-\psi({\bf x})+Wx_{2}-\gamma)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|f(-\psi({\bf x}))|\leq\|\psi\|_{\infty}^{1/(p-1)}\quad\textrm{if}\,\,x_{2}<0.

Thus, for any
{\bf x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\|f(\psi-Wx_{2}-\gamma)\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}+\|f(-\psi+Wx_{2}-\gamma)\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-})}+\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\psi\|_{L^{q^{*}}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}),

where the last inequality is due to (
4.21
) and (
4.24
).
Combining all the above estimates for
\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\psi
,
\partial_{x_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}\psi
, and
\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\psi
, we conclude (
4.23
) of
(Claim 2)
.

Therefore, by applying the Sobolev embedding theorem to
\partial_{{\bf x}}\psi
, and using (
4.20
), (
4.21
) and (
4.23
), we deduce

\|\psi\|_{C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\bigl(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\bigr).

This completes the proof of the lemma for the case
p<2
.

(Case 2:
p\geq 2
)

As in the proof for the case
p<2
, we have
\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\psi\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
q\in(1,p)
; in particular, for
q\in(2,p)
.
Then by Sobolev embedding,
\psi\in C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
\beta\in(0,1)
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

During the proof of Lemma
4.13
, we have established that
\psi\in H_{\text{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, that is,
\psi
satisfies the elliptic equation (
4.17
) in the sense of
H^{2}
-Sobolev functions.

Proposition 4.15

(Regularity of the vortex)
.

The odd extension
\widetilde{\omega}
(with respect to the
x_{2}
-variable) of any maximizer
\omega\in S_{\mu}
inherits the same regularity as the vorticity function
f
in (
4.13
).
In particular, if
p\in(4/3,3/2)
, i.e.,
1/(p-1)\in(2,3)
,
then
\widetilde{\omega}
satisfies

\widetilde{\omega}\in C^{2,1/(p-1)-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

and we have

(4.25)

\|\widetilde{\omega}\|_{C^{2,1/(p-1)-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\Delta\psi\|_{C^{2,1/(p-1)-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C,

where
C=C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})})>0
.
A similar statement also holds for the remaining case
p\geq 3/2
; see Remark
4.16
.

Proof.

We consider only the case
p\in(4/3,3/2)
, as the proofs for the remaining cases are similar.

We fix an arbitrary
{\bf x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and take a cut-off function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\chi=1
in
B_{1}({\bf x}_{0})
and
{\mathrm{supp}}\,\chi\subset B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})
.
From (
4.17
), we see that
\chi\cdot\psi
satisfies the following equation:

\displaystyle-\Delta(\chi\cdot\psi)

\displaystyle=g\quad\textrm{in}\,\,B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}),

\displaystyle\chi\cdot\psi

\displaystyle=0\quad\textrm{on}\,\,\partial B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}),

where

g:=-\chi\Delta\psi-2\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi-\psi\Delta\chi.

(Step 1)
There exists
\alpha=\alpha(p)\in(0,1)
such that

(4.26)

\|g\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}\leq C

for some
C=C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})})>0
.

Indeed, since
\psi\in C^{1,\beta}
for some
\beta>0
by (
4.18
) of Lemma
4.13
, it is clear that

\|2\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi+\psi\Delta\chi\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})})

for any
\alpha\leq\beta
.
For the term
\chi\Delta\psi
, it suffices to show

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bf y},{\bf z}\in B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}),\\
{\bf y}\neq{\bf z}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|\Delta\psi({\bf y})-\Delta\psi({\bf z})\right|}{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}|^{\alpha}}\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}).

To this end, we consider the following cases for the locations of the points
{\bf y}=(y_{1},y_{2})
and
{\bf z}=(z_{1},z_{2})
.

(a) One point in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
and one in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
:
Suppose that
{\bf y}\in B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
and
{\bf z}\in B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
.
From the condition
\psi(y_{1},0)=\psi(z_{1},0)=0
, we see that

\displaystyle(\psi(y_{1},0)-W\cdot 0-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}=(\psi(z_{1},0)-W\cdot 0-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}=0.

Then, for
\alpha\leq 1/(p-1)-2
(recall that
2<1/(p-1)<3
), we have

(4.27)

\displaystyle\frac{\left|(\psi({\bf y})-Wy_{2}-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}+(-\psi({\bf z})+Wz_{2}-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\right|}{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}|^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\frac{\left|(\psi({\bf y})-Wy_{2}-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\right|}{|{\bf y}-(y_{1},0)|^{1/(p-1)}}\cdot|{\bf y}-(y_{1},0)|^{1/(p-1)-\alpha}+\frac{\left|(-\psi({\bf z})+Wz_{2}-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\right|}{|{\bf z}-(z_{1},0)|^{1/(p-1)}}\cdot|{\bf z}-(z_{1},0)|^{1/(p-1)-\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\left|(\psi({\bf y})-Wy_{2}-\gamma)_{+}\right|^{1/(p-1)}}{|{\bf y}-(y_{1},0)|^{1/(p-1)}}+\frac{\left|(-\psi({\bf z})+Wz_{2}-\gamma)_{+}\right|^{1/(p-1)}}{|{\bf z}-(z_{1},0)|^{1/(p-1)}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{|\psi({\bf y})|^{1/(p-1)}}{|{\bf y}-(y_{1},0)|^{1/(p-1)}}+\frac{|\psi({\bf z})|^{1/(p-1)}}{|{\bf z}-(z_{1},0)|^{1/(p-1)}}\leq 2\|\psi\|_{C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}).

Here, we use the conditions
{\bf y}\in B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
,
{\bf z}\in B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
for the second and third inequalities, as well as the fact that
\psi(y_{1},0)=\psi(z_{1},0)=0
together with
\psi\in C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
We set
\alpha=\min\{\beta,1/(p-1)-2\}
.

(b) Both points in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
or
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
:
By symmetry, it suffices to consider the case
{\bf y},{\bf z}\in B_{2}({\bf x}_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
.
Since
\psi\in C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have

(4.28)

\displaystyle\frac{\left|(\psi({\bf y})-Wy_{2}-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}-(\psi({\bf z})-Wz_{2}-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}\right|}{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}|^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\frac{\left|f(\psi({\bf y})-Wy_{2}-\gamma)-f(\psi({\bf z})-Wz_{2}-\gamma)\right|}{|\left(\psi({\bf y})-Wy_{2}-\gamma\right)-\left(\psi({\bf z})-Wz_{2}-\gamma\right)|}\cdot\frac{|\left(\psi({\bf y})-Wy_{2}-\gamma\right)-\left(\psi({\bf z})-Wz_{2}-\gamma\right)|}{|{\bf y}-{\bf z}|^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}),

where
\alpha=\min\{\beta,1/(p-1)-2\}
.

(Step 2)
We now verify that the function
\psi
from Lemma
4.13
is indeed belongs to
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Since we have (
4.26
) from
(Step 1)
, by the classical Schauder theory
[
64
, Corollary 6.9]
, there is a unique solution
\varphi\in C^{2,\alpha}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))
to the following equation:

\displaystyle-\Delta\varphi

\displaystyle=g\quad\textrm{in}\,\,B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}),

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle=0\quad\textrm{on}\,\,\partial B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}).

Moreover, since
\varphi
is also an
H^{2}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))
solution to the above equation, we deduce that

\chi\psi=\psi=\varphi\quad\textrm{in}\,\,B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}),

by the uniqueness theorem of the Sobolev space theory.
In particular, applying the
a priori
estimate
[
64
, Theorem 6.6]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\leq\|\varphi\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|g\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}),

where the last inequality is due to Lemma
4.13
and (
4.26
).
Since the above estimate is uniform in the choice of
{\bf x}_{0}
, we conclude that
\psi\in C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with the estimate

\|\psi\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\bigl(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\bigr).

(Step 3)
Finally, we prove that
\widetilde{\omega}\in C^{2,1/(p-1)-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Since
f\in C^{2,1/(p-1)-2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\psi\in C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\alpha\leq 1/(p-1)-2
, we can directly verify that

\|g\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(B_{2}({\bf x}_{0}))}\lesssim\|\psi\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})})

as done in (
4.27
).
We then we apply a higher regularity
a priori
estimate
[
64
, Theorem 6.19]
to the
C^{2,\alpha}
solution
\chi\psi
, obtaining:

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{C^{4,\alpha}(B_{1}({\bf x}_{0}))}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|\psi\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|g\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}).

Since
{\bf x}_{0}
is arbitrary, this yields

\|\psi\|_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(p,\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}).

Combining this with the fact that
f\in C^{2,1/(p-1)}(\mathbb{R})
and the relation (
4.17
), we obtain (
4.25
), which establishes

\widetilde{\omega}=-\Delta\psi\in C^{2,1/(p-1)-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

An application of classical Schauder theory yields the following regularity results:
For any
\omega\in S_{\mu}
, its odd extension
\widetilde{\omega}
and the corresponding stream function
\psi
solving (
4.17
) satisfy

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}\in\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}C^{2,1/(p-1)-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p\in(4/3,3/2),\\
C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p=3/2,\\
C^{1,1/(p-1)-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p\in(3/2,2),\\
C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p=2,\\
C^{0,1/(p-1)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p>2.\end{array}\right.\quad\psi\in\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}C^{4,1/(p-1)-2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p\in(4/3,3/2),\\
C^{3,1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p=3/2,\\
C^{3,1/(p-1)-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p\in(3/2,2),\\
C^{2,1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p=2,\\
C^{2,1/(p-1)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})&\textrm{if}\,\,p>2.\end{array}\right.

for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
Moreover, by exploiting the regularity results for the stream function, one can further deduce the optimal regularity of the maximizer
\omega
.
See Remark
4.25
for details.

Remark 4.17

(Regularity of detached maximizer)
.

As mentioned in Remark
1.7
, if we do not restrict attention to the Sadovskii vortex (the case where the vortex touches the
x_{1}
-axis), one can construct vortex pair solutions to (
1.1
) with regularity beyond
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Here we outline a construction of a non-touching vortex pair with even higher regularity exceeding
C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Suppose that for any
p\in(1,4/3)
, one can find
\mu_{0}>0
and
\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}(p,\mu_{0})>0
such that the following holds:
for any
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
and
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
, we have

(4.29)

I_{\mu,1,\lambda}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\sup_{\omega\in K_{\mu,1}}E_{p,\lambda}(\omega)\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }0.

Then, based on arguments in the proofs of Propositions
4.11
and
4.15
, we have the following:
for any given integer
m\geq 1
, there exists a steady vortex pair
\widetilde{\omega}\in C^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to (
1.4
) as a maximizer of
E_{p,\lambda}(\cdot)
for
p<1+1/m
.

To verify (
4.29
), we use the Sadovskii vortex patch
\omega_{\text{patch}}=\mathbf{1}_{A}
, introduced in Section
3.2
, as a trial function for the penalized energy
E_{p,\lambda}(\cdot)
.
This vortex patch maximizes the kinetic energy
E(\cdot)
and satisfies

E(\omega_{\text{patch}})\simeq\mu^{4/3},\quad\|x_{2}\omega_{\text{patch}}\|_{1}=\mu,\quad\|\omega_{\text{patch}}\|_{1}\simeq\mu^{2/3},

provided that
\mu>0
is sufficiently small.
More precisely, by (
3.7
) and (
3.10
) in Lemma
3.14
(or
[
47
, Lemma 4.6]
), together with the identity

\left\|\omega_{\text{patch}}\right\|_{p}^{p}=\left\|\omega_{\text{patch}}\right\|_{1},

there exists a constant
\mu_{0}>0
such that for any
\mu\in(0,\mu_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle E_{p,\lambda}(\omega_{\text{patch}})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E(\omega_{\text{patch}})-\frac{\lambda}{p}\left\|\omega_{\text{patch}}/\lambda\right\|_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }E(\omega_{\text{patch}})-\frac{1}{p\lambda^{p-1}}\|\omega_{\text{patch}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mbox{ }\mu^{4/3}-\frac{1}{p\lambda^{p-1}}\mu^{2/3}

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }\mu^{4/3}\left(1-\frac{\mu_{0}^{-2/3}}{p\lambda^{p-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2}\mu^{4/3}\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }0

provided that
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
for some large
\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}(p,\mu_{0})>0
.
This verifies (
4.29
).

By combining the vorticity formulation (
4.13
) with the regularity of the stream function, we can also establish the compactness of the support of each maximizer.

Lemma 4.18
.

For each
\omega\in S_{\mu}
, the support of
\omega\in S_{\mu}
is compact in
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
.

Proof.

From the relation (
4.13
) in Proposition
4.11
, that is,
\omega=(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
, it is clear that

(4.30)

\displaystyle Wx_{2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Wx_{2}+\gamma\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\quad(W>0)

on
\operatorname{supp}\omega
.
On the other hand, by Lemma
4.13
, we know
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
is sufficiently smooth, in particular,
\mathcal{G}[\omega]/x_{2}
is countinuous in
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
.
Then applying Hardy’s inequality with respect to
x_{2}
variable yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(x_{2}^{-1}\int_{0}^{x_{2}}\partial_{y_{2}}\mathcal{G}[\omega](x_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{2}\right)^{2}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}x_{2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }4\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{x_{2}}\mathcal{G}[\omega]\left({\bf x}\right)\right|^{2}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}x_{2}

for each
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
.
By integrating both sides on
x_{1}\in(-\infty,\infty)
and using the fact that
\mathcal{G}[\omega](x_{1},0)=0
, we see that
\|\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})/x_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 4\|\nabla\mathcal{G}[\omega]\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim E(\omega)<\infty
and then,

(4.31)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})}{x_{2}}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{as}\,\,|{\bf x}|\to\infty.

From (
4.30
) and (
4.31
), we deduce that
\operatorname{supp}\omega
is bounded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
. The lemma is proved.
∎

4.4.
Proof of Theorem
4.2

In this subsection, we complete the proof of Theorem
4.2
by establishing the Lyapunov stability of
S_{\mu}
constructed in Proposition
4.7
.

We present the following compactness result for the set
S_{\mu}
of maximizers, which plays a key role in the proof of Theorem
4.2
.
The proof follows from previously established properties of the maximizer and Lions’ concentration-compactness principle; Lemma
2.4
.

Proposition 4.19

(Compactness of maximizing sequence)
.

Let
\mu>0
and
\{\omega_{n}\geq 0\}
be a maximizing sequence such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\omega_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu,\quad\sup_{n}\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\infty,\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{p}[\omega_{n}]\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }I_{\mu}.

Then there exists a subsequence
\{\omega_{n_{k}}\}
, a sequence
\{y^{(k)}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
and a (non-negative) function
\omega\in S_{\mu}
such that

(4.32)

\displaystyle\left\|\omega_{n_{k}}\left(\cdot+\left(y^{(k)},0\right)\right)-\omega\right\|_{p}+\left\|x_{2}\left(\omega_{n_{k}}\left(\cdot+\left(y^{(k)},0\right)\right)-\omega\right)\right\|_{1}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{as}\,\,k\to\infty.

While postponing the proof of Proposition
4.19
, we first prove Theorem
4.2
based on Proposition
4.7
and Proposition
4.19
.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Thanks to Proposition
4.7
, we have already established the existence of a maximizer,
i.e.
,
S_{\mu}\neq\emptyset
, where every maximizer satisfies the Steiner symmetry condition up to a
x_{1}
-translation.
The remaining is to prove the Lyapunov stability of
S_{\mu}
. We prove it by a contradiction argument.
Let us suppose that the stability result of Theorem
4.2
is not true.
Then there exist a constant
\varepsilon_{0}
, a sequence
\{\theta_{0,n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of nonnegative
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
initial data, and a sequence
\{t_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of positive numbers such that the following holds:
for each
n\geq 1
, we have

(4.33)

\displaystyle\|\theta_{0,n}\|_{1}\leq 1+\frac{1}{n},\quad\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\left\{\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega\|_{p}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0,n}-\omega)\|_{1}\right\}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{n},

and

\displaystyle\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\left\{\left\|\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot\right)-\omega\right\|_{p}+\left\|x_{2}\left\{\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot\right)-\omega\right\}\,\right\|_{1}\right\}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\varepsilon_{0},

where
\theta_{n}(t,\cdot)
is the global-in-time weak solution to (
1.3
) with the initial data
\theta_{0,n}
.
From (
4.33
), we take a sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}\subset S_{\mu}
so that

\|\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n}\|_{p}+\|x_{2}(\theta_{0,n}-\omega_{n})\|_{1}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0

as
n\to\infty
.
Note that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\theta_{0,n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\|x_{2}\omega_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu\quad\mbox{and}\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\theta_{0,n}\|_{1}\leq 1.

Also, we have
\sup_{n}\|\theta_{0,n}\|_{p}<\infty
by (
4.5
) of Lemma
4.5
, and then
\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{p}(\theta_{0,n})=I_{\mu}
by (
2.7
) of Proposition
2.2
.
Thus, by the conservation property of the Yudovich solution,
\{\theta_{n}(t_{n})\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a maximizing sequence satisfying all assumptions in Proposition
4.19
.

Now by applying Proposition
4.19
to the sequence
\{\theta_{n}(t_{n})\}
, we obtain a subsequence still denoted by
\{\theta_{n}\}
and a sequence of real numbers
\{y^{(n)}\}
such that, for some
\hat{\omega}\in S_{\mu}
, we get the strong convergence

\displaystyle\left\|\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot+\left(y^{(n)},0\right)\right)-\hat{\omega}\right\|_{p}+\left\|x_{2}\left\{\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot+\left(y^{(n)},0\right)\right)-\hat{\omega}\right\}\right\|_{1}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{as}\,\,n\to\infty.

Finally, by letting
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle 0\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\left\|\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot+\left(y^{(n)},0\right)\right)-\hat{\omega}\right\|_{p}+\left\|x_{2}\left\{\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot+\left(y^{(n)},0\right)\right)-\hat{\omega}\right\}\,\right\|_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}\left\{\inf_{\omega\in S_{\mu}}\left(\left\|\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot\right)-\omega\right\|_{p}+\left\|x_{2}\left\{\theta_{n}\left(t_{n},\cdot\right)-\omega\right\}\,\right\|_{1}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\mbox{ }\varepsilon_{0},

which is a contradiction. The theorem is proved.
∎

We conclude this subsection with the proof of Proposition
4.19
.

Proof of Proposition
4.19
.

We consider a maximizing sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}\subset K_{\mu_{n}}
for the maximal energy
I_{\mu}
.
By Lemma
4.5
, the sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
.
We define
\rho_{n}=x_{2}\omega_{n}
and apply Lemma
2.4
.
Standard concentration–compactness arguments (
cf
. Proposition
3.15
) imply that, up to a subsequence (still denoted by
\{\omega_{n}\}
), one of the following three alternatives occurs: (i)
Compactness
, (ii)
Vanishing
, or (iii)
Dichotomy
. We treat each case separately.

Occurrence of Case (i) Compactness:

There exists a sequence
\{{\bf y}_{n}=(y_{1,n},y_{2,n})\}\subset\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
such that for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
R=R(\varepsilon)>0
satisfying

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\mu-\varepsilon.

We distinguish two subcases depending on the behavior of
y_{2,n}
:

(a)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}y_{2,n}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\infty
,

(b)

\sup_{n}y_{2,n}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\infty
.

We show that (a) leads to a contradiction, thus (b) must hold.

Case (a):

\limsup_{n\to\infty}y_{2,n}=\infty
.

In this case, up to a subsequence,
y_{2,n}\to\infty
.
Additionally, we can arrange that

(4.34)

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\setminus B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})}x_{2}\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2\varepsilon.

We claim that in this setting, the energy
E(\omega_{n})
tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
.
If so, it would follow that

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{p}[\omega_{n}]\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0,

contradicting the fact that
I_{\mu}>0
.
Thus, case (a) must be ruled out.

To show
\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})=0
, we decompose the energy as follows:

\displaystyle 2E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]\omega_{n}\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}.

We estimate each term separately by using uniform estimates on
\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]
together with the smallness of the impulse outside
B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})
.
Indeed, for the first term, using inequality (
2.4
) of Proposition
2.2
with
\theta=1/2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\left\|\frac{\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]}{x_{2}^{1-1/p}}\right\|_{\infty}\int_{B({\bf y}_{n},R)\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}^{1-1/p}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{C\mu}{(y_{2,n}-R)^{1/p}}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

For the second term, by applying Hölder’s inequality and (
4.34
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\left\|\frac{\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]}{x_{2}^{1-1/p}}\right\|_{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})}x_{2}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)^{1-1/p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B_{R}({\bf y}_{n})}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon^{1-1/p}.

Thus, taking the limit as
n\to\infty
and then letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we deduce that

\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0,

contradicting
I_{\mu}>0
.
Thus, case (a) is ruled out, and we are in case (b):

\sup_{n}y_{2,n}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\infty.

Case (b):

\sup_{n}y_{2,n}<\infty
.

After translating in
x_{1}
, we may assume
y_{1,n}=0
,
i.e.
,
{\bf y}_{n}=(0,y_{2,n})
.
Moreover, by choosing
R>0
large enough, we may take
y_{2,n}=0
without loss of generality.
Thus, there exists a ball
B=B_{R}(0)
such that

(4.35)

\displaystyle\mu\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\geq\mu-\varepsilon.

Since
\{\omega_{n}\}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{p}
by Lemma
4.5
, we can extract a subsequence, still denoted by
\{\omega_{n}\}
, such that

\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\rightharpoonup\mbox{ }\omega\quad\text{in }L^{p}

for some
\omega\in L^{p}
.
First, it is trivial that
\|\omega\|_{1}\leq 1
.
Passing to the limit in the above inequality, we obtain
