\mu\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\geq\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\mu-\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, it follows that

\|x_{2}\omega\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu,
and
hence

\omega\in K_{\mu}.

We claim that

(4.36)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E(\omega).

If this is true, then using the weak lower semicontinuity of the
L^{p}
norm, we have

\|\omega\|_{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}.

Then identity (
4.36
) implies

\displaystyle I_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{p}(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})-\frac{1}{p}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }I_{\mu}.

This forces

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}=\|\omega\|_{p},

and by uniform convexity of
L^{p}
, we deduce that
\omega_{n}\to\omega
in
L^{p}
.
Next, we estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}|\omega_{n}-\omega|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}|\omega_{n}-\omega|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}x_{2}|\omega_{n}-\omega|\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\|\omega_{n}-\omega\|_{p}+2\varepsilon.

Taking the limit as
n\to\infty
and then sending
\varepsilon\to 0
, we obtain (
4.32
), confirming that
x_{2}\omega_{n}\to x_{2}\omega
in
L^{1}
.
Since
E_{p}(\omega_{n})\to E_{p}(\omega)
, we conclude that the limit function
\omega\in K_{\mu}
is a maximizer of
I_{\mu}
,
i.e.
,
\omega\in S_{\mu}
.

Thus, it remains to prove (
4.36
).
We begin by decomposing the energy:

\displaystyle 2E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

Moreover, we further decompose
I_{1}
as

\displaystyle I_{1}=\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}+\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}=:I_{1,1}+I_{1,2}.

Using the symmetry
G({\bf x},{\bf y})=G({\bf y},{\bf x})
, we see that

\displaystyle I_{1,2}=\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=I_{2}.

Consequently, we get

2E(\omega_{n})=I_{1,1}+I_{1,2}+I_{2}=I_{1,1}+2I_{2},

and we derive the estimate

\displaystyle\left|2E(\omega_{n})-I_{1,1}\right|\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\lesssim\varepsilon^{1-1/p},

where the last inequality follows by applying the bound

\displaystyle I_{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\left\|\frac{\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]}{x_{2}^{1-1/p}}\right\|_{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}x_{2}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)^{1-1/p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)^{1/p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon^{1-1/p}

from inequality (
2.4
) of Proposition
2.2
with
\theta=1/2
(to control
\|\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]/x_{2}^{1-1/p}\|_{\infty}
) combined with (
4.35
), the smallness of the impulse outside
B
.
A similar argument for
E(\omega)
gives

\displaystyle 2\left|E(\omega_{n})-E(\omega)\right|\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\left|\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{B\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\left(\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})-\omega({\bf x})\omega({\bf y})\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right|

\displaystyle\quad+C\varepsilon^{1-1/p}.

Since
G({\bf x},{\bf y})\in L^{p^{\prime}}(B\times B)
with
1/p^{\prime}=1-1/p
, and
\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\rightharpoonup\omega({\bf x})\omega({\bf y})
in
L^{p}(B\times B)
, we take the limits
n\to\infty
first and then
\varepsilon\to 0
to conclude (
4.36
) as desired.

Now the proof of Proposition
4.19
is complete if we exclude the remaining cases:
(ii) Vanishing
and
(iii) Dichotomy
.

Elimination of Case (ii) Vanishing:

Suppose now that vanishing occurs:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\sup_{{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{B_{R}({\bf y})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{n}\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0\quad\text{for each }R>0.

To rule out this case, it suffices to show

\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0,

because this would imply
I_{\mu}\leq 0
(recalling that
E_{p}(\omega_{n})\leq E(\omega_{n})
), contradicting
I_{\mu}>0
.

We decompose the energy
E(\omega_{n})
into near and far-field terms.
For any
R\geq 1
,

\displaystyle 2E(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}=\mbox{ }\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq R}+\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R}.

By applying appropriate estimates on the Green’s function, we can show that both contributions become small as
n\to\infty
and then
R\to\infty
.

Indeed, using the bound
G({\bf x},{\bf y})<\pi^{-1}x_{2}y_{2}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{-2}
, we estimate the first term as

\displaystyle\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq R}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\leq\frac{\mu^{2}}{\pi R^{2}}.

For the second term, we further split it into two parts:

\displaystyle\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\iint\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\\
G\geq Rx_{2}y_{2}\end{subarray}}+\iint\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\\
G<Rx_{2}y_{2}\end{subarray}}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\mathbf{I}_{n,R}+\mathbf{II}_{n,R}.

To estimate
\mathbf{I}_{n,R}
, we first note that the bound

Rx_{2}y_{2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }G({\bf x},{\bf y})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}

yields
|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\leq R^{-1/2}<1
.
Moreover, since

|{\bf x}-{\bf y}^{*}|\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }|{\bf x}^{*}-{\bf y}|\leq|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|+2x_{2}<1+2x_{2},

it holds that

\displaystyle 4\pi\cdot G({\bf x},{\bf y})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\log{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}^{*}|}-\log{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|}\leq\log\left(1+2x_{2}\right)+\left|\log\left|{\bf x}-{\bf y}\right|\right|\leq\mbox{ }2x_{2}+\left|\log{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|}\right|.

Then, for
1/p^{\prime}=1-1/p
, we estimate
\mathbf{I}_{n,R}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{n,R}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\iint\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\\
G\geq Rx_{2}y_{2}\end{subarray}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R^{-1/2}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\|\omega_{n}\|_{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega_{n}({\bf x})\left(\int_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R^{-1/2}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{p^{\prime}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C(1+\mu_{n})\left\{\left(\int_{0}^{R^{-1/2}}r\left(\log r\right)^{p^{\prime}}\textrm{d}r\right)^{1/{p^{\prime}}}+R^{-1/{p^{\prime}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\to\mbox{ }0\quad\text{as }R\to\infty.

Similarly, we estimate
\mathbf{II}_{n,R}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{II}_{n,R}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\iint\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|<R,\\
G<Rx_{2}y_{2}\end{subarray}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{n}({\bf x})\omega_{n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }R\mu\left(\sup_{{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{B_{R}({\bf y})\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{n}({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Thus, taking first the limit
n\to\infty
and then
R\to\infty
, we conclude that

\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{n})=0.

This establishes that the case
(ii) Vanishing
does not occur.

Elimination of Case (iii) Dichotomy:

Assume, for contradiction, that dichotomy occurs for some
\alpha\in(0,\mu)
.
Fix an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
.
Then there exist non-negative functions
\omega_{1,n}
and
\omega_{2,n}
, which are bounded by
\omega_{n}
, satisfying that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\big(\|\omega_{1,n}\|_{1}+\|\omega_{2,n}\|_{1}\big)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,\quad\operatorname{supp}\omega_{1,n}\cap\operatorname{supp}\omega_{2,n}=\emptyset,

and for the quantities

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle:=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{1,n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x},\quad\beta_{n}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{2,n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x},\quad d_{n}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\operatorname{dist}\left(\operatorname{supp}\omega_{1,n},\operatorname{supp}\omega_{2,n}\right)

with the non-negative function
\omega_{3,n}:=\omega_{n}-\omega_{1,n}-\omega_{2,n}
, we also have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(\left\|x_{2}\omega_{3,n}\right\|_{1}+\left|\alpha_{n}-\alpha\right|+\left|\beta_{n}-(\mu-\alpha)\right|\right)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon,

\displaystyle d_{n}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }\infty\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Furthermore, by extracting a suitable subsequence, we may assume

(4.37)

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|x_{2}\omega_{3,n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2\varepsilon,\quad\alpha_{n}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }\overline{\alpha},\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{n}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }\overline{\beta}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

for some
\overline{\alpha}\in(\alpha-\varepsilon,\alpha+\varepsilon)
and
\overline{\beta}\in(\mu-\alpha-\varepsilon,\mu-\alpha+\varepsilon)
.
Under these assumptions, we will arrive at a contradiction.

(Step 1)

A straightforward computation yields the following decomposition of the energy functional:

\displaystyle 2E(\omega_{n})=\mbox{ }

\displaystyle 2E(\omega_{1,n})+2E(\omega_{1,n})

\displaystyle+\mbox{ }

\displaystyle 2\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{1,n}({\bf x})\omega_{2,n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle+\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})(2\omega_{n}({\bf x})-\omega_{3,n}({\bf x}))\omega_{3,n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=:\mbox{ }

\displaystyle 2E(\omega_{1,n})+2E(\omega_{1,n})+2\mathbf{I}_{n}+\mathbf{J}_{n}.

Using the pointwise bound
G({\bf x},{\bf y})\leq x_{2}y_{2}|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{-2}
, we estimate the cross interaction term
\mathbf{I}_{n}
by

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{I}_{n}\right|\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mathbf{I}_{n}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\iint_{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|\geq d_{n}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{1,n}({\bf x})\omega_{2,n}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\frac{\mu^{2}}{d_{n}^{2}}.

For
\mathbf{J}_{n}
, applying the inequality (
2.5
) in Proposition
2.2
and using the uniform bound on
x_{2}\omega_{3,n}
from (
4.37
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{J}_{n}\right|\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mathbf{J}_{n}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\|2\omega_{n}-\omega_{3,n}\|_{1}^{1/2}\|2\omega_{n}-\omega_{3,n}\|_{p}^{1/2}\|x_{2}\omega_{3,n}\|_{1}^{1-1/p}\|\omega_{3,n}\|_{1}^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\varepsilon^{1-1/p}.

Putting these estimates together, we obtain the following upper bound for the penalized energy:

\displaystyle E_{p}(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }E(\omega_{n})-\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega_{n}^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }E(\omega_{n})-\frac{1}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left(\omega_{1,n}+\omega_{2,n}\right)^{p}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega_{1,n})+E_{p}(\omega_{2,n})+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2d_{n}^{2}}+C\varepsilon^{1-1/p}.

(Step 2)

Next, we apply the Steiner symmetrization to the functions
\omega_{i,n}
for
i=1,2
, and denote their symmetrized versions by
\omega_{i,n}^{*}
.
This allows us to derive the following estimate:

(4.38)

\displaystyle E_{p}(\omega_{n})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega_{1,n}^{*})+E_{p}(\omega_{2,n}^{*})+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\pi d_{n}^{2}}+C\varepsilon^{1-1/p},

(4.39)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\big(\|\omega_{1,n}^{*}\|_{1}+\|\omega_{2,n}^{*}\|_{1}\big)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,\quad\|\omega_{1,n}^{*}\|_{p}+\|\omega_{2,n}^{*}\|_{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C,

(4.40)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{1,n}^{*}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\alpha_{n},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{2,n}^{*}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\beta_{n}.

For each fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we may pass to a further subsequence (still denoted by
\omega_{i,n}^{*}
) such that

\omega_{i,n}^{*}\mbox{ }\rightharpoonup\mbox{ }\overline{\omega}_{i}^{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in }L^{p}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\overline{\omega}_{i}^{\varepsilon}\|_{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{i,n}^{*}\|_{p}

for some non-negative functions
\overline{\omega}_{i}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}
,
i=1,2
.
Since
\omega_{i,n}^{*}
is symmetric and non-increasing in
x_{1}>0
, we apply Lemma
4.8
to conclude

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}E(\omega_{i,n}^{*})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E(\overline{\omega}_{i}^{\varepsilon}),\quad i=1,2.

Thus, by taking
n\to\infty
in (
4.38
), we obtain

(4.41)

\displaystyle I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\overline{\omega}_{1}^{\varepsilon})+E_{p}(\overline{\omega}_{2}^{\varepsilon})+C\varepsilon^{1/2},

(4.42)

\displaystyle\|\overline{\omega}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\|_{1}+\|\overline{\omega}_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,\quad\|\overline{\omega}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\|_{p}+\|\overline{\omega}_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{p}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C,

(4.43)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\overline{\omega}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\overline{\alpha},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\overline{\omega}_{2}^{\varepsilon}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\overline{\beta}.

Since
\overline{\omega}_{i}^{\varepsilon}
remains the symmetry and monotonicity properties in
x_{1}>0
, we may apply the same argument to
\{\overline{\omega}_{i}^{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
As a result, passing to the limit in (
4.41
) yields weak convergence:

\overline{\omega}_{i}^{\varepsilon}\mbox{ }\rightharpoonup\mbox{ }\overline{\omega}_{i}\quad\text{in }L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})

for some non-negative functions
\overline{\omega}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, satisfying

\displaystyle I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\overline{\omega}_{1})+E_{p}(\overline{\omega}_{2}),

\displaystyle\|\overline{\omega}_{1}\|_{1}+\|\overline{\omega}_{2}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\overline{\omega}_{1}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\alpha,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\overline{\omega}_{2}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\mu-\alpha.

If
\overline{\omega}_{1}\equiv 0\equiv\overline{\omega}_{2}
, then we obtain
I_{\mu}\leq 0
, contradicting
I_{\mu}>0
by Lemma
4.4
.
Therefore, we may assume without loss of generality that
\overline{\omega}_{1}\not\equiv 0
.

(Step 3)

Define
\nu_{1}:=1-\|\overline{\omega}_{2}\|_{1}>0
, and apply Lemma
4.9
along with the scaling described in Remark
4.1
to obtain a maximizer
\omega_{1}\not\equiv 0
in the class
\widetilde{K}_{\alpha,\nu_{1}}
satisfying

\displaystyle E_{p}(\omega_{1})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\sup_{\omega\in\widetilde{K}_{\alpha,\nu_{1}}}E_{p}(\omega)\quad\mbox{with}\quad\int x_{2}\omega_{1}\textrm{d}{\bf x}=\alpha.

Note that
\omega_{1}
has a compact support in
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
, by Lemma
4.18
.
Combining this with the earlier inequality yields

\displaystyle I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega_{1})+E_{p}(\overline{\omega}_{2}),

\displaystyle\|\omega_{1}\|_{1}+\|\overline{\omega}_{2}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{1}\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\alpha\mbox{ },\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\overline{\omega}_{2}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\mu-\alpha.

If it happens that
\overline{\omega}_{2}\equiv 0
, then
I_{\mu}\leq I_{\alpha}
, contradicting the strict inequality
I_{\mu}>I_{\alpha}
from Lemma
4.4
.
Hence, we must have
\overline{\omega}_{2}\not\equiv 0
.

We now define
\nu_{2}:=1-\|\omega_{1}\|_{1}>0
, and again apply Lemma
4.9
(using the scaling in Remark
4.1
) to construct a maximizer
\omega_{2}\not\equiv 0
in the class
\widetilde{K}_{\mu-\alpha,\nu_{2}}
such that

\displaystyle I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega_{1})+E_{p}(\omega_{2}),

\displaystyle\|\omega_{1}\|_{1}+\|\omega_{2}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }1,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{1}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\alpha,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega_{2}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\mu-\alpha.

Since both
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
are compactly supported, we may translate one of them in the
x_{1}
-direction so that their supports become disjoint:

\operatorname{supp}\omega_{1}\cap\operatorname{supp}\omega_{2}=\emptyset.

Consequently, their sum
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}
belongs to
K_{\mu}
and is nontrivial.
Using the definition of
E_{p}
and the disjointness of supports, we compute

\displaystyle I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega_{1})+E_{p}(\omega_{2})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})-\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{1}({\bf x})\omega_{2}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }I_{\mu}-\iint G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega_{1}({\bf x})\omega_{2}({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle<\mbox{ }I_{\mu},

which is a contradiction.
This contradiction shows that the
(iii) Dichotomy
scenario is impossible. The proposition is proved.
∎

4.5.
Proof of Theorem
1.6
: regular Sadovskii vortex family

This section aims to prove Proposition
4.20
below, which establishes the existence of Sadovskii vortex as a maximizer
I_{\mu}
under small impulse. It will be used later to prove Theorem
4.3
.

Proposition 4.20

(small
\mu
implies Sadovskii vortex)
.

There exists a constant
\tilde{\mu}>0
such that the following holds:
For any
\mu\in(0,\tilde{\mu})
and for each maximizer
\omega\in S_{\mu}
, there exists a suitable translation in the
x_{1}
-direction such that

B_{r}(\mathbf{0})\mbox{ }\subset\mbox{ }{\mathrm{supp}}\,\widetilde{\omega}

for some
r>0
, where
\widetilde{\omega}
denotes the odd extension of
\omega
with respect to
x_{2}
-variable.
In other words, if
\mu<\tilde{\mu}
, then the support of any maximizer
\omega\in S_{\mu}
is in contiguous contact with the
x_{1}
-axis.

To prove Proposition
4.20
, let us begin with the following useful identity with respect to the maximum
I_{\mu}
of (penalized) energy
E_{p}(\omega)
and its impulse
\mu
, together with traveling speed
W
and flux constant
\gamma
.
A similar identity for the patch type problem can be found in
[
47
, Lemma 3.21]
, which deals with the kinetic energy
E(\omega)
, not the penalized energy
E_{p}(\omega)
.

Lemma 4.21

(Pohožaev type identity)
.

For any
\mu>0
, we have the following identity:

(4.44)

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{3p-4}{4p-4}W\mu\mbox{ }+\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2}\gamma\|\omega\|_{1}\quad\textrm{for any}\,\,\omega\in S_{\mu},

where
W
and
\gamma
are the unique constants satisfying (
4.13
);

\omega\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}.

Proof.

We first note that, as in the proof of
[
47
, Lemma 3.21]
, a direct calculation gives

\displaystyle\Delta f\left({\bf x}\right)\left({\bf x}\cdot\nabla f\right)\left({\bf x}\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}x_{1}}\left[\frac{x_{1}}{2}\left\{\left(\frac{\textrm{d}f}{\textrm{d}x_{1}}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{\textrm{d}f}{\textrm{d}x_{2}}\right)^{2}\right\}+x_{2}\frac{\textrm{d}f}{\textrm{d}x_{1}}\frac{\textrm{d}f}{\textrm{d}x_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}x_{2}}\left[\frac{x_{2}}{2}\left\{\left(-\frac{\textrm{d}f}{\textrm{d}x_{1}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\textrm{d}f}{\textrm{d}x_{2}}\right)^{2}\right\}+x_{1}\frac{\textrm{d}f}{\textrm{d}x_{1}}\frac{\textrm{d}f}{\textrm{d}x_{2}}\right],

for sufficiently smooth
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
.
By applying the above identity with the regularity result Lemma
4.18
to the odd extension (in
x_{2}
variable) of
\omega\in S_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
, it holds that

\displaystyle 0\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\Delta\psi({\bf x})\left({\bf x}\cdot\nabla\psi({\bf x})\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega({\bf x})\left({\bf x}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

and then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}{\bf x}\cdot\nabla\left[\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]\left({\bf x}\right)-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)_{+}^{p/(p-1)}\right]\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }

\displaystyle\frac{p}{p-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega({\bf x})\left({\bf x}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}-\frac{p}{p-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}{\bf x}\cdot\left(0,W\right)\omega\left({\bf x}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }

\displaystyle-\frac{p}{p-1}W\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{2}\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }-\frac{p}{p-1}\cdot W\mu.

This, with integration by parts, yields

2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]\left({\bf x}\right)-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)_{+}^{p/(p-1)}\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{p}{p-1}\cdot W\mu.

Thus, for
\omega\in S_{\mu}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p}{p-1}\right)W\mu\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]\left({\bf x}\right)-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)_{+}^{p/(p-1)}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }2E(\omega)-W\mu-\gamma\|\omega\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }2\left(I_{\mu}+\frac{1}{p}\|\omega\|_{p}^{p}\right)-W\mu-\gamma\|\omega\|_{1},

that is,

(4.45)

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left(\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{p}{p-1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)W\mu+\frac{1}{2}\gamma\|\omega\|_{1}-\frac{1}{p}\|\omega\|_{p}^{p}.

On the other hand, due to the relation (
4.13
) in Proposition
4.11
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega^{p}\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega\cdot\omega^{p-1}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{{\mathrm{supp}}\,\omega}\omega\left(\mathcal{G}\left[\omega\right]-Wx_{2}-\gamma\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p}{p-1}\right)W\mu,

and then by replacing
\|\omega\|_{p}^{p}
with
(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p}{p-1})W\mu
in (
4.45
), we get the desired result. The lemma is proved.
∎

Under the small impulse regime, we first show that the flux constant
\gamma
in the vorticity formulation vanishes.

Lemma 4.22
.

There exists a constant
\tilde{\mu}>0
such that for
\mu\in(0,\tilde{\mu})
, every maximizer
\omega\in S_{\mu}
satisfies (
4.13
) with
\gamma=0
, that is,

\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})-Wx_{2}\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}.

We divide the proof into two cases:
p\leq 2
and
p>2
.

Proof of Lemma
4.22
for
p\in(4/3,2]

.

To prove that
\gamma=0
for any sufficiently small
\mu
, we first claim that the following: for every maximizer
\omega\in S_{\mu}
satisfying (
4.13
),

\gamma>0\quad\textrm{implies}\quad\|\omega\|_{1}=1.

Indeed, if
\|\omega\|_{1}<1
, by taking
h_{1}=0
in (
4.14
) in the proof of Proposition
4.11
, we see that
\omega+\varepsilon\eta\in K_{\mu}
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
.
Then proceeding as in (
4.15
) leads to
\omega=(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2})_{+}^{1/(p-1)}
, which is a contradiction to
\gamma>0
by the uniqueness of
W
and
\gamma
in (
4.13
).
It means that, to prove
\gamma=0
for sufficiently small
\mu
, it is enough to show
\|\omega\|_{1}<1
for small
\mu
.

Suppose that
\omega\in S_{\mu}
is of full mass, that is,
\|\omega\|_{1}=1
. Observe that
\alpha:=1/(p-1)\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}\Big(\mathcal{G}\left[\omega\right]\left({\bf y}\right)-Wy_{2}-\gamma\Big)_{+}^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf y},{\bf x})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{\alpha}\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega({\bf x})\left(\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x},

where we use Jensen’s inequality with the assumption
\|\omega\|_{1}=1
for the second inequality, and the last equality is due to the symmetry
G({\bf x},{\bf y})=G({\bf y},{\bf x})
.
If
x_{2}<4\mu
, by (
2.3
) of Lemma
2.1
,

\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{1/2}\mu^{3/2}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\mu^{2}.

If
x_{2}\geq 4\mu
, on the set
\{{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:0<y_{2}<2\mu
}, we have
x_{2}>2y_{2}
, or equivalently,
x_{2}-y_{2}\geq x_{2}/2
.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }C\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}\left\{\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)\right\}^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{\alpha}\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}\left(\frac{y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right)^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{\alpha}\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}y_{2}^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{1}{|y_{1}|^{2}+|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{2}}\right)^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\textrm{d}y_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{\alpha}\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}y_{2}^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{1}{|y_{1}|^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}\right)^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\textrm{d}y_{2}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }Cx_{2}^{1-\alpha}\mu^{1+\alpha},

and since
x_{2}\geq 4\mu
and
\alpha\geq 1
, the last term in the above inequalities is bounded by a constant times
\mu^{2}
.
On the other hand, for any
\ell>0
,

\ell\int_{x_{2}\geq\ell}\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{x_{2}\geq\ell}x_{2}\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\mu,

and then

1\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{0<x_{2}<\ell}\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\frac{\mu}{\ell}.

Now by combining this with
\ell=\mu/2
and the aforementioned estimates for each cases
x_{2}<4\mu
and
x_{2}\geq 4\mu
, we are led to

1\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2\int_{0<x_{2}<2\mu}\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\omega({\bf x})\left(\int_{0<y_{2}<2\mu}G({\bf x},{\bf y})^{\alpha}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\mu^{2}.

That is, the full mass condition
\|\omega\|_{1}=1
leads to
\mu\gtrsim 1
.
Hence,
\|\omega\|_{1}<1
for small
\mu
and the Lemma
4.22
is proved for the case
p\in(4/3,2]
.
∎

The following lemma is useful in the proof of Lemma
4.22
for the case
p>2
.

Lemma 4.23
.

For any
\mu\in(0,1]
, we have

I_{\mu}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\mu^{(4p-4)/(3p-4)}I_{1}.

Proof.

We fix a nontrivial
\nu\in S_{1}
, that is,
\nu
is a maximizer of
E_{p}(\cdot)
with the prescribed unit impulse.
Set

\omega_{\mu}({\bf x})\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\kappa\cdot\nu(\kappa^{1-p/2}{\bf x}),

where
\kappa=\mu^{\delta}
with
\delta=2/(3p-4)
and
p>4/3
.
We see that

\|x_{2}\omega_{\mu}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu^{(3p/2-2)\delta}\|x_{2}\nu\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu^{(3p/2-2)\delta}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu,\quad\|\omega_{\mu}\|_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu^{(p-1)\delta}\|\nu\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\mu^{(p-1)\delta}.

Thus, we have
\omega_{\mu}\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
(see (
4.11
) for the definition of
\widetilde{K}_{\mu}
) for any
\mu\in(0,1]
.
Furthermore, since

E(\omega_{\mu})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu^{(2p-2)\delta}E(\nu)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\|\omega_{\mu}\|_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu^{(2p-2)\delta}\|\nu\|_{p}^{p},

it holds that

\displaystyle\mu^{(4p-4)/(3p-4)}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu^{(2p-2)\delta}I_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\mu^{(2p-2)\delta}\left(E(\nu)-\frac{1}{p}\|\nu\|_{p}^{p}\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }E_{p}(\omega_{\mu})\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\sup_{\theta\in\widetilde{K}_{\mu}}E_{p}(\theta)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }I_{\mu},

where the last equality is due to (
4.12
) in Remark
4.10
.
∎

We now turn to the case
p>2
to complete the proof of Lemma
4.22
.

Proof of Lemma
4.22
for
p>2
.

First, we derive an upper bound

\|\omega\|_{1}\lesssim\frac{\mu^{2/3}}{W^{2/3}}

which implies that
\mu\ll 1
yields
\|\omega\|_{1}<1
.
As in the case
p\leq 2
,

\|\omega\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{0<x_{2}<\ell}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\mu\ell^{-1}

for any
\ell>0
.
On the other hand, for
\omega\in S_{\mu}
and any
\alpha>0
,

(4.46)

\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\omega\cdot\frac{\mathcal{G}[\omega]}{Wx_{2}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{W\alpha}\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\mathcal{G}[\omega]\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

by the relation
\omega^{p-1}=(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2}-\gamma)_{+}
and the strict positivity of
W>0
in (
4.16
).
Note that by (
4.25
) of Proposition
4.15
and (
4.5
) of Lemma
4.5
, we have

0\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\omega^{p-1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\mathcal{G}[\omega]\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\psi\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\|\omega\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }1

provided by
\mu<1
.
Then, since
G({\bf x},{\bf y})=G({\bf y},{\bf x})>0
,

0\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}G({\bf y},{\bf x})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}G({\bf y},{\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

and

\displaystyle\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\psi\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}\right)\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\left(\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}G({\bf y},{\bf x})\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\alpha^{3/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}y_{2}^{1/2}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\alpha^{3/2}\mu^{1/2}\|\omega\|_{1}^{1/2}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }C\alpha^{3/2}\mu^{1/2},

where the first inequality is due to (
2.3
) of Lemma
2.1
.
Thus we get

\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\frac{\alpha^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}}{W}

and

(4.47)

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{0<x_{2}<\ell}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\mu\ell^{-1}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{2^{-k-1}\ell<x_{2}\leq 2^{-k}\ell}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)+\mu\ell^{-1}

(4.48)

\displaystyle\leq\mbox{ }C\frac{\ell^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}}{W}+\mu\ell^{-1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\frac{\mu^{2/3}}{W^{2/3}},

where we take
\ell=W^{2/3}\mu^{1/3}
for the last inequality.

Now observe that if the desired conclusion is not true, we have sequences of positive numbers
\{\mu_{n}\}
and
\{\gamma_{n}\}
satisfying
(1\geq)\,\mu_{n}\searrow 0
as
n\to\infty
and
\gamma_{n}>0
for all
n=1,2,\cdots
, respectively.
Here, we denote
\omega_{n}\in S_{\mu_{n}}
be the maximizer corresponding to
\mu_{n}
, and
W_{n}
is the traveling speed:

\omega_{n}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]-W_{n}x_{2}-\gamma_{n}\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)}.

As mentioned at the beginning of the proof of the case
p\leq 2
, the strict positivity of
\gamma_{n}
forces
\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}=1
.
From (
4.47
)

1\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\frac{\mu_{n}^{2/3}}{W_{n}^{2/3}},\quad\textrm{that is,}\quad W_{n}\lesssim\mu_{n}.

This with Lemma
4.23
and the identity (
4.44
) in Lemma
4.21
, we obtain

(4.49)

C\mu_{n}^{(4p-4)/(3p-4)}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }I_{\mu_{n}}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }W_{n}\mu_{n}+\gamma_{n}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\mu_{n}^{2}+\gamma_{n}.

Since
(4p-4)/(3p-4)<2
for
p>2
, from (
4.49
) we deduce that

0\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\mu_{n}^{(4p-4)/(3p-4)}\mbox{ }\lesssim\mbox{ }\gamma_{n}

for all sufficiently large
n
(recall that
\mu_{n}\searrow 0
as
n\to\infty
).
Using this lower bound for
\gamma_{n}
, we can proceed similarly to (
4.46
).
In particular, for any
\alpha>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\omega_{n}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]}{\gamma_{n}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\mu_{n}^{-(4p-4)/(3p-4)}\int_{\alpha<x_{2}\leq 2\alpha}\mathcal{G}[\omega_{n}]\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\mu_{n}^{-(4p-4)/(3p-4)+1/2}\alpha^{3/2}

and then

(4.50)

\displaystyle 1\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{2^{-k-1}\ell<x_{2}\leq 2^{-k}\ell}\omega_{n}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)+\mu_{n}\ell^{-1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\mu_{n}^{-(4p-4)/(3p-4)+1/2}\ell^{3/2}+\mu_{n}\ell^{-1}

for any
\ell>0
.
By taking

\ell\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }C\mu_{n}^{\beta},\quad\textrm{where}\quad\beta\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{5}+\frac{2}{5}\cdot\frac{4p-4}{3p-4}

for some absolute constant
C>0
, we minimize the last term of (
4.50
).
Then we reach

1\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\|\omega_{n}\|_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C\left(\mu_{n}^{4/5}\right)^{(p-2)/(3p-4)}\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{as}\,\,n\to\infty,

which is a contradiction.
This means that for each
p>2
, there exists an absolute number
\tilde{\mu}
such that for any
\mu\leq\tilde{\mu}
, every maximizer
\omega\in S_{\mu}
satisfies

\omega\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2})_{+}^{1/(p-1)},\quad\textrm{that is},\,\,\gamma(\omega)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0.

We have now completed the proof in the case
p>2
, thereby concluding the proof of Lemma
4.22
.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

In the proof of Lemma
4.21
, we have seen that for any
\omega\in S_{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p}{p-1}\right)W\mu,

\displaystyle I_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\gamma\|\omega\|_{1}\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\left(\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{p}{p-1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)W\mu-\frac{1}{p}\|\omega\|_{p}^{p}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{2}{p}\right)\|\omega\|_{p}^{p}.

We are now ready to prove the main goal of this section with the help of Lemma
4.22
.

Proof of Proposition
4.20
.

By Lemma
4.22
, there exists an absolute constant
\tilde{\mu}
such that for any
\omega\in S_{\mu}
with
\mu<\tilde{\mu}
, it satisfies an expression

\omega\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }(\mathcal{G}[\omega]-Wx_{2})_{+}^{1/(p-1)}

for some constant
W>0
.
Recall that the operator
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
is given by

\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}G({\bf x},{\bf y})\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y},\quad G({\bf x},{\bf y})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\left(1+\frac{4x_{2}y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\right).

Consider the odd extension (with respect to
x_{2}
variable)
\psi
of
\mathcal{G}[\omega]
.
It satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{2}}\psi({\bf x})\mbox{ }

\displaystyle=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{-x_{2}+y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}|^{2}}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\frac{x_{2}+y_{2}}{|{\bf x}-{\bf y}^{*}|^{2}}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=:\mbox{ }u^{1}_{l}({\bf x})+u^{1}_{nl}({\bf x}),

where
{\bf y}^{*}=(y_{1},-y_{2})
.

To prove the statement, we establish that

(4.51)

\partial_{x_{2}}\psi(0,0)\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }2W.

Indeed, if (
4.51
) holds, then by the continuity of
\partial_{x_{2}}\psi
(Lemma
4.13
), there exists a small
r>0
such that

\partial_{x_{2}}\psi\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }2W\quad\textrm{in}\,\,B_{r}(0,0).

For any
{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in B_{r}(0,0)\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, we then obtain

(4.52)

\displaystyle\psi({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{0}^{x_{2}}\partial_{x_{2}}\psi(x_{1},s)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}s\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }2Wx_{2}\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }Wx_{2},

which implies that
{\bf x}\in\{\psi-Wx_{2}>0\}
.
Consequently, there exists
r>0
such that

B_{r}(0,0)\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\subset\left\{\psi-Wx_{2}>0\right\}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }{\mathrm{supp}}\,\omega,

and it completes the proof of the proposition.

It remains to prove (
4.51
).
Since

\partial_{x_{2}}\psi(0,0)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }2u^{1}_{nl}(0,0),

Proving (
4.51
) is equivalent to showing that

(4.53)

u^{1}_{nl}(0,0)\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }W.

We remark that

W\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}u^{1}_{nl}\,\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}}{\|\omega\|_{1}}

from the identity (
4.16
) in Remark
4.12
.
Under the Steiner symmetry of
\omega
(as established in Proposition
4.7
, up to a translation in the
x_{1}
-direction), we claim that
u^{1}_{nl}
attains its
unique
maximum over
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
at the origin.
This implies (
4.53
):

W\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}u^{1}_{nl}(0,0)\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\right)\|\omega\|_{1}^{-1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }u^{1}_{nl}(0,0).

The claim is proved by establishing the following two sub-claims concerning
u^{1}_{nl}(x_{1},x_{2})
, the nonlocal part of velocity:

(Claim 1)
For any
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
, we have

u^{1}_{nl}(x_{1},x_{2})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }u^{1}_{nl}(-x_{1},x_{2})\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }0.

Moreover, for fixed
x_{2}\geq 0
, the function
u^{1}_{nl}(x_{1},x_{2})
is decreasing in
x_{1}\geq 0
.

(Claim 2)

u^{1}_{nl}(0,x_{2})>0
decreases in
x_{2}\geq 0
.

By obaining
(Claim 1)
and
(Claim 2)
, we verify that
u^{1}_{nl}
attains its
unique
maximum in
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
at the origin, and therefore we obtain deired inequality (
4.53
) as mentioned above.

Proof of (Claim 1)
:
For
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
, we have

u^{1}_{nl}(x_{1},x_{2})\mbox{ }=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{|{\bf y}-{\bf x}^{*}|^{2}}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\textrm{d}y_{2}\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }\mbox{ }0,

where
{\bf x}^{*}=(x_{1},-x_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}}
.
For
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
y_{2}>0
, we see that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{(y_{1}+x_{1})^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}\omega(y_{1}-x_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}\omega(x_{1}-y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{(y_{1}-x_{1})^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1},

where the second equality is due to the Steiner symmetry assumption on
\omega
.
This implies

u^{1}_{nl}(x_{1},x_{2})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }u^{1}_{nl}(-x_{1},x_{2})\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{for}\mbox{ }x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}.

Next, we show that
u_{nl}^{1}(x_{1},x_{2})
is a decreasing in
x_{1}\geq 0
.
We denote

\displaystyle\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}x_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{|{\bf y}-{\bf x}^{*}|^{2}}\omega({\bf y})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{2(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}+x_{2})}{|{\bf y}-{\bf x}^{*}|^{4}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\mbox{ }=:\mbox{ }f(x_{1};x_{2},y_{2})

for each fixed
y_{2}>0
and
x_{2}\geq 0
.
We show
f(x_{1}):=f(x_{1};x_{2},y_{2})<0
for
x_{1}>0
, under assumption that

y_{2}\in\left\{x_{2}>0:\omega(0,x_{2})\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }0\right\},

since otherwise we have
f\equiv 0
.
By the Steiner symmetry of
\omega
, there exists
l=l(y_{2})\in(0,\infty)
such that

f(x_{1})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{-l}^{l}\frac{2(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}+x_{2})}{|{\bf y}-{\bf x}^{*}|^{4}}\mbox{ }\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}.

For
x_{1}>l
, we have
y_{1}<l<x_{1}
, making the integrand is negative, so
f(x_{1})<0
.
For
0<x_{1}<l
, we split the integral as

f(x_{1})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{-l}^{x_{1}}+\int_{x_{1}}^{l}\mbox{ }=:\mbox{ }J_{1}+J_{2},

where
J_{1}<0
and
J_{2}>0
.
From the relation
-l<2x_{1}-l<x_{1}
, we see

J_{1}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\int_{2x_{1}-l}^{x_{1}}\frac{2(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}+x_{2})}{|{\bf y}-{\bf x}^{*}|^{4}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle J_{1}+J_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mbox{ }\int_{2x_{1}-l}^{x_{1}}\frac{2(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}+x_{2})}{|{\bf y}-{\bf x}^{*}|^{4}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}+\int_{x_{1}}^{l}\frac{2(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}+x_{2})}{|{\bf y}-{\bf x}^{*}|^{4}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{ }-\int_{0}^{l-x_{1}}\frac{2y_{1}(y_{2}+x_{2})}{\left|y_{1}^{2}+\left(y_{2}+x_{2}\right)^{2}\right|^{2}}\omega\left(x_{1}-y_{1},y_{2}\right)\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}+\int_{0}^{l-x_{1}}\frac{2y_{1}(y_{2}+x_{2})}{\left|y_{1}^{2}+\left(y_{2}+x_{2}\right)^{2}\right|^{2}}\omega(x_{1}+y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{ }-\int_{0}^{l-x_{1}}\frac{2y_{1}\mbox{ }(y_{2}+x_{2})}{\left|y_{1}^{2}+\left(y_{2}+x_{2}\right)^{2}\right|^{2}}\left\{\omega\left(x_{1}-y_{1},y_{2}\right)-\omega\left(x_{1}+y_{1},y_{2}\right)\right\}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }0,

where we use the Steiner symmetry condition of
\omega
, that is,
\omega(y_{1},y_{2})
is even in
y_{1}
and

\omega(x_{1}-y_{1},y_{2})=\omega(y_{1}-x_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\omega(y_{1}+x_{1},y_{2})

for each
y_{1}\in(0,l-x_{1})
.
Thus,
f(x_{1})\leq 0
for
x_{1}>0
(for each
x_{2}\geq 0
,
y_{2}>0
) and integrating in
y_{2}
gives

\frac{\textrm{d}u^{1}_{nl}}{\textrm{d}x_{1}}(x_{1},x_{2})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}x_{1}}f(x_{1};x_{2},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{2}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }0

for
x_{1}>0
with fixed
x_{2}\geq 0
.
This completes the proof of
(Claim 1)
.

Proof of (Claim 2)
: For
x_{2}\geq 0
, we have

u^{1}_{nl}(0,x_{2})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}\mbox{ }\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\textrm{d}y_{2}\mbox{ }>\mbox{ }0.

Similar to the proof of (Claim 1), for each
y_{2}
satisfying
(0,y_{2})\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\omega
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}\mbox{ }\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }2\int_{0}^{l}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}

for some
l=l(y_{2})>0
.
By taking the derivative in
x_{2}
variable,

\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}x_{2}}\int_{0}^{l}\frac{y_{2}+x_{2}}{y_{1}^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{0}^{l}\frac{y_{1}^{2}-(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}{\left|y_{1}^{2}+\left(y_{2}+x_{2}\right)^{2}\right|^{2}}\omega(y_{1},y_{2})\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\mbox{ }=:\mbox{ }g(x_{2}).

It is clear that
g(x_{2})<0
when
y_{2}+x_{2}\geq l
.
If
0<y_{2}+x_{2}<l
, we decompose the integral as

g(x_{2})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\int_{0}^{y_{2}+x_{2}}+\int_{y_{2}+x_{2}}^{l}\mbox{ }=:\mbox{ }J_{3}+J_{4},

where
J_{3}<0
and
J_{4}>0
.
As the function
\omega(y_{1},y_{2})
decreases in
y_{1}>0
, we have

J_{3}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\omega(y_{2}+x_{2},y_{2})\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{y_{2}+x_{2}}\frac{y_{1}^{2}-(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}{\left|y_{1}^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}\right|^{2}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\right)\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }0

and

0\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }J_{4}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\omega(y_{2}+x_{2},y_{2})\cdot\left(\int_{y_{2}+x_{2}}^{\infty}\frac{y_{1}^{2}-(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}{\left|y_{1}^{2}+\left(y_{2}+x_{2}\right)^{2}\right|^{2}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}y_{1}\right).

Therefore, we have

g(x_{2})\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\omega(y_{2}+x_{2},y_{2})\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{y_{1}^{2}-(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}}{\left|y_{1}^{2}+(y_{2}+x_{2})^{2}\right|^{2}}\mbox{ }dy_{1}\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0,

where the last equality is due to the identity

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{2}-1}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}x\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }0

This implies

\frac{\textrm{d}u_{nl}^{1}(0,x_{2})}{\textrm{d}x_{2}}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }0\quad\textrm{for}\,\,x_{2}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }0

and thus we proved
(Claim 2)
. The proposition is proved.
∎

4.5.1.
C^{2,\alpha}
Sadovskii vortex

By combining the results from previous sections, we prove the existence of a
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
Sadovskii vortex,
i.e.
, a counter-rotating vortex pair solution that contiguously touches the
x_{1}
-axis.

Proof of Theorem
4.3
(Theorem
1.6
)
.

We take
\mu\in(0,\tilde{\mu})
, where
\tilde{\mu}
is from Proposition
4.20
.
For given
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we take
p=1+1/(2+\alpha)>4/3
, that is,

\frac{1}{p-1}-2\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\alpha.

Then by Propositions
4.15
and
4.7
, for any
\mu>0
, there exists a Steinery-symmetric maximizer
\omega\in K_{\mu}
such that
E_{p}(\omega)=I_{\mu}
whose odd extension to the whole plane is in
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Furthermore, by Lemma
4.22
, the maximizer
\omega
has no flux constant
\gamma=0
:

\omega({\bf x})\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]({\bf x})-Wx_{2}\right)_{+}^{1/(p-1)},

while Proposition
4.20
says that the maximizer
\omega\in K_{\mu}
above is in contact with the
x_{1}
axis,
i.e.
, its odd extension
\widetilde{\omega}
is a Sadovskii vortex.
The theorem is proved.
∎

Remark 4.25
.

With the choice
p=1+1/(2+\alpha)
in the proof of Theorem
4.3
, the touching vortex pair
\widetilde{\omega}
attains
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity but cannot belong to any higher space
C^{2,\beta}
for
\beta>\alpha
.
Specifically,
1+1/(2+\alpha)
is the largest value of
p
that ensures
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity.

Indeed, since
\mathcal{G}[\omega]\in C^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}})
and
\mathcal{G}[\omega](x_{1},0)=0
for all
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, it follows from (
4.52
) that

\mathcal{G}[\omega](0,s)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }(2W+\delta)s+O(s^{2})\quad\textrm{for some}\,\,\delta>0

provided that
s\geq 0
is sufficiently small.
Then, along the line
{\bf x}=(0,s)
,
\omega\in S_{\mu}
satisfies

\omega({\bf x})\big|_{{\bf x}=(0,s)}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\left(\mathcal{G}[\omega]\left(0,s\right)-Ws\right)^{1/(p-1)}_{+}\ \simeq\ \left(W+\delta\right)^{1/(p-1)}\,s^{1/(p-1)}.

Since
1/(p-1)=2+\alpha
, we see that
\omega
behaves like
s^{2+\alpha}
near
s=0
.
This means that
\omega
belongs to
C^{2,\alpha}
but not to
C^{2,\beta}
for any
\beta>\alpha
.
Hence, the choice of
p
precisely determines the level of regularity achievable.

Appendix A
Perturbed set near Sadovskii vortex patch having smooth boundary and finite length

In this section, we prove Lemma
3.26
by establishing a smooth approximation of the
boundary curve
of each Sadovskii vortex patch in
S_{\mu}
(see Lemma
3.14
-(i)).
The resulting perturbed initial data will possess two arbitrarily long and thin tails.
We note that each Sadovskii vortex patch
\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}
is characterized by its boundary curve
l:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
by

A=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:|x_{1}|<l(x_{2})\}.

One may consider a smooth approximation
l_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(0,\infty)
of the curve
l\in L^{1}(0,\infty)
satisfying

l_{\varepsilon}\to l\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }\mbox{ }L^{1}(0,\infty)\quad\mbox{as }\mbox{ }\varepsilon\to 0.

However, we note that the perturbed set
A_{\varepsilon}
defined by

A_{\varepsilon}:=\{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:|x_{1}|<l_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})\}

does not have a smooth boundary since the boundary
\partial A_{\varepsilon}
has a cusp at each of its intersections with
x_{2}
-axis.
In the proof, we focus on generating a perturbed set that does not possess such a cusp.

Proof of Lemma
3.26
.

Let
\mu>0
be small enough so that the estimate (
3.8
) in Lemma
3.14
holds. We denote the uniform constant
C>0
in (
3.8
) by
C_{0}
.
We choose any
\omega\in S_{\mu}
satisfying the Steiner symmetry condition, in particular,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}x_{1}\omega\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0.

Lemma
3.14
-(i) says that the Steiner symmetry condition allows us to characterize
\omega
by its continuous boundary curve

l:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that

\omega=\mathbf{1}_{A}\quad\mbox{where}\quad A:=\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }|x_{1}|<l(x_{2})\mbox{ }\}.

The estimate (
3.8
) in Lemma
3.14
implies that

\|l\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\infty)}<C_{0}\mu^{1/3}\quad\mbox{and}\quad l\equiv 0\quad\mbox{in}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\left[C_{0}\mu^{1/3},\infty\right).

Step 1: Smooth approximation of the boundary curve
l

We will establish a smooth approximation of the boundary curve
l
. Let
\varepsilon>0
.
For the continuous curve
l\in C([0,\infty))
, we define its even extension
\bar{l}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\bar{l}(s):=l(|s|).

We note that
\bar{l}\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and

{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\bar{l}}\mbox{ }\subset\mbox{ }(-C_{0}\mu^{1/3},C_{0}\mu^{1/3}).

Consider a nonnegative smooth perturbation of
\bar{l}
denoted by
\bar{l}_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying

{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\bar{l}_{\varepsilon}}\subset(-C_{0}\mu^{1/3},C_{0}\mu^{1/3})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\|\bar{l}-\bar{l}_{\varepsilon}\|_{\left(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}\right)(\mathbb{R})}<\varepsilon.

(The perturbation
\bar{l}_{\varepsilon}
can be obtained by the convolution of
\bar{l}
with a smooth nonnegative function whose support is highly localized near the origin).
Let
l_{\varepsilon}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be the restriction of
\bar{l}_{\varepsilon}
to the half line
[0,\infty)
.
Then we obtain that

{\mathrm{supp}}\,{l_{\varepsilon}}\subset[0,C_{0}\mu^{1/3}),\quad l_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}(0,\infty),\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\|l-l_{\varepsilon}\|_{\left(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}\right)(0,\infty)}<\varepsilon.

Step 2:
L^{\infty}
bound of the approximated curve
l_{\varepsilon}

We again recall the estimate (
3.8
) in Lemma
3.14
:

\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\sup\left\{\mbox{ }\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\eta}|{\bf x}|\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\Big|\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\eta\in S_{\mu}\quad\mbox{satisfies }\mbox{ }\int x_{1}\eta\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}=0\right\}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}.

By assuming

\varepsilon\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\varepsilon_{\mu}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2}\left(\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}-\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\mbox{ }\right)

we get

\|l_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\|l_{\varepsilon}-l\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|l\|_{L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\varepsilon_{\mu}+\|l\|_{L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }\varepsilon_{\mu}+\sup_{{\bf x}\in{\mathrm{supp}}\,\omega}|{\bf x}|\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon_{\mu}+\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}-\varepsilon_{\mu}.

Step 3: Modifying
l_{\varepsilon}
in a way of creating a thin and high peak

We choose any
L>2C_{0}\mu^{1/3}
.
We start with an arbitrary boundary point of
A
.
Let
a\in(0,\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3})
be any number satisfying
l_{\varepsilon}(a)>0
. We choose small
\delta>0
such that

(A.1)

\delta<\min\{\varepsilon,3a,C_{0}\mu^{1/3}-a\}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad l_{\varepsilon}>0\quad\mbox{in }\mbox{ }[a-\delta,\mbox{ }a+\delta].

We will find a nonnegative function
K_{\varepsilon,L}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(a-\delta,a+\delta)
having small
L^{1}
norm and satisfying that the function
l_{\varepsilon}\cdot(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)+\varepsilon
attaints the maximum value
L
.
We choose a nonnegative function
K\in C^{\infty}_{c}(-\delta,\delta)
satisfying

\|K\|_{L^{1}}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{and}\quad\max{K}=1.

For each
r\geq 1
, we define
K_{r}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(a-\delta,a+\delta)
as
K_{r}(s):=rK(a+rs)
to observe that

\|K_{r}\|_{L^{1}}=\|K\|_{L^{1}}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{and}\quad\max{K_{r}}=r.

By defining
f:[1,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
as

f(r):=\max_{[a-\delta,a+\delta]}\left(l_{\varepsilon}\cdot(K_{r}+1)\right),

we obtain that

f(1)\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2\|l_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }2C_{0}\mu^{1/3}-2\varepsilon_{\mu}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }L-2\varepsilon\quad\mbox{ and }\lim_{r\to\infty}f(r)=\infty,

where the second result follows from the observation that

\frac{f(r)}{r}\mbox{ }\geq\mbox{ }\frac{1}{2\delta r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}l_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot K_{r}(s)\mbox{ }ds\mbox{ }\to\mbox{ }\frac{l_{\varepsilon}(a)}{2\delta}\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }r\to\infty.

We note that
f
is continuous due to the continuity of
\mbox{ }l_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot(K_{r}(s)+1)\mbox{ }
in

(r,s)\in[1,\infty)\mbox{ }\times\mbox{ }[a-\delta,a+\delta].

Then the intermediate value theorem gives us a constant
R=R(L)>1
such that
f(R)=L-\varepsilon
.
Then we put
K_{\varepsilon,L}:=K_{R}
to obtain that

\|K_{\varepsilon,L}\|_{L^{1}}<\varepsilon\quad\mbox{and}\quad\max_{[a-\delta,a+\delta]}\left(l_{\varepsilon}\cdot(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)\right)=L-\varepsilon>0.

Moreover, from the relation
\|l_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}<C_{0}\mu^{1/3}-\varepsilon_{\mu}<L-\varepsilon
, observe that the function
\mbox{ }l_{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)\mbox{ }
attains its global maximum value (which is
L-\varepsilon
) only in the set

{\mathrm{supp}}\,{K_{\varepsilon,L}}\mbox{ }\subset\mbox{ }(a-\delta,\mbox{ }a+\delta),

i.e.
, we get

\max\left(l_{\varepsilon}\cdot(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)\right)\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }L-\varepsilon.

Step 4: Mollification of cusps

Up to now, we have constructed the smooth curve
l_{\varepsilon,L}:=\mbox{ }l_{\varepsilon}\cdot(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)\mbox{ }
having a highly-localized peak in the interval
(a-\delta,a+\delta)
.
Note that the curve
l_{\varepsilon,L}
meets its axis of domain in the manner of
all of its derivatives tending to
0
.
This implies the existence of a
cusp
on the boundary of the set

\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }|x_{1}|<l_{\varepsilon,L}(x_{2})\mbox{ }\}.

We will mollify this cusp by constructing a modified curve
\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}
satisfying that, for some interval
[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)
, it holds

{\mathrm{supp}}\,{\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}}=[a,b],\quad\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}\in C^{\infty}(a,b),

together with

\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}^{(n)}(s)\to\infty\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }s\searrow a\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}^{(n)}(s)\to-\infty\quad\mbox{ as }\mbox{ }s\nearrow b

for each
n\geq 1
.
Moreover, for the set
\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }x_{1}<\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}(x_{2})\mbox{ }\}
to be simply-connected, we desire that

\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}>0\quad\mbox{ in }\mbox{ }(a,b).

We first consider a function
H:[0,1]\to[0,1]
which decreases strictly and satisfies
H=H^{-1},\mbox{ }H\in C^{\infty}(0,1),\mbox{ }H(0)=1
,
H(1)=0
, and

\lim_{x\searrow 0}H^{(n)}(x)=0\quad\mbox{for each}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }n\geq 1.

Using
H
, we define the function
H_{\varepsilon}:[\delta,\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}+\delta]\to\mathbb{R}
such that

H_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} \quad&H\left(\frac{2\delta-x}{\delta}\right)\quad&\mbox{in}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\left[\delta,\mbox{ }2\delta\right),\\
\quad&1\quad&\mbox{in}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\left[2\delta,\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}\right),\\
\quad&H\left(\frac{x-C_{0}\mu^{1/3}}{\delta}\right)\quad&\mbox{in}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\left[C_{0}\mu^{1/3},\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}\mbox{ }+\delta\right).\end{aligned}\right.

We define the function
\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
as

\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }H_{\varepsilon}\cdot\left(l_{\varepsilon,L}+\varepsilon\right).

We note that

{\mathrm{supp}}\,\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}=\big[\delta,\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}+\delta\big]\quad\mbox{and}\quad\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}>0\quad\mbox{in}\mbox{ }\mbox{ }\left(\delta,\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}+\delta\right),

while at the end points of the support, it holds that

\lim_{x\searrow\delta}\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}^{(n)}(x)=\infty\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{x\nearrow C_{0}\mu^{1/3}+\delta}\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}^{(n)}(x)=-\infty\quad\mbox{for each }\mbox{ }n\geq 1

as desired.
From the assumption on
\delta
given in (
A.1
), we note that the interval
[2\delta,C_{0}\mu^{1/3}]
, on which it holds that
H_{\varepsilon}\equiv 1
, contains the sub-interval
(a-\delta,a+\delta)
only on which the function
l_{\varepsilon,L}
attains its global maximum value
L-\varepsilon
.
So the function
\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}
still attains its global maximum value
L
only in the interval
(a-\delta,a+\delta)
.
Lastly, it holds that
\mbox{ }\|\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}-l\|_{L^{1}(0,\infty)}\lesssim_{\mu}\varepsilon\mbox{ }
by

\|\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}-l\|_{L^{1}}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\varepsilon\|H_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}}+\|l_{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)-l\|_{L^{1}}+\|l_{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)(H_{\varepsilon}-1)\|_{L^{1}},

where
\|H_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}}<C_{0}\mu^{1/3}
and

\|l_{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)-l\|_{L^{1}}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\|l_{\varepsilon}-l\|_{L^{1}}+\|l_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|K_{\varepsilon,L}\|_{L^{1}}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }\varepsilon+C_{0}\mu^{1/3}\varepsilon,

together with

\|l_{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon,L}+1)(H_{\varepsilon}-1)\|_{L^{1}}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}l_{\varepsilon}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}+\int_{C_{0}\mu^{1/3}}^{C_{0}\mu^{1/3}+\delta}l_{\varepsilon}\mbox{ }\textrm{d}{\bf x}\mbox{ }\leq\mbox{ }2\delta\cdot\|l_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }2C_{0}\mu^{1/3}\varepsilon.

Step 5: Perturbed set near Sadovskii vortex patch

We now define the open, bounded, and simply-connected set
\Omega\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
by

\Omega\mbox{ }:=\mbox{ }\{\mbox{ }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mbox{ }:\mbox{ }|x_{1}|<\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}(x_{2})\mbox{ }\}

satisfying the Steiner symmetry condition, we observe that its boundary
\partial\Omega
is in
C^{\infty}
and of finite length.
Moreover, we have

\sup_{{\bf x}\in\Omega}|x_{1}|=L\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\|\omega-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}\|_{1}=2\|\zeta_{\varepsilon,L}-l\|_{L^{1}(0,\infty)}\lesssim_{\mu}\varepsilon.

Lastly, we have

\sup_{{\bf x}\in\Omega}x_{2}\mbox{ }=\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}+\delta\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }C_{0}\mu^{1/3}+\varepsilon\mbox{ }<\mbox{ }1

by assuming further that
\mu
and
\varepsilon
are small enough.
It completes the proof.
∎
